# Sūrah al-Mā'idah (Chapter 5) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**The Charter of Covenants and the Table of Communion**

Al-Mā'idah, "The Table Spread," is the fifth chapter of the Qur'an, comprising 120 verses of late Medinan revelation. It takes its name from the miraculous supper requested by the disciples of Jesus in verses 112–115—a heavenly banquet sought as a physical token of reassurance, which Jesus inaugurates with prayer and which Allah grants under the solemn condition that anyone who denies truth after that will suffer unprecedented retribution. Known in classical scholarship as *Sūrat al-ʿUqūd* (the Chapter of Contracts) from its striking opening imperative (*“O believers! Fulfil your obligations,”* 5:1), al-Mā'idah is universally recognized as the final comprehensive legal sūrah revealed to the Prophet ﷺ. In prophetic tradition, ʿĀ'ishah advised believers to hold fast to al-Mā'idah because *“whatever you find in it of lawful (*ḥalāl*), consider it lawful, and whatever you find in it of unlawful (*ḥarām*), consider it unlawful,”* reflecting the fact that its legal rulings were un-abrogated (*muḥkam*) and represented the ultimate maturation of the Islamic legal order.

**The Structural Architecture of the Sūrah**

The movement of al-Mā'idah weaves seamlessly between sacred ritual law, penal statutes, interfaith argument, and the cosmic teaching about the Last Day. The opening movement (1–11) establishes the foundational sanctity of agreements, detailing dietary permissions and prohibitions, the ritual laws of purification (*wuḍūʾ* and *tayammum*), the marriageability of chaste believing and scriptural women, and the solemn covenant taken at the spring of guidance, anchored by the command: *“Do not let hatred of a people lead you to injustice; be just, that is closer to righteousness”* (5:8). The second movement (12–26) reviews the broken covenants of the Children of Israel and the Christians, demonstrating how the forgetting of divine admonitions spawned enduring enmity among them, punctuated by the tragedy of their refusal to enter the Holy Land under Moses (*“Go, you and your Lord, and fight! We are sitting right here,”* 5:24). The third movement (27–40) investigates the origins of human violence through the primeval fratricide of Cain and Abel (*Qābīl and Hābīl*), deriving from it the immortal sanctification of life: *“Whoever kills a soul... It is as if he had slain all mankind, and whoever saves one, it is as if he had saved all mankind”* (5:32), followed by the penal ordinances for banditry (*ḥirābah*) and theft. The fourth movement (41–50) forms the great judicial charter of revelation, comparing the Torah, the Gospel, and the Qur'an as divine lights and criteria, concluding that each scriptural community must be held to divine standards, culminating in the repudiation of pre-Islamic pagan ignorance (*ḥukm al-jāhiliyyah*). The fifth movement (51–86) regulates political alliances, denounces the mockery of prayer by hypocrites, refutes the Christian deification of Mary and Jesus (*“The Messiah, son of Mary, was not but a messenger,”* 5:75), and notes the spiritual tenderness of Christian monks contrasted with the hostility of dogmatic deniers. The sixth movement (87–108) legislates prohibitions against intoxicants, gambling, idolatrous altars, and divination arrows, sets the expiation for broken oaths, sanctifies the pilgrim state (*iḥrām*), and governs deathbed gifts. The grand finale (109–120) stages the awe-inspiring Last-Day interrogation of Jesus on the Day of Judgment, in which Christ categorically disavows that he ever commanded his followers to worship him or his mother beside Allah, sealing the discourse in verse 120: *“To Allah belongs the dominion of the heavens and the earth and whatever is within them, and He is over all things competent.”*

---

## Verse 5:1

> O believers! Honour your obligations. All grazing livestock has been made lawful to you—except what is hereby announced to you and hunting while on pilgrimage. Indeed, Allah commands what He wills

**Contracts Are the Frame of Faithful Life**

The opening address yā ayyuhā lladhīna āmanū calls those who claim faith to live up to its implications, and the first command is awfū bi-l-'uqūd — honour your obligations. 'Uqūd, plural of 'aqd, means a knot that ties, then by extension any binding agreement where two sides fasten themselves to a course. Classical lexicographers explain 'aqd as what joins two ends firmly, and jurists apply it to every firm commitment: the covenant with Allah to worship Him alone, the pledge taken through messengers, and the contracts between people such as marriage, sale, and alliance. Al-Ṭabarī records from Ibn 'Abbās that 'uqūd includes what Allah made obligatory and what people obligate upon themselves. That breadth matters because this sūrah will detail food laws, oaths, and treaties, and it begins by rooting all of them in fidelity. *“Fulfil the covenant of Allah when you have entered into it”* (16:91) states the same principle in another place, and *“Fulfil every covenant, for every covenant will be questioned”* (17:34) links keeping promises to accountability. The verse therefore does not list contracts; it establishes a habit of honouring them.

**Why Grazing Livestock Defines the Lawful Baseline**

Uḥillat lakum bahīmat al-an'ām declares grazing livestock lawful. Bahīmah means a quadruped that does not clearly express, and an'ām specifies the herd animals Arabs relied upon: camels, cattle, sheep and goats. The Qur'an elsewhere affirms the same provision: *“And the grazing livestock He has created for you; in them is warmth and benefits and from them you eat”* (16:5).

**Allah Commands What He Wills — Sovereignty in Legislation**

Inna Allāha yaḥkumu mā yurīd closes the verse with a principle that explains everything before it: Allah commands what He wills. Yaḥkumu, from ḥ-k-m, conveys judging, decreeing, and governing with wisdom, not arbitrary power. The same root yields ḥikmah, wisdom, and ḥukm, ruling. The sūrah insists that the right to declare lawful and unlawful belongs to the One who creates and sustains. *“Judgment belongs only to Allah”* (12:40) and *“He does not associate anyone in His judgment”* (18:26) reinforce this. Some exegetes read mā yurīd as indicating that divine rulings track divine wisdom, even when the wisdom is not immediately apparent to humans; others emphasize sheer sovereignty, that no one can question what He legislates. Both are true: the rulings have wisdom, and they bind because He willed them. This clause also answers the pre-Islamic practice of permitting and forbidding by whim. Where tribes made lawful what they liked and forbade what they disliked, the verse says legislation belongs to Allah, whose will is informed by knowledge and mercy. That sets the stage for verse 3's famous declaration that the faith has been perfected.

**A Medinan Opening That Sets the Sūrah’s Tone**

Al-Mā'idah is a Medinan sūrah revealed after the conquest of Makkah, and many reports place parts of it on the Day of 'Arafah during the Farewell Pilgrimage in 10 AH. The Prophet ﷺ was riding his camel al-'Aḍbā' when the sūrah descended, so heavy that the animal knelt, as reported in Musnad Aḥmad (17699, graded ḥasan by al-Arnā'ūṭ). That circumstance fits the content: final rulings about food, oaths, justice, and relations with the People of the Book. The sūrah is called al-Mā'idah, the Table Spread, after the request of the disciples in verses 112-115, and also Sūrat al-'Uqūd, the Chapter of Contracts, because it opens with 'uqūd. These two names capture its two concerns: law and faith history. Opening with contracts signals that Islam is not only belief but a system of trust — with Allah, with people, and with the natural world that provides food. The mention of grazing livestock immediately after contracts links ethics to sustenance, showing that how we eat reflects what we honour. The next verse will extend that ethic to pilgrimage rites and to cooperation in righteousness, building outward from this foundation.

A report in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (1730) describes how the Prophet ﷺ instructed pilgrims about what game they must avoid, which shows how seriously the Companions took this restraint.

---

## Verse 5:2

> O believers! Do not violate Allah’s rituals ˹of pilgrimage˺, the sacred months, the sacrificial animals, the ˹offerings decorated with˺ garlands, nor those ˹pilgrims˺ on their way to the Sacred House seeking their Lord’s bounty and pleasure. When pilgrimage has ended, you are allowed to hunt. Do not let the hatred of a people who once barred you from the Sacred Mosque provoke you to transgress. Cooperate with one another in goodness and righteousness, and do not cooperate in sin and transgression. And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah is severe in punishment

**Sacred Months, Garlands, and Pilgrims Seeking Bounty**

The verse lists four protected categories: the sacred months, the sacrificial animals, the garlanded offerings, and those heading to the Sacred House. Al-shahr al-ḥarām refers to the four months where fighting was forbidden: Dhū al-Qa'dah, Dhū al-Ḥijjah, Muḥarram, and Rajab, a practice confirmed since the time of Ibrāhīm. Al-hady means animals driven to Makkah for sacrifice, and al-qalā'id, garlands, are collars placed on them to mark them as offerings, so no one would harm them. The final category, āmmīn al-bayt al-ḥarām yabtaghūna faḍlan min rabbihim wa riḍwānan, describes pilgrims and traders seeking Allah's bounty and pleasure. Faḍl means surplus or grace, often used for provision and trade, while riḍwān is deep pleasure. The pairing shows that seeking livelihood on the way to ḥajj was not condemned if the primary aim remained devotion. *“There is no blame upon you for seeking bounty from your Lord during pilgrimage”* (2:198) confirms this. By protecting these persons and things, the verse secures safe passage for worship, a principle that later jurists extended to amān, safe conduct.

**Hunting Becomes Lawful After Iḥrām Ends**

Wa idhā ḥalaltum fa-iṣṭādū gives permission to hunt when pilgrimage has ended. Ḥalaltum, from ḥ-l-l, means to become lawful or to exit the state of iḥrām, the opposite of aḥramtum. The command form iṣṭādū is not an obligation but a removal of prohibition, a common Qur'anic phrase: after a ban, a command returns the act to its original permissibility. This explains why the Qur'an says fa-iṣṭādū rather than merely stating hunting is allowed. The same structure appears in *“When the prayer is finished, disperse in the land”* (62:10), where the command means permission to resume work. Jurists differ on whether game caught while in iḥrām must be compensated if the hunter was forgetful, but they agree intentional violation requires expiation, as verse 95 will detail. The placement of this permission right after protecting pilgrims shows balance: restraint during sanctity, ease after it, reflecting *“Allah intends ease for you, not hardship”* (2:185). Wa lā yajrimannakum shana'ānu qawmin a ṣaddūkum 'an al-masjid al-ḥarām a ta'tadū forbids letting hatred provoke transgression. The occasion, according to al-Ṭabarī, was that the polytheists barred Muslims from the Sacred Mosque at Ḥudaybiyah, and some Companions considered retaliating by blocking their trade routes. This principle is repeated with even greater force in *“Do not let hatred of a people lead you to injustice; be just, that is closer to righteousness”* (5:8). Ibn Kathīr notes that the verse establishes that enmity must be contained within limits set by Allah.

**Severity in Punishment Balances Mercy in Law**

Inna Allāha shadīd al-'iqāb closes with warning. Shadīd means intense, firm, not easily repelled, and 'iqāb comes from 'aqaba, to follow, because punishment follows the crime. The phrase appears after a series of permissions and prohibitions to remind that ease in law does not mean laxity in accountability. The same closure occurs in *“And be mindful of Allah; surely Allah is severe in punishment”* (5:2) itself and in *“Know that Allah is severe in punishment and that Allah is Forgiving, Merciful”* (5:98), pairing severity with mercy. Commentators note that the verse began with honouring obligations and ends with fear of consequence, framing law between love and awe. The severity mentioned is not arbitrary harshness but the necessary counterpart to violated sanctities: those who violate pilgrims or sacred months disrupt public worship, so the deterrent must be clear. Yet the sūrah immediately follows this with detailed allowances for necessity in verse 3, showing that severity targets defiance, not hardship. The next verse will detail forbidden foods, continuing the pattern of clear boundaries followed by merciful exceptions.

The same root appears in *“And whoever honours the symbols of Allah, that is from piety of hearts”* (22:32).

A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2580) reports the Prophet ﷺ said believers are like a building, each part strengthening the other, which jurists cite as practical illustration of this verse.

---

## Verse 5:3
> Forbidden to you are carrion, blood, and swine; what is slaughtered in the name of any other than Allah; what is killed by strangling, beating, a fall, or by being gored to death; what is partly eaten by a predator unless you slaughter it; and what is sacrificed on altars. You are also forbidden to draw lots for decisions. This is all evil. Today the disbelievers have given up all hope of ˹undermining˺ your faith. So do not fear them; fear Me! Today I have perfected your faith for you, completed My favour upon you, and chosen Islam as your way. But whoever is compelled by extreme hunger—not intending to sin—then surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**The Food the Book Withholds**

The verse lists what the community may not eat: an animal that died on its own, blood, the flesh of swine, and anything slaughtered while a name other than God's was called over it. The Book gives the same four in the Sūrah of the Cow (2:173 — *“He has only forbidden you ˹to eat˺ carrion, blood, swine, and what is slaughtered in the name of any other than Allah.”*) and repeats them in the Sūrah of the Cattle, where it adds that the blood meant is the blood poured out, and that a person is only held to blame for what he does deliberately (6:145 — *“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “I do not find in what has been revealed to me anything forbidden to eat except carrion, running blood, swine—which is impure—or a sinful offering in the name of any other than Allah.”*). The sūrah then adds the animals that died by violence rather than by the knife — strangled, beaten, fallen, gored, or partly eaten by a wild beast — with the exception that whatever is still alive when a person reaches it may be slaughtered properly. The last item on the list is what was sacrificed on the stone altars. What all of it has in common is the question the Book keeps asking about meat: whose name was over the act, and whether the animal's life was taken as it should be.

**Arrows in Place of a Decision**

The verse adds one more prohibition among the foods: the drawing of marked arrows to decide a matter. The practice belonged to the days before Islam, when a man in doubt about a journey, a marriage or a trade would draw one of the arrows and let the lot settle it, and the arrows carried yes, no or nothing at all. The Book calls the practice a going out of the way of obedience, and it names the arrows again with wine, gambling and idols as the dirt of Satan's work (5:90 — *“O believers! Intoxicants, gambling, idols, and drawing lots for decisions are all evil of Satan’s handiwork. So shun them so you may be successful”*). The reason the practice is placed among the food laws is that both are about where a person takes his provision and his guidance from. A man who lets a stick decide his affairs has handed over a decision that belongs to God, in the same way as a man who slaughters for a name other than God's.

**"This Day I Have Perfected Your Religion"**

The verse then turns from food to the state of the community with a sentence the whole Muslim world knows: this day the disbelievers have given up hope of your religion, so do not fear them — fear Me. The despair was the recognition that the message would not be put out by argument or by force; the conquest of Makkah and the pilgrimage of the year after it had settled that. Then comes the announcement at the centre of the passage: today I have perfected your religion for you, completed My favour on you, and chosen submission as your religion. A report in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī tells of a Jew who said to ʿUmar that if this sentence had been sent down to his people they would have taken the day as a festival, and ʿUmar answered that he knew exactly where and when it came down — on ʿArafah, on a Friday (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 45). The commentators note that ʿUmar wept over it, because he understood that a thing which is finished is also a thing whose messenger is near the end of his work.

**The Allowance for the Starving**

The verse closes with the exception that keeps the laws livable: whoever is driven to eat the forbidden by hunger, without turning deliberately to sin, will find God forgiving and merciful. The same sentence ends the list in the Sūrah of the Cattle (6:145 — *“But if someone is compelled by necessity—neither driven by desire nor exceeding immediate need—then surely your Lord is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.””*), and the jurists built on it the rule that a person in need may take of what is otherwise forbidden as much as is needed to survive, and no more. The condition is stated twice in the wording, so that the allowance cannot be stretched into a licence: the man eating is not choosing the forbidden thing, he is saving his own life, and the Book places that permission immediately after the announcement of the perfected religion, as if to say that a law which cannot bend for a dying man would not be a mercy.

**Two Questions the Jurists Settled From This Passage**

The rule about the name spoken over the animal gave the schools one of their clearest differences. The Hanafīs held that God's name must be spoken at the slaughter for the meat to be lawful, and they pointed to the command to eat only what His name has been mentioned over (6:121 — *“Do not eat of what is not slaughtered in Allah’s Name.”*); the Shāfiʿīs and the Mālikīs held that leaving it out by mistake does not make the meat unlawful, because the verse in front of us forbids what is slaughtered for a name other than God's, not what is slaughtered with no name at all. A second question came out of the same passage read with the verse that follows it, where the food of the People of the Book is made lawful (5:5 — *“Today all good, pure foods have been made lawful for you.”*), and the jurists worked out from the two verses together what may be taken from the meat of other communities living among the Muslims.

---

## Verse 5:4

> They ask you, ˹O Prophet,˺ what is permissible for them ˹to eat˺. Say, “What is good and lawful. Also what is caught by your hunting animals and birds of prey which you have trained as instructed by Allah. So eat what they catch for you, but mention the Name of Allah over it ˹first˺.” And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah is swift in reckoning

**A Question About Food Becomes a Principle**

Yas'alūnaka mādhā uḥilla lahum shows the Companions asking what is made lawful for them to eat. The question follows a long list of prohibitions in verse 3, so the natural concern is what remains. The Qur'an often answers such questions by giving a principle rather than an exhaustive list, so the answer can cover new cases. Qul uḥilla lakum al-ṭayyibāt — say, what is good and lawful has been made lawful. Al-ṭayyibāt, plural of ṭayyib, means what is wholesome, pleasant, and pure, both in taste and in moral sense. It contrasts with khabā'ith, impure things, mentioned in *“He makes lawful for them the good things and forbids for them the impure”* (7:157). The term therefore includes both legal permissibility and natural wholesomeness. Early exegetes explain that ṭayyibāt covers all foods that Arabs considered good and that revelation did not forbid, excluding carrion and filth. By framing the answer this way, the verse teaches that the default is purity and goodness, not suspicion. The lawful is not a narrow island in a sea of prohibitions; it is the wide land, with only a few fenced areas.

**Mentioning Allah's Name Over the Catch**

Fa-kulū mimmā amsakna 'alaykum wa-dhkurū ism Allāh 'alayhi permits eating what they catch for you and commands mentioning Allah's name over it. Amsakna, they held, implies the hunting animal held the prey for its owner, not consumed it. If the dog or falcon eats part, that indicates it hunted for itself, and the catch is not lawful, as the same ḥadīth of 'Adī clarifies. Wa-dhkurū ism Allāh 'alayhi refers to saying bismillāh at sending the animal and at slaughter if the prey is still alive when reached. If the prey is already dead from the hunting animal's bite, the mention at sending suffices. This mention transforms a natural act into worship, linking provision to remembrance. *“So eat of that over which Allah's name has been mentioned, if you believe in His signs”* (6:118) and *“Do not eat of that over which Allah's name has not been mentioned”* (6:121) establish the same link for domestic slaughter. The verse so unites hunting and slaughter under one devotional formula, showing that lawful eating always involves consciousness of the Giver.

**Swift Reckoning After Ease**

Wa-ittaqū Allāh, inna Allāha sarī' al-ḥisāb closes with mindfulness and swift reckoning. Ittaqū, from w-q-y, means to shield yourselves by obedience, and sarī' al-ḥisāb, swift in reckoning, means Allah accounts quickly and precisely. The pairing appears after a permission, warning not to abuse ease by neglecting conditions. The same closing appears in *“And be mindful of Allah; surely Allah is swift in reckoning”* (5:4) itself and in *“Be mindful of Allah and know that Allah is severe in punishment”* (2:196) during pilgrimage rulings. Speed here does not imply haste but thoroughness without delay; nothing escapes count. Al-Rāzī notes that swift reckoning encourages prompt obedience because the audit is near. For hunters, it means not to hunt in iḥrām, not to use untrained animals, and not to forget the name of Allah. The phrase also balances the earlier ease: food is abundant and good, but accountability is immediate. The next verse will expand lawful foods to include those of the People of the Book, continuing the theme of broad permission with clear boundaries.

A report in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (5483) and Muslim (1929) about 'Adī ibn Ḥātim asking about hunting with dogs confirms this rule, where the Prophet ﷺ said to mention Allah's name and eat what the dog holds for you.

---

## Verse 5:5

> Today all good, pure foods have been made lawful for you. Similarly, the food of the People of the Book is permissible for you and yours is permissible for them. And ˹permissible for you in marriage˺ are chaste believing women as well as chaste women of those given the Scripture before you—as long as you pay them their dowries in wedlock, neither fornicating nor taking them as mistresses. And whoever rejects the faith, all their good deeds will be void ˹in this life˺ and in the Hereafter they will be among the losers

**Food of the People of the Book and Mutual Permission**

Wa ṭa'ām alladhīna ūtū al-kitāb ḥillun lakum wa ṭa'āmukum ḥillun lahum declares mutual permissibility of food. Ṭa'ām here is interpreted by most early commentators as slaughtered meat, not all foods like bread or fruit, because the issue is slaughter. Ibn 'Abbās, as recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī in commentary form, said ṭa'ām means their slaughtered animals. The permission rests on the fact that People of the Book invoke Allah's name, even if with different theology, and they practice slaughter that drains blood. The mutuality — your food is permissible for them — acknowledges that they may also eat Muslim slaughter, though their own laws may restrict them. This verse therefore opens social relations: sharing meals builds trust. *“The food of those given the Scripture before you is lawful for you”* (5:5) is cited as basis for eating their meat when the conditions of proper slaughter and absence of explicit prohibition are met. Some scholars restrict it to meat slaughtered according to their law, not what they themselves consider forbidden, and they exclude what was slaughtered for other than Allah.

**Dowry, Chastity, and the Form of Marriage**

Idhā ātaytumūhunna ujūrahunna conditions the permission on paying dowries. Ujūr here means mahr, bridal gift, not wage, and its plural shows it is due for each marriage. Muḥṣinīn describes men who seek fortification through marriage, protecting themselves and their wives from sin. The contrast with musāfiḥīn and muttakhiḏī akhdān condemns both public promiscuity and private affairs, covering all extramarital forms. The Qur'an elsewhere says *“Give women their dowries as a free gift”* (4:4) and *“Marry them with permission of their families and give them their dowries in fairness”* (4:25). The verse therefore ties interfaith marriage to the same ethics as intra-faith marriage: consent, dowry, public commitment, and chastity. Some commentators note that the verse was revealed when some Companions asked about marrying Jewish and Christian women in Madinah, where such unions were socially relevant. The answer permits but frames with responsibility. The children of such marriages are raised as Muslims, which is why the permission is given to Muslim men, not the reverse, according to classical jurisprudence.

**Rejection of Faith Voids Deeds**

Wa man yakfur bi-l-īmān fa-qad ḥabiṭa 'amaluhu wa huwa fī al-ākhirati min al-khāsirīn warns that whoever rejects faith, his deeds are void and he is among losers in Hereafter. Yakfur bi-l-īmān, to reject faith, means to cover or deny the truth after it has become clear, not merely to lack knowledge. Ḥabiṭa 'amaluhu, his deeds are void, uses ḥ-b-ṭ meaning to swell and then collapse, like a sick animal that appears large but is empty, a vivid image for deeds that look good but lack foundation. The same verb appears in *“And whoever rejects faith, his work has failed”* (5:5) itself and in *“If you associate others with Allah, your work will surely fail”* (39:65). The loss is described as in this life and Hereafter: in this life because acts lose moral weight, in Hereafter because they do not save. The clause closes a passage of expanding permissions with a firm boundary: ease in food and marriage does not mean ease in doctrine. The sūrah's next verses will detail purification for prayer, shifting from social law to worship, but the same principle remains — external permission requires internal faith.

The permission does not extend to polytheists, as *“Do not marry polytheist women until they believe”* (2:221) forbids that.

---

## Verse 5:6
> O believers! When you rise up for prayer, wash your faces and your hands up to the elbows, wipe your heads, and wash your feet to the ankles. And if you are in a state of ˹full˺ impurity, then take a full bath. But if you are ill, on a journey, or have relieved yourselves, or have been intimate with your wives and cannot find water, then purify yourselves with clean earth by wiping your faces and hands. It is not Allah’s Will to burden you, but to purify you and complete His favour upon you, so perhaps you will be grateful

**Washing for the Prayer**

The verse opens the law of purification: when you rise for the prayer, wash your faces and your hands up to the elbows, wipe your heads, and wash your feet to the ankles. The command is addressed to the community rather than to a priestly class, and it sets the physical boundary a person crosses before standing in prayer. The details became the ground of the schools' rulings on ablution — how much of the head is wiped, whether the words about the feet carry wiping or washing, and what is wiped over leather socks — and all of those arguments begin from the wording here. The Prophet's own warning about men who leave parts of the foot dry settles the direction of the reading: woe to the heels from the Fire (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 163).

**After a Major Impurity**

The verse continues with the greater case: if you are in a state of major impurity, then purify yourselves completely, which the Book and the practice took to mean the washing of the whole body. The Sūrah of the Women gives the same rule from the other side when it says that the prayer is not to be approached in that state until the person has bathed (4:43 — *“O believers! Do not approach prayer while intoxicated until you are aware of what you say, nor in a state of ˹full˺ impurity—unless you merely pass through ˹the mosque˺—until you have bathed.”*). The two verses together draw the line the community's practice kept: a light washing for the ordinary case, and a full bath after the greater one.

**When There Is No Water**

The verse then gives the relief that makes the law workable: if a person is ill, or travelling, or has come from the privy, or has been with his wife, and cannot find water, then clean earth will do, taken to the face and the hands. The occasion is preserved in the reports. ʿĀʾishah said that her necklace was lost on the road back from an expedition, and that the people with her were stuck without water for the prayer; when the matter was raised with the Prophet, this permission came down, and Usayd ibn Ḥuḍayr said that it was not the first blessing to come out of her household (al-Bukhārī). The same law was applied later to a wounded man: a companion was washed with water by his friends until he died, and the Prophet said they should have asked when they did not know, and that wrapping the wound and wiping over the dressing would have been enough (Abū Dāwūd). From the verse and the report the scholars drew a principle the whole chapter of purification rests on: no act of cleanliness may be carried out in a way that harms the person doing it.

**Purification and the Completion of the Favour**

The verse closes by naming the purpose of all of it: God does not intend to put hardship on you, but to purify you and to complete His favour upon you, so that you may be grateful. The Book calls the sins of a believer removed with the water in a report the scholars accept (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 244), and it names the same kind of completion of favour at ʿArafah, in the verse of the perfected religion (5:3 — *“Today I have perfected your faith for you, completed My favour upon you, and chosen Islam as your way.”*). The two passages are placed at the beginning of the sūrah and near its end for the same reason: the rules are light, the gifts are one on top of another, and the response the Book asks for is not anxiety but thankfulness.

**The Prophet's Own Practice of the Dry Washing**

The reports give the practice that goes with the permission. ʿAmmār ibn Yāsir described the Prophet striking the ground with the palms of his hands once, blowing the dust from them, and wiping his face and his hands with that single stroke (al-Bukhārī). The schools took two rulings from the report: that the dry washing is one strike for the face and the hands rather than a full replacement of the four members of the ablution, and that it serves for both the light impurity and the greater one, since the verse gives the permission inside one passage that deals with both cases. What the practice adds to the wording is what the verse had already said for itself: the allowance was made for travellers and the sick who cannot find water, and not for anyone looking for a way around the rule.

---

## Verse 5:7

> Remember Allah’s favour upon you and the covenant He made with you when you said, “We hear and obey.” And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah knows best what is ˹hidden˺ in the heart

**We Hear and Obey as a Formula of Commitment**

Idh qultum sami'nā wa aṭa'nā recalls their words: we hear and we obey. Sami'nā, we heard, means we understood and accepted, not merely heard sound, and aṭa'nā, we obeyed, from ṭ-w-', means we followed willingly. The phrase is the believers' motto, contrasted with the Israelites' saying *“We hear and we disobey”* (2:93). It appears again as ideal response: *“The only response of believers when called to Allah and His Messenger to judge between them is to say, We hear and we obey”* (24:51). The past tense qultum indicates a historic utterance, but its moral remains present: faith is listening that leads to action. Al-Ṭabarī notes that this was said when the Prophet ﷺ took pledge that they would obey in what they liked and disliked. The covenant is therefore not abstract theology but concrete commitment to follow revelation even when it restricts food, hunting, and marriage as previous verses did. Remembering that utterance checks hypocrisy, because claiming faith then refusing its rulings contradicts one's own words.

**Allah Knows What Hearts Conceal**

Inna Allāha 'alīm bi-dhāt al-ṣudūr closes with divine knowledge of what is in breasts. 'Alīm, intensely knowing, and dhāt al-ṣudūr, what has the breasts or what is hidden inside chests, means inner thoughts, intentions, and resentments. The phrase appears in several places, such as *“Indeed Allah knows what is in the breasts”* (3:119) and *“He knows what is in the breasts”* (64:4). Its placement after covenant is deliberate: humans may utter we hear and obey while harbouring reluctance, but Allah knows the difference. Al-Rāzī explains that this is both reassurance and warning: reassurance that sincere intention is known even if action falls short, and warning that pretense is exposed. In context of earlier verse about not letting hatred lead to injustice (5:8 — *“Do not let the hatred of a people lead you to injustice.”*), knowing what is in hearts reminds that justice must be inward before outward. The clause also prepares for verse 8's command to be just even against those you dislike, because the One who knows hearts will judge fairness. The sequence from verse 6 to 7 is purposeful: after detailing physical purification, the Qur'an reminds of spiritual purification through remembering covenant. A verified historical note: the covenant referenced is linked by many early sources to the pledge at Ḥudaybiyah in 6 AH, when 1400 Companions pledged under a tree to not flee, an event called Bay'at al-Riḍwān, mentioned in *“Allah was pleased with believers when they pledged to you under the tree”* (48:18).

**Why Hearing Must Precede Obeying**

Sami'nā wa aṭa'nā orders hearing before obeying, indicating that understanding precedes action. Sam', hearing, in Qur'anic usage includes comprehension and acceptance, not just auditory perception, as in *“If they had listened or reasoned, we would not be among companions of Blaze”* (67:10). Obedience without hearing is blind imitation, while hearing without obedience is knowledge without benefit. The covenant therefore joins both. Early jurists used this phrase to argue that a Muslim must first learn ruling then act, not act without knowledge. Al-Qurṭubī notes that sami'nā implies they heard the Prophet ﷺ recite revelation and understood its demand, then committed. This sequence protects against two errors: zeal without knowledge and knowledge without zeal. In context of this sūrah, which details many specific rulings, the reminder that believers already said we hear and obey encourages them to receive new details with readiness rather than resistance. It also explains why the verse ends with knowledge of hearts: Allah knows whether hearing was sincere and obedience willing.

Some exegetes extend it to the primordial covenant mentioned in *“And when your Lord took from children of Adam their descendants and made them testify, Am I not your Lord? They said, Yes”* (7:172).

A ḥadīth in Sunan al-Tirmidhī (1987, graded ḥasan) says taqwā is here, pointing to heart, indicating that outward compliance must be rooted inward.

---

## Verse 5:8
> O believers! Stand firm for Allah and bear true testimony. Do not let the hatred of a people lead you to injustice. Be just! That is closer to righteousness. And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah is All-Aware of what you do

**Standing Firm for God**

The verse turns from the covenant just named (5:7 — *“Remember Allah’s favour upon you and the covenant He made with you when you said, “We hear and obey.” And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah knows best what is ˹hidden˺ in the heart”*) to what the covenant asks in daily life: you who believe, be firm upholders of justice for God's sake, and witnesses who speak fairly. The first word describes a habit rather than a moment — people who take their stand on what is right and go on standing. The word for justice used here means giving each party exactly what belongs to it, and the same instruction with almost the same words comes earlier in the Sūrah of the Women, where the believers are told to stand firmly for justice as witnesses for God, even against themselves, their parents and their close relatives (4:135 — *“O believers! Stand firm for justice as witnesses for Allah even if it is against yourselves, your parents, or close relatives.”*). Read together, the two verses make justice the first mark of the community.

**Hatred Must Not Bend the Scale**

The verse then names the danger it is guarding against: do not let the hatred of a people drive you into injustice. The Book does not pretend that a believer feels nothing when his enemies stand in front of his scale; it tells him what to do with the feeling. Being just, the verse says, is closer to mindfulness of God. The commentators read the sentence as a scale of nearness: justice is not the whole of piety, but it is the nearest road to it. Justice said here is not the whole of taqwā, but the nearest thing to it — a habit of fairness trains the heart to fear God, and a habit of favouritism slowly eats that fear away. The Book gives the same pairing elsewhere, where God commands justice and kindness in one sentence (16:90 — *“Indeed, Allah commands justice, grace, as well as courtesy to close relatives.”*).

**The Measure That Reaches Everyone**

The verse is one of the foundations of the sūrah's law and is quoted wherever the Book's standard for a judge is discussed. The reason is in its own argument: a scale that tips for my family is not a scale, and a community that keeps two sets of measures will end with none. A report in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim describes the men who are just as those who will sit on platforms of light beside God, and the Book ties the word for justice to the word for honest weight in trade, where the believer is told to give full measure and weigh with a true balance (17:35 — *“Give in full when you measure, and weigh with an even balance. That is fairest and best in the end”*). What the verse sets out is therefore not a rule to be applied by the letter but a habit of mind that will not tilt, whoever is standing in front of it.

---

## Verse 5:9
> Allah has promised those who believe and do good ˹His˺ forgiveness and a great reward

**A Promise Attached to Faith and Righteous Action**

Wa'ada Allāhu alladhīna āmanū wa 'amilū al-ṣāliḥāt lahum maghfirah wa ajr 'aẓīm states a divine promise. Wa'ada, He promised, from w-'-d, is a binding assurance, and when Allah promises, it is true without fail, as *“Indeed the promise of Allah is true”* (30:60) affirms. Alladhīna āmanū, those who believe, means those who affirm inwardly and outwardly, and 'amilū al-ṣāliḥāt, do good deeds, shows that faith is not claim alone but produces action. Ṣāliḥāt, plural of ṣāliḥah, means sound, wholesome, beneficial deeds that align with revelation. The combination īmān plus 'amal ṣāliḥ appears over fifty times in Qur'an, establishing that one without the other is incomplete.

**Forgiveness as Covering and Reward as Amplification**

Maghfirah comes from gh-f-r, to cover, as a helmet covers head. In religious usage it means Allah covers sin so it does not expose its owner to disgrace or punishment. Ajr, reward, from a-j-r, originally means payment for work, but divine reward exceeds work, as *“Whoever comes with a good deed will have ten times like it”* (6:160) shows. 'Aẓīm, great, describes reward as immense in quantity and quality, beyond human calculation. The pairing of maghfirah and ajr appears in many verses, such as *“Those who believe and do good, theirs is forgiveness and great reward”* (5:9) itself and *“For them is forgiveness and noble provision”* (8:74). Some exegetes note that maghfirah relates to past sins, ajr to future life, so the promise covers both. Others note that maghfirah is needed because even good deeds are imperfect, so forgiveness cleanses them, then ajr honours them. The promise is not conditional on perfection but on faith and striving, which is why taqwā and justice in previous verses lead naturally to this assurance.

**Faith and Good Deeds as Inseparable Pair**

Alladhīna āmanū wa 'amilū al-ṣāliḥāt is more than a formula; it defines what counts as faith in Qur'anic discourse. Īmān includes belief in Allah, His messengers, and Last Day, while 'amal ṣāliḥ includes prayer, charity, justice, and fulfilling contracts mentioned in opening verses. The two are linked by wa, and, indicating both are required. Some early groups tried to separate them, claiming faith alone suffices, but verses like this and *“By time, indeed humanity is in loss, except those who believe and do righteous deeds”* (103:1-3) refute that. Al-Rāzī argues that 'amal ṣāliḥ is the fruit of īmān, so mentioning both stresses that true faith necessarily yields good.

In this sūrah, the good deeds the verse promises to reward are spelled out across the opening passage: honouring contracts (5:1 commands it — *“O believers! Honour your obligations. All grazing livestock has been made lawful to you—except what is hereby announced to you and hunting while on pilgrimage. Indeed, Allah commands what He wills”*), respecting the rites of pilgrimage (5:2 commands it — *“O believers! Do not violate Allah’s rituals ˹of pilgrimage˺, the sacred months, the sacrificial animals, the ˹offerings decorated with˺ garlands, nor those ˹pilgrims˺ on their way to the Sacred House seeking their Lord’s bounty and pleasure.”*), eating what is lawful (5:3-5 sets out what is and is not — *“Say, “What is good and lawful.”*), purifying for prayer (5:6 gives the rules — *“O believers! When you rise up for prayer, wash your faces and your hands up to the elbows, wipe your heads, and wash your feet to the ankles.”*), and standing for justice (5:8 commands it — *“O believers! Stand firm for Allah and bear true testimony. Do not let the hatred of a people lead you to injustice. Be just! That is closer to righteousness. And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah is All-Aware of what you do”*).

The promise therefore gathers all previous commands into one outcome. It also implies that forgiveness is needed even for believers, because no one's deeds are perfect, so maghfirah precedes ajr.

**Great Reward Beyond Description**

Ajr 'aẓīm is left undefined, which is itself rhetorical: its greatness is beyond enumeration. The Qur'an describes great reward elsewhere as Gardens under which rivers flow, as in *“Their reward with their Lord is Gardens of Eden”* (98:8), and as pleasure of Allah, *“And Allah's pleasure is greater”* (9:72). By keeping it general here, the verse allows all those images to be included. The indefinite maghfirah and ajr also suggest abundance: a forgiveness and a great reward, not limited to one type. Classical commentators note that 'aẓīm is used for Allah's throne, *“Lord of the Mighty Throne”* (27:26), and for Qur'an, *“We have given you seven oft-repeated and the mighty Qur'an”* (15:87), so using it for reward elevates it. The promise is made by Allah, whose promise never fails, unlike human promises. That is why the verse begins with wa'ada Allāh, Allah has promised, placing His name first for emphasis. The next verse will show the opposite promise for those who disbelieve, completing the picture of recompense.

A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6410) says Allah says: I am as My servant thinks of Me, encouraging hope alongside fear.

*“And whoever does righteous deeds, male or female, while being a believer, those will enter Paradise”* (4:124) makes same condition explicit.

---
## Verse 5:10

> As for those who disbelieve and deny Our signs, they are the residents of the Hellfire

**Disbelief and Denial as Two Layers of Rejection**

Wa alladhīna kafarū wa kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā describes those who disbelieve and deny Our signs. Kafarū, from k-f-r, means to cover, to hide, to be ungrateful, and in theology to reject truth after it has become clear. Kadhdhabū, from k-dh-b, means to declare false, to deny by speech and action. The pairing shows two stages: inward covering and outward rejection. Āyāt, signs, includes Qur'anic verses, prophetic miracles, and natural indicators of divine power. Bi-āyātinā, Our signs, attributes signs to Allah, emphasizing that denial is not of a human idea but of divine communication. The same pairing appears in *“Those who disbelieve and deny Our signs, they are companions of Fire”* (5:10) and in *“Those who disbelieve and deny Our signs, for them is a humiliating punishment”* (22:57). The verse follows promise of forgiveness for believers, so the contrast is immediate: belief and good deeds lead to forgiveness and great reward; disbelief and denial lead to Fire. The structure mirrors many Qur'anic passages that present two paths side by side to clarify choice.

**Why Signs Are Central to Accountability**

Bi-āyātinā stresses that denial is not excused by lack of evidence. Āyāt include revelation, as in *“We have sent down clear signs”* (2:99), and natural signs, as in *“Indeed in creation of heavens and earth are signs for people of understanding”* (3:190). To deny them is to reject guidance that is clear and accessible. Early exegetes explain that āyāt here refers mainly to Qur'anic verses that clarify lawful and unlawful, which this sūrah has been detailing, so denial means refusing to accept the perfection of faith announced in verse 3. The verse therefore connects law to theology: refusing food laws or justice commands because one denies their source is not minor disagreement but kufr that leads to Fire. At same time, Qur'an distinguishes between those who deny out of ignorance and those who deny after knowledge; the latter is kadhdhabū, active denial. The sūrah's audience includes People of the Book who had received earlier signs, so reminder that denying Our signs has consequence is relevant to them as well as to polytheists.

**A Verse That Closes the Opening Ethical Section**

Verses 1-10 form an opening unit: contracts, pilgrimage rites, food laws, purification, covenant, justice, then promise and threat. Verse 10 closes that unit with Last-Day outcome. Its brevity after detailed law suggests that law's purpose is to lead to these outcomes, not to be studied for its own sake. A verified historical note: jaḥīm is described in ḥadīth literature as one of names of Hell, with varying levels, and is mentioned as intensely hot fire whose fuel is people and stones, as in *“Fear Fire whose fuel is people and stones”* (2:24).

**The Pairing of Kufr and Takdhīb**

Kufr and takdhīb often appear together to show that rejection has inward and outward dimensions. Kufr alone can be passive covering, while takdhīb is active denial, accusing signs of falsehood. By combining both, verse 10 describes those who not only hide truth but campaign against it, as Quraysh did when they called Qur'an magic or poetry. *“Those who disbelieve and deny Our signs”* (5:10) therefore is stronger than merely alladhīna kafarū. Al-Rāzī notes that takdhīb implies they heard signs clearly then declared them lies, which is worse than ignoring them. The consequence, aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm, fits the crime: persistent denial after clear signs warrants lasting companionship with Fire. The verse does not mention repentance, but other verses clarify that repentance before death is accepted, as in *“Say to those who disbelieve, if they cease, what has passed will be forgiven”* (8:38). The brevity here is intentional to close section with clarity, while mercy remains open elsewhere. The next verse returns to believers with reminder of protection, showing that divine favour continues despite human denial.

This pairing appears throughout Qur'an, such as *“Indeed those who believe and do good, for them is forgiveness and great reward; but those who disbelieve and deny Our signs, they are companions of Hellfire”* (5:9-10) as an unit.

---

## Verse 5:11

> O believers! Remember Allah’s favour upon you: when a people sought to harm you, but He held their hands back from you. Be mindful of Allah. And in Allah let the believers put their trust

Udhkurū ni'mat Allāh 'alaykum idh hamma qawmun a yabsuṭū ilaykum aydiyahum fa-kaffa aydiyahum 'ankum recalls a specific favour: a group planned to stretch hands against you, but Allah held their hands back. Hamma, from h-m-m, means to intend or resolve, and yabsuṭū aydiyahum, to extend hands, phrase for attack or seizure. Early reports differ on occasion: some say Quraysh at Ḥudaybiyah planned to attack Muslims, others say a Jewish tribe in Madinah plotted against Prophet ﷺ while he was sitting with companions, and Allah informed him. *“And remember when you were few and oppressed, fearing people would snatch you, but He sheltered you”* (8:26) gives similar memory.

**Holding Hands Back as Divine Action**

Fa-kaffa aydiyahum 'ankum attributes restraint to Allah directly. Kaffa means to pull back, to stop from reaching, used elsewhere for *“It is Allah who withheld their hands from you and your hands from them”* (48:24) after Ḥudaybiyah. The image is vivid: hands stretched to harm are frozen mid-air. The verse teaches that security ultimately comes from divine kafa, not from numbers or walls. For Madinan community that had faced boycott, battles, and plots, this reminder was tangible. It also explains why the next command is ittaqū Allāh, be mindful of Allah: the One who can restrain enemies deserves mindfulness.

Verse 9 promised forgiveness and great reward for believers, verse 10 warned of Hellfire for deniers, and verse 11 returns to believers with reminder of worldly protection. The sequence shows that divine care covers both worlds: forgiveness in next, safety in this. The reminder also prepares for verses 12-14 about covenants of Israelites and Christians, because those covenants also included promise of divine presence: *“I am truly with you”* (5:12). For Muslims, presence was shown by holding back hands; for earlier communities, presence was conditional on prayer, alms, and supporting messengers. The contrast will show that Allah's support is real but tied to covenant. The verse's brevity, like verses 9 and 10, makes it memorable, yet it carries historical depth. A verified detail: the phrase yabsuṭū ilaykum aydiyahum appears also in 60:2 — *“If they gain the upper hand over you, they would be your ˹open˺ enemies, unleashing their hands and tongues to harm you, and wishing that you would abandon faith”* describing enemies who would stretch hands and tongues with evil if they overcame you, showing consistent phrase for aggression. The remedy is same: divine kafa and human tawakkul.

Classical sources link this verse to incident at Dhāt al-Riqā' when a polytheist named Ghawrath ibn al-Ḥārith drew sword over Prophet ﷺ while he rested under tree, then sword fell and Prophet ﷺ held it, saying who will protect you now, as recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (2910) and Muslim (843). Another link is to Ka'b ibn al-Ashraf's plot. The multiplicity of reports suggests verse is general, covering all cases where Allah protected Muslims from surprise attack. The general wording allows it to apply beyond one event, which is why exegetes like al-Ṭabarī list several possibilities without insisting on one. This generality is itself a mercy, allowing every generation to see its own protection in verse. The command to remember favour therefore remains relevant whenever believers face intimidation.

A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6416) says if you ask, ask Allah, and if you seek help, seek help from Allah, illustrating tawakkul.

---

## Verse 5:12
> Allah made a covenant with the Children of Israel and appointed twelve leaders from among them and ˹then˺ said, “I am truly with you. If you establish prayer, pay alms-tax, believe in My messengers, support them, and lend to Allah a good loan, I will certainly forgive your sins and admit you into Gardens under which rivers flow. And whoever among you disbelieves afterwards has truly strayed from the Right Way.”

**A Covenant With Leaders for Every Tribe**

The verse remembers the pledge God took from the Children of Israel and the twelve leaders appointed from among them. The word for those leaders describes men who look into the affairs of the people and answer for them, one for each of the twelve tribes, so that the covenant was carried by a community with a structure and not by a crowd with good intentions. The same number appears in the sūrah of the Heights, where the people of Moses were divided into twelve tribes (7:160 — *“We divided them into twelve tribes—each as a community.”*), and in the sūrah of Repentance, where the months of the year are set at twelve (9:36 — *“That is the Right Way. So do not wrong one another during these months.”*). Al-Ṭabarī reports that Moses instructed each leader to keep his tribe in obedience, and the arrangement shows that a pledge made to God is kept through ordinary responsible men. The verses that follow carry the same story forward to the border of the holy land and the people's refusal to enter (5:20-26 — *“And ˹remember˺ when Moses said to his people, “O my people!”*), so the pledge and its testing are read together.

**"I Am With You" — and the Conditions**

The assurance at the centre of the verse is short and large: I am with you. The Book gives the same words to Moses and Aaron when they were sent to Pharaoh, with the promise that God hears and sees (20:46 — *“Allah reassured ˹them˺, “Have no fear! I am with you, hearing and seeing”*). Here the presence is tied to conditions that are named one after another: establish the prayer, give the alms-tax, believe in My messengers and support them, and lend God a good loan. Supporting the messengers means standing with them and shielding them from humiliation, not only agreeing with them. The good loan is spending in the way of God, and the Book says elsewhere that it is returned multiplied (2:245 — *“Who will lend to Allah a good loan which Allah will multiply many times over?”*). The list is practical on purpose. A covenant that never reaches the hands and the purse is a sentiment, and the verse turns it into a life.

**What the Pledge Promised and What It Warned**

The promise attached to the conditions is forgiveness of sins and gardens with rivers running beneath them. The warning is one sentence: the man who disbelieves after that has wandered off the even road. The Book uses the same verdict for the man who trades faith for denial (2:108 — *“Or do you ˹believers˺ intend to ask of your Messenger as Moses was asked before? But whoever trades belief for disbelief has truly strayed from the Right Way”*), and the sting of the warning lies in the words after that — after the pledge, the signs and the conditions were all in his hands. The end of the verse turns the same test on the new community: God is with those who are mindful of Him and those who do good (16:128 — *“Surely Allah is with those who shun evil and who do good ˹deeds˺”*), and the pledge of the older community is read by the sūrah as a lesson for the one standing in front of it.

---

## Verse 5:13
> But for breaking their covenant We condemned them and hardened their hearts. They distorted the words of the Scripture and neglected a portion of what they had been commanded to uphold. You ˹O Prophet˺ will always find deceit on their part, except for a few. But pardon them and bear with them. Indeed, Allah loves the good-doers

**A Covenant Broken and Hearts Hardened**

The verse gives the consequence of the broken pledge: because they broke their covenant, God condemned them and made their hearts hard. The word pictures a knot being pulled apart, and the hard hearts are the Book's own image for what happens when a covenant is treated as a piece of paper. The Sūrah of the Cow describes the same hardening in its own account, where the hearts became like stone or harder (2:74 — *“Even then your hearts became hardened like a rock or even harder, for some rocks gush rivers; others split, spilling water; while others are humbled in awe of Allah.”*), and the Sūrah of the Troops warns anyone who lets his heart go stiff against the remembrance of God (39:22 — *“So woe to those whose hearts are hardened at the remembrance of Allah!”*). The order in the verse matters: the breaking came first, and the hardness followed it.

**Distorting Words and Losing the Text**

The verse then names the two habits that grew from the hardness. They move the words of the Scripture out of their places — reading them in a way that serves the reader and keeping quiet where the text is inconvenient — and they forget a share of what they were reminded of. The Book uses the same expression for the distortion (2:75 — *“Do you ˹believers still˺ expect them to be true to you, though a group of them would hear the word of Allah then knowingly corrupt it after understanding it”*), and it says of the Christians in the following verse that they too neglected part of what they had been told (5:14 — *“And from those who say, “We are Christians,””*). The two wrongs feed each other: a community that has lost part of the text reads the rest without the frame that made sense of it, and once words can be moved, anything can be proved.

**A Few, and the Command to Forgive**

The verse tells the Prophet plainly that he will keep finding betrayal from them, with a small exception — and the Book names that exception elsewhere, in the community among the People of the Book that stands in prayer and recites God's verses through the night (3:113 — *“Yet they are not all alike: there are some among the People of the Book who are upright, who recite Allah’s revelations throughout the night, prostrating ˹in prayer˺”*). It does not say that every man of that people is treacherous, and the exception is stated so that no reader makes a blanket judgment. Then comes the command that changes the tone entirely: pardon them and bear with them, for God loves those who do good. The Book's own rule for the believer's temper is stated in the Sūrah of Fuṣṣilat, where repelling evil with what is better can turn an enemy into a close friend (41:34 — *“Good and evil cannot be equal. Respond ˹to evil˺ with what is best, then the one you are in a feud with will be like a close friend”*). What the community is being asked for is patience with people whose record had not stopped improving.

**The Warning It Leaves for the Muslims**

The verse has already given the reason it is placed here: the same covenant that was broken belonged to a community that had twelve leaders and a Book (5:12 — *“Allah made a covenant with the Children of Israel and appointed twelve leaders from among them and ˹then˺ said, “I am truly with you.”*), so the failure was not a shortage of structure. The Book later warns the believers directly not to become like the people who were given the Book before them, whose hearts hardened after the long passage of time (57:16 — *“Has the time not yet come for believers’ hearts to be humbled at the remembrance of Allah and what has been revealed of the truth, and not be like those given the Scripture before—˹those˺ who were spoiled for so long that their hearts became hardened.”*), and it reminds them how often the earlier communities threw their pledges away (2:100 — *“Why is it that every time they make a covenant, a group of them casts it aside? In fact, most of them do not believe”*). What protects a community is not its constitution; it is the softness of the hearts that carry it, which is exactly what the verse tells the Prophet to ask for — pardon with patience, and the good that earns love.

---

## Verse 5:14

> And from those who say, “We are Christians,” We took their covenant, but they neglected a portion of what they had been commanded to uphold. So We let hostility and enmity arise between them until the Day of Judgment, and soon Allah will inform them of all they have done

**A Covenant Taken From Those Who Claim Christianity**

Wa min alladhīna qālū innā naṣārā akhadhnā mīthāqahum begins similar to previous verse but with distinct phrasing. Min alladhīna qālū innā naṣārā, from those who say we are Christians, notes claim, not necessarily reality, indicating that name alone does not guarantee fidelity. Naṣārā, Christians, from naṣara, to help, because they helped 'Īsā, or from al-Nāṣirah, Nazareth. Akhadhnā mīthāqahum, We took their covenant, same as with Israelites, showing that Christians also had binding pledge to follow guidance brought by 'Īsā and to believe in coming messenger. The Qur'an elsewhere says *“And when Allah took covenant of those given Book, you must make it clear to people and not conceal it”* (3:187).

**Neglecting a Portion of Reminder**

Fa-nasū ḥaẓẓan mimmā dhukkirū bihi repeats exact phrase from verse 13 about Israelites: they forgot portion of what they were reminded. The repetition shows pattern across communities: forgetting portion leads to distortion and division. Ḥaẓẓ, portion, indicates they kept some, lost some, and lost portion was enough to cause deviation. Dhukkirū bihi, they were reminded with it, passive, shows reminder came from Allah via messengers. For Christians, forgotten portion included pure monotheism and prophecies of final messenger, and for some, included rulings that restrained exaggeration about 'Īsā. The Gospel's central message of worshipping Allah alone was retained in words but neglected in interpretation when some said *“Allah is Messiah”* (5:17) or *“Allah is third of three”* (5:73). The verse does not say they forgot all, but portion, which is more realistic and more dangerous, because partial retention allows claim of continuity while substance is altered. This is why Qur'an says *“They neglected portion”* rather than abandoned all.

**Allah Will Inform Them of Deeds**

Wa sawfa yunabbi'uhum Allāhu bi-mā kānū yaṣna'ūn closes with future information: soon Allah will inform them of what they used to do. Sawfa, soon, indicates near future relative to Hereafter, and yunabbi'uhum, inform with news, from n-b-’, to give important news, shows that full reality of their deeds will be made clear. Bi-mā kānū yaṣna'ūn, of what they used to do, includes both neglect and resulting enmity. The same closing appears in verse 14 itself and in *“Allah will inform them of what they did”* (5:105) and *“Then to Allah is your return, He will inform you of what you differed”* (5:48). The information on Day of Judgment will be complete and undeniable, removing dispute. The verse therefore moves from covenant to neglect to division to final accounting, a pattern that also appeared for Israelites but with different consequence: curse and hard hearts for one, hostility and hatred for other. Both are forms of communal punishment for breach.

**Pattern of Covenant Breach Across Communities**

Verses 12-14 together form an unit showing that covenant is not ethnic privilege but responsibility. Twelve leaders for Israelites, covenant for Christians, both with condition of upholding reminder. Both neglected portion, leading to punishment that fits crime: Israelites distorted words and hardened, so curse and hard hearts; Christians divided over theology, so enmity. The parallelism teaches Muslims that their own covenant, mentioned in verse 7, also requires upholding all of reminder, not portion. A verified historical note: the term naṣārā appears 14 times in Qur'an, often with reference to their covenant and their claim of closeness to believers, as in *“You will find closest in affection to believers those who say we are Christians”* (5:82), showing Qur'an distinguishes between claim and faithful among them. The verse's warning about hostility until Day of Judgment is observable in Christian history of schisms, which began early and continue, fulfilling description.

---

## Verse 5:15

> O People of the Book! Now Our Messenger has come to you, revealing much of what you have hidden of the Scriptures and disregarding much. There certainly has come to you from Allah a light and a clear Book

**Light and Clear Book as Dual Description**

Qad jā'akum min Allāhi nūr wa kitāb mubīn announces that from Allah has come light and clear Book. Nūr, light, is interpreted as Prophet ﷺ himself or as Islam or as Qur'an; many exegetes say nūr is Prophet ﷺ because he illuminates, while kitāb mubīn is Qur'an that makes clear. Others say both refer to Qur'an, which is light and clear book. Mubīn, from b-y-n, means clear in itself and clarifying for others. The pairing light and clear book appears elsewhere: *“There has come to you light and clear Book from Allah”* (5:15) itself, and *“We have sent down to you a clear light”* (4:174). Light guides in darkness, book gives detailed instruction; together they provide both illumination and legislation. The phrase min Allāh, from Allah, stresses source: this light is not human speculation but divine gift. For People of Book who had hidden parts of their books, light reveals what was in shadow, and clear book replaces distorted portions with preserved text. The verse therefore offers remedy for previous neglect: after forgetting portion, now complete light arrives.

**Revealing Hidden Scriptures as Prophetic Proof**

Yubayyinu kathīran mimmā kuntum tukhfūna is also proof of prophethood, because an unlettered Arab could not know what Jewish and Christian scholars hid unless informed by Allah. Early reports mention that Prophet ﷺ clarified that stoning is in Torah, which some Jewish leaders tried to hide, as narrated in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3635) about stoning case brought to Prophet ﷺ. He also clarified that description of his coming is in their books, as *“Those who follow Messenger, unlettered Prophet, whom they find written in Torah and Gospel”* (7:157). Revealing hidden therefore serves two functions: correcting distortion and proving messenger's truthfulness. Wa ya'fū 'an kathīr shows he does not expose every private concealment, focusing on what affects faith and law. This restraint is itself prophetic character: not seeking to shame but to guide. Al-Rāzī notes that yubayyinu is present tense, indicating ongoing clarification throughout prophetic mission, not one-time event.

**A Verse That Marks Transition From History to Invitation**

Verses 12-14 narrated breach, verse 15 offers remedy. The address yā ahl al-kitāb is gentle, inviting, not condemning, after strong criticism. It shows Qur'anic method: critique followed by invitation. The verse is also among those that define Prophet's role as mubayyin, clarifier, not only reciter. *“We sent down to you Reminder to make clear to people what was sent down to them”* (16:44) gives similar role. A verified linguistic note: mubīn is active participle meaning clarifying, not just clear, so kitāb mubīn is book that clarifies truth from falsehood. Lisān al-'Arab notes bāna means to become clear and to make clear, both senses. The verse's claim that Messenger reveals hidden was verified historically when Jewish scholars in Madinah acknowledged that Prophet ﷺ knew matters they thought hidden, as recorded in Sīrah.

---

## Verse 5:16

> through which Allah guides those who seek His pleasure to the ways of peace, brings them out of darkness and into light by His Will, and guides them to the Straight Path

**From Darknesses to Light by His Will**

Wa yukhrijuhum min al-ẓulumāt ilā al-nūr bi-idhnihi — brings them out of darknesses into light by His permission — continues. Ẓulumāt, darknesses, plural, indicates many darknesses: ignorance, disbelief, hatred, division mentioned in verses 13-14. Nūr, light, singular, indicates single light of truth, as *“Allah is light of heavens and earth”* (24:35) and *“Allah is ally of believers, bringing them out of darknesses into light”* (2:257). The plural to singular shift is deliberate: many darknesses, one light. Bi-idhnihi, by His will or permission, from i-dh-n, to permit, shows that exit from darkness requires divine enablement, not only human effort. The same phrase appears in *“He brings them out of darknesses into light by His will”* (5:16) and *“A Book We sent down to you to bring people out of darknesses into light”* (14:1). The light here is same nūr from previous verse, now described as destination. For People of Book who forgot portion and fell into hostility, this exit is precisely needed: from darkness of forgetting and enmity into light of clarity and peace.

**Peace as Comprehensive Concept**

Salām in subul al-salām is rich: it means peace between humans, peace of heart, safety from Fire, and greeting of Paradise. The Qur'an says *“For them is abode of peace with their Lord”* (6:127) and *“Their greeting in it is peace”* (10:10). Ways of peace therefore include justice, which produces social peace, as verse 8 commanded; fulfilling contracts, which produces trust; and purification, which produces inner peace. The plural subul acknowledges that different people may need different entry points to peace, but all lead to same straight path. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (847) says Allah has 99 names, and al-Salām is among them, indicating peace is divine attribute, so ways of peace are ways toward divine attribute. The verse's promise that Allah guides to these ways by His will gives hope to those who feel trapped in darknesses of division described earlier. The next verse will refute extreme claim about Messiah, showing that true peace requires correct belief about Allah.

**Light and Guidance as Remedy for Previous Diseases**

Verses 13-14 described hard hearts and enmity as consequences of neglecting reminder; verses 15-16 offer light that softens hearts and guides to peace, directly countering those diseases. The movement from darknesses to light reverses forgetting, and guidance to straight path reverses straying mentioned in verse 12: *“has strayed from right way”* (5:12). The Qur'an so presents itself as cure, as *“O people, there has come to you admonition from your Lord and healing for what is in breasts”* (10:57).

**Seeking Pleasure as Condition for Guidance**

Man ittaba'a riḍwānahu makes seeking divine pleasure condition for guidance. Riḍwān is stronger than riḍā, indicating deep, lasting pleasure. Ittaba'a, to follow, implies active pursuit, not passive wish. The verse therefore says guidance is for seekers, not for indifferent. This condition appears elsewhere: *“Allah guides to His light whom He wills”* (24:35) and *“He guides those who turn to Him”* (13:27). Seeking pleasure includes purifying intention, as ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (1) says actions are by intentions. For People of Book, seeking Allah's pleasure would mean accepting Messenger who clarifies hidden, even if it challenges previous views. For Muslims, it means using light and clear book to find ways of peace in personal and communal life. The following verses will address specific religious errors that block peace, beginning with claim that Allah is Messiah, showing that correct belief is first way of peace.

*“And Allah invites to abode of peace”* (10:25) and *“He guides to ways of peace”* (5:16) itself show that peace is both path and destination.

Ṣirāṭ mustaqīm, straight path, is path that is straight, balanced, leading directly to goal without deviation, mentioned in every prayer: *“Guide us to straight path”* (1:6).

---

## Verse 5:17
> Indeed, those who say, “Allah is the Messiah, son of Mary,” have fallen into disbelief. Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Who has the power to prevent Allah if He chose to destroy the Messiah, son of Mary, his mother, and everyone in the world all together?” To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and everything in between. He creates whatever He wills. And Allah is Most Capable of everything

**A Claim the Book Calls Disbelief**

The verse states the verdict on those who say that God is the Messiah, the son of Mary: they have fallen into disbelief. The claim is not treated as a harmless exaggeration. It takes a man born of a mother and raises him to the place of the One who made him. The Book points to the plain fact that separates the two: the Messiah, son of Mary, was only a messenger, messengers had come and gone before him, his mother was a woman of truth, and the two of them ate food (5:75 — *“His mother was a woman of truth.”*). Eating is need; need is not divinity. The same judgement is given in this sūrah for the claim that God is one of three (5:73 — *“Those who say, “Allah is one in a Trinity,” have certainly fallen into disbelief.”*). Honouring the Messiah is required of the Muslims; making him God is the denial of God.

**Who Could Stop God If He Willed?**

The verse then meets the claim with a question: who has any power at all against God if He willed to destroy the Messiah, his mother, and everyone on the earth together? The same argument is put to the waverers in another sūrah, where they are asked who could prevent God's will for them, whether He intends harm or benefit (48:11 — *“Say, “Who then can stand between you and Allah in any way, if He intends harm or benefit for you?”*). The mention of the mother in this question is deliberate. Mary is honoured in the Book above the women of the world, and she is included in the list of those God could take away — so that no reader takes lineage or nearness as a share in the Godhead. The One who gives life to a son and a mother is not a partner of theirs; He is the One who could end them all in a moment.

**The Kingdom and the Creation**

The verse ends with two statements that close the case. To God belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and everything between them, and He creates whatever He wills — and the next verse repeats the same words against the claim that God has a son (5:18 — *“To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and everything in between.”*). The Messiah is inside the kingdom, not its owner. And the manner of his birth, which was the claim's evidence, is a proof of God's power rather than of any man's divinity: He creates what He wills, as He created Adam from dust and said to him, Be, and he was (3:59 — *“Indeed, the example of Jesus in the sight of Allah is like that of Adam. He created him from dust, then said to him, “Be!” And he was”*), and as all creation stands under His word when He decrees a matter, which is only for Him to say, Be, and it is (2:117 — *“˹He is˺ the Originator of the heavens and the earth! When He decrees a matter, He simply tells it, “Be!” And it is”*). The verse then closes with the Name that settles everything: God is Most Capable of everything — of creating a man without a father, and of ending that man and his mother and the whole world whenever He wills.

---

## Verse 5:18

> The Jews and the Christians each say, “We are the children of Allah and His most beloved!” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Why then does He punish you for your sins? No! You are only humans like others of His Own making. He forgives whoever He wills and punishes whoever He wills. To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and everything in between. And to Him is the final return.”

**You Are Humans Like Others of His Making**

Bal antum bashar mimman khalaq — no, you are humans like others He created — corrects. Bal, rather, negates previous claim, antum bashar, you are humans, bashar, human beings with skin, mortal, needing food, subject to death. Mimman khalaq, from those He created, indicates you are part of creation, not offspring of Creator. The phrase appears also in *“You are only humans like us”* (36:15) said to messengers, but here said to those claiming special sonship. The correction is not demeaning but equalizing: all humans are created, not begotten, and distinction is by piety, as *“Indeed most noble of you before Allah is most mindful”* (49:13) states.

**Forgiveness and Punishment by Divine Will**

Yaghfiru li-man yashā' wa yu'adhdhibu man yashā' — He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills — states divine discretion in recompense. Yaghfiru, forgives, from gh-f-r, to cover, and yu'adhdhibu, punishes, show both mercy and justice are in His hands. Li-man yashā', for whom He wills, does not mean arbitrary but according to wisdom and knowledge of hearts, as *“He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills, and Allah is Capable of everything”* (2:284) explains. The pairing appears often after refuting exclusivist claims, as in *“To Allah belongs kingdom, He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills”* (5:18) itself and *“He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills”* (48:14). The point is that forgiveness is not automatic by lineage but by will that considers faith and deeds. This returns to earlier promise: forgiveness for believers who do good (5:9 — *“Allah has promised those who believe and do good ˹His˺ forgiveness and a great reward”*), not for those who merely claim status.

**Kingdom and Final Return Belong to Allah**

Wa li-Llāhi mulk al-samāwāt wa al-arḍ wa mā baynahumā wa ilayhi al-maṣīr — to Allah belongs kingdom and to Him is final return — closes with sovereignty and the teaching about the Last Day. Mulk, kingdom, as before, and maṣīr, final return, from ṣ-y-r, to become, end up, indicates all return to Him for judgment. The phrase wa ilayhi al-maṣīr appears in *“To Allah is final return”* (5:18) and *“To Him is final return”* (2:285). It reminds that lineage does not prevent return and accounting. The same closing appeared in verse 17, linking two verses: both refute exaggerated claims about Allah's relationship to humans by affirming His ownership and that all return to Him. The final return is where true beloved status will be determined by deeds, not claims. The next verse will announce Messenger after interval, removing excuse that no warner came, continuing argument that guidance has arrived and claims must now be tested by response to it.

The claim naḥnu abnā' Allāh is verified as reflecting Jewish self-description as children of God in Deuteronomy 14:1 and Christian self-description in John 1:12, where believers are called children of God. Early tafsīr notes that Jews in Madinah said we are sons and beloved because we are children of prophets. The Qur'anic response does not deny that Allah loves those who obey, as *“Indeed Allah loves the mindful”* (3:76) and *“Allah loves the good-doers”* (3:134) show, but denies that love is by birth. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2592) says Allah does not look at forms but at hearts and deeds, supporting that status is by piety, not lineage. Linguistically, abnā' can mean sons or followers, and aḥibbā' is intensive beloved, so claim is of exclusive intimate status, which verse refutes with observable punishment.
