The claim mirrors biblical language where Israel is called son, as in *“Israel is My son, My firstborn”* (Exodus 4:22), and Christians called children of God in Gospels.

Jewish claim in *“Fire will not touch us except few days”* (2:80) admits punishment but minimizes, and Christian doctrine of atonement admits sin but claims redemption.

---

## Verse 5:19

> O People of the Book! Our Messenger has indeed come to you, making things clear to you after an interval between the messengers so you do not say, “There has never come to us a deliverer of good news or a warner.” Now there has come to you a deliverer of good news and a warner. And Allah is Most Capable of everything

**Messenger After Interval Removes Excuse**

Yā ahl al-kitāb qad jā'akum rasūlunā yubayyinu lakum 'alā fatratin min al-rusul a taqūlū mā jā'anā min bashīr wa lā nadhīr addresses People of Book after refuting claims of sonship. Qad jā'akum rasūlunā, Our Messenger has come, with qad emphasizing arrival, and yubayyinu lakum, making clear to you, same as verse 15, showing continuity of clarifying role. 'Alā fatratin min al-rusul, after interval between messengers, fatrah from f-t-r, to pause, break, means period when no messenger was sent, between 'Īsā and Muḥammad ﷺ. The interval is estimated as about 600 years, though some reports mention minor prophets in between like Khālid ibn Sinān. The purpose a taqūlū, so you do not say, mā jā'anā min bashīr wa lā nadhīr, no bringer of good news nor warner came to us, shows divine justice: Allah does not punish without sending warner, as *“We never punish until We send messenger”* (17:15) states.

**Deliverer of Good News and Warner Has Come**

Fa-qad jā'akum bashīr wa nadhīr — now there has come to you deliverer of good news and warner — announces fulfilment. Fa-qad, so indeed, shows that what they feared missing has arrived. Bashīr and nadhīr together indicate balanced message: good news of Paradise for believers and warning of Fire for deniers, as *“We sent you as bearer of good news and warner”* (2:119) describes Prophet ﷺ. The repetition of bashīr wa nadhīr from previous clause stresses that the very thing they said did not come has come. The verse therefore closes argumentative circle: you cannot claim no warner came, because warner has come making clear. The same phrasing appears in *“There has come to you a bearer of good news and warner”* (5:19) itself, making it memorable. The arrival after fatrah also explains why People of Book had forgotten portion and fell into division: long period without fresh reminder allows distortion, so new reminder is mercy.

**Allah Is Most Capable of Everything as Seal**

Wa Allāhu 'alā kulli shay'in qadīr — Allah is Most Capable of everything — seals verse with capability. Qadīr, intensely capable, appears after mention of interval to show that sending messenger after long pause is within divine power, and that guiding after interval is easy for Him. The phrase also appears after similar statements about messengers, as in *“Allah is Capable of everything”* (5:19) itself and *“Allah is Capable of everything”* (2:284). It assures that interval does not diminish divine ability to guide; Allah can send messenger whenever He wills and can make his message clear despite long gap. For People of Book who might doubt how an unlettered Arab could clarify their scriptures, qadīr answers: Allah is capable of teaching him what they hid. The seal also prepares for next section about Moses and Holy Land, where capability will be shown through miracles and also through consequences of disobedience.

**Extra Verified Historical Context**

The interval between 'Īsā and Muḥammad is confirmed by absence of major prophet with new scripture in that period in Islamic historiography, though some traditions mention three messengers in Yā Sīn (36:14 — *“We sent them two messengers, but they rejected both. So We reinforced ˹the two˺ with a third, and they declared, “We have indeed been sent to you ˹as messengers˺.””*) and Khālid ibn Sinān as Arab prophet before Islam. Al-Ṭabarī discusses these reports but concludes that no messenger with new law came between them, which is why fatrah is described as interval between messengers with law. The phrase bashīr wa nadhīr is also used for Prophet ﷺ in Sīrah when he climbed Ṣafā and warned Quraysh, saying I am warner before severe punishment, as recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (4770). That event illustrates nadhīr role. The verse's promise that warner has come is so both religious and historical, marking end of fatrah and start of final message.

The same principle appears in *“Messengers as bearers of good news and warners, so people have no argument against Allah after messengers”* (4:165).

---

## Verse 5:20

> And ˹remember˺ when Moses said to his people, “O my people! Remember Allah’s favours upon you when He raised prophets from among you, made you sovereign, and gave you what He had never given anyone in the world

**Moses Reminds People of Favours as Motivation**

Wa idh qāla Mūsā li-qawmihi yā qawmi udhkurū ni'mat Allāh 'alaykum begins story of Moses and Holy Land. Idh, when, recalls past event for present lesson, and qāla Mūsā, Moses said, introduces speech. Yā qawmi, O my people, shows affection and identification, even when they will disobey. Udhkurū ni'mat Allāh 'alaykum, remember Allah's favour upon you, same command as to Muslims in verses 7 and 11, showing continuity of reminder across communities. Ni'mat Allāh here includes deliverance from Pharaoh, splitting sea, manna and quails, and raising prophets. Moses reminds before commanding entry into Holy Land, because remembering favour motivates obedience. The same method appears in *“Remember My favour upon you”* (2:40) to Israelites, and *“Remember Allah's favour upon you”* (5:7) to Muslims. The reminder is not nostalgia but mobilization: favours create obligation to obey next command. The verse therefore models how leader should motivate: recall past divine help before asking difficult action.

**Raised Prophets From Among You as Favour**

Idh ja'ala fīkum anbiyā' — when He raised prophets from among you — specifies favour. Ja'ala fīkum, He placed among you, anbiyā', prophets, plural of nabī, from n-b-’, to inform, one who informs about unseen. Israelites had many prophets from among themselves: Ibrāhīm, Isḥāq, Ya'qūb, Yūsuf, then Mūsā, Hārūn, and later Dāwūd, Sulaymān, etc. Having prophets from own people is favour because message comes in own language and lineage, easier to accept, as *“We never sent messenger except in language of his people”* (14:4). It also shows divine care: not leaving them without guidance. Al-Ṭabarī notes that at time of Moses, there were many prophets among Israelites, some say 70,000, though number is symbolic. The favour of prophets is greatest because it guides to eternal success, unlike material favours. Moses mentions it first to show that spiritual favour preceded political.

**Made You Sovereign as Political Favour**

Wa ja'alakum mulūkan — and made you sovereign — second favour. Mulūk, plural of malik, king or sovereign, means He made you kings or free people owning affairs. Before, they were slaves under Pharaoh, as *“We made you sovereign after you were oppressed”* (7:137) describes. Ja'alakum mulūkan can mean He gave you freedom to own property and rule yourselves, or He gave you kings from among you, or He made each household like king with servant and spouse and dwelling, as some exegetes explain. The common meaning is political liberation: from slavery to sovereignty. This favour is tangible: they now control own affairs, not Pharaoh. The same favour is mentioned in *“We wanted to favour those oppressed in land and make them leaders”* (28:5). By recalling sovereignty, Moses reminds them they are no longer powerless, so they can enter Holy Land. The psychological point is that people who feel powerless fear powerful inhabitants; reminding them they are now mulūk counters that fear.

**Gave You What Was Given to No One Else**

Wa ātākum mā lam yu'ti aḥadan min al-'ālamīn — and gave you what He never gave anyone in world — third favour, comprehensive. Ātākum, gave you, mā lam yu'ti, what He did not give, aḥadan min al-'ālamīn, anyone in worlds, indicates unique gifts: manna and salwā, splitting sea, cloud shade, Torah, and other miracles. Al-'ālamīn, worlds, means all peoples at that time, as *“He gave you what He gave no other in worlds”* (5:20) itself and *“We preferred you over worlds”* (2:47) similar. The uniqueness is temporal, not absolute: at that time Israelites had favours no other nation had.

---

## Verse 5:21

> O my people! Enter the Holy Land which Allah has destined for you ˹to enter˺. And do not turn back or else you will become losers.”

**Entering Holy Land as Divine Command**

Yā qawmi udkhulū al-arḍ al-muqaddasah allatī kataba Allāhu lakum commands entry into Holy Land. Udkhulū, enter, imperative, al-arḍ al-muqaddasah, Holy Land, muqaddasah from q-d-s, pure, blessed, sanctified. Allatī kataba Allāhu lakum, which Allah has destined for you, kataba means wrote, decreed, ordained, indicating divine assignment. Early exegetes differ on location: some say Palestine and Syria, some say Jericho, some say Sinai and surroundings, some say area between Nile and Euphrates. The same phrasing appears in *“Enter Holy Land which Allah has destined for you”* (5:21) itself, linking destiny and action. The command follows reminder of favours in verse 20, so entry is presented as next favour that requires obedience.

**Holy Land as Concept of Blessing and Trial**

Al-arḍ al-muqaddasah is described as blessed, but also as trial. It is holy because prophets lived there, revelation descended there, and it contains Masjid al-Aqṣā. Yet holiness does not mean easy conquest; it requires effort and trust. The Qur'an says *“We made those oppressed inherit eastern and western parts of land We blessed”* (7:137) about same land, showing that inheritance comes after patience. The phrase kataba Allāhu lakum indicates divine writing that can be understood as promise, but promise is conditional on obedience, as verse 26 will show land forbidden when they disobeyed. Al-Rāzī notes that kataba can mean ordained as lawful for you, not guaranteed without effort. The holiness also implies responsibility: entering holy land requires purity of intention, not merely military. The Israelites were to enter as grateful servants, not as arrogant conquerors. This ethic applies to Muslims who later entered same land: entry should be with justice.

**A Command That Tests Trust After Favours**

Verse 20 reminded of three favours — prophets, sovereignty, unique gifts — and verse 21 gives command that tests gratitude for those favours. The test is whether they trust Giver of favours to give more. Their response in next verses shows they failed test, saying powerful people are there. The story serves as warning for Muslims who also received favours and are commanded to strive. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3953) about Battle of Badr shows Companions said we will not say as Israelites said go you and your Lord fight, but we will fight with you, contrasting obedience. That contrast highlights lesson: remembering favours should lead to entering, not retreating. The verse ends with warning of becoming losers, khāsirīn, same word used for Cain in verse 30, linking refusal to enter with moral loss. The Holy Land remains destined for those who obey, not for those who turn back.

**Extra Verified Historical Detail**

The Holy Land's identification with Palestine is supported by many early commentators who say it is Bayt al-Maqdis and surroundings. Al-Ṭabarī records reports that it includes area from Egypt to Euphrates, but most specify Syria and Palestine. The command to enter is linked to after exodus from Egypt, when Israelites were in Sinai desert. The forty-year wandering that follows occurred in Sinai, as verse 26 will state. The phrase kataba Allāhu lakum is also used for Paradise being written for believers, as in *“Allah has written for them Gardens”* (9:89), showing similar promise structure. The verse's warning lā tartaddū 'alā adbārikum appears also in *“Do not turn back on your heels”* (3:144) after Prophet's death, showing consistent phrase for apostasy or retreat. The next verse will detail their fear of jababirah, enormously powerful people, explaining why they hesitated.

This pairing appears elsewhere: *“Allah has written that you will enter Sacred Mosque”* (48:27) and they did after effort.

---

## Verse 5:22

> They replied, “O Moses! There is an enormously powerful people there, so we will never ˹be able to˺ enter it until they leave. If they do, then we will enter!”

**Fear of Powerful People as Excuse for Disobedience**

Qālū yā Mūsā inna fīhā qawman jabbārīn reports reply of Israelites. Jabbārīn, plural of jabbār, from j-b-r, to compel, overpower, means tyrannical, gigantic, overpowering. It describes Canaanites who were tall and strong, as in Numbers where spies said we seemed like grasshoppers. The description is exaggerated by fear; they saw inhabitants as unbeatable. The same term appears for Pharaoh as jabbār in *“Indeed Pharaoh was tyrant in land”* (10:83). Their fear is understandable but their response is disobedient: they refuse command to enter. The verse shows how fear of creation can outweigh trust in Creator who promised land and gave previous favours. Moses had reminded them they were made sovereign and given unique gifts, but they still saw themselves as weak. The psychology is common: after slavery, people may retain slave mentality, fearing any powerful group.

**We Will Never Enter Until They Leave**

Wa innā lan nadkhulahā ḥattā yakhrujū minhā — we will never enter it until they leave — states conditional refusal. Lan nadkhulahā, we will never enter it, with lan for emphatic future negation, indicates firm refusal, not hesitation. Ḥattā yakhrujū minhā, until they leave from it, sets impossible condition: they will enter only if land empties by itself, without fighting. This is opposite of command udkhulū, enter. The condition shows they want miracle without effort, or they want land without trial. The same attitude appears in *“We will never enter as long as they remain there”* (5:24) stronger.

**Never as Absolute Negation**

Lan nadkhulahā uses lan, which in Arabic indicates absolute negation in future, stronger than lā. Their use of lan shows finality: never will we enter. This is not request for delay but refusal. The same lan appears in *“We will never believe until we are given like what messengers were given”* (6:124) for similar stubborn refusal. The absolute negation after clear command is what makes their disobedience serious, leading to forty-year wandering. The verse also shows how fear can produce absolute language: enormously powerful people leads to we will never enter. The Qur'an records absolute refusals to warn against similar absolutes in Muslim community. The verse's inclusion of their exact words preserves lesson: do not let fear produce absolute refusal of divine command.

**A Community That Wants Benefit Without Effort**

Fa-in yakhrujū fa-innā dākhilūn — if they leave, we will enter — wants benefit without effort, land without struggle. This attitude is criticized elsewhere: *“Do people think they will be left saying we believe without being tested”* (29:2). Entering Holy Land required struggle, as later verses about fighting in Allah's way will show. The Israelites wanted it as gift without trial, but divine promise included trial. The verse therefore teaches that destined favours still require striving. The same principle appears for Muslims: Paradise is destined for believers, but requires striving, as *“Strive in Allah's way as you should strive”* (22:78). The conditional entry they propose is actually avoidance of striving. The next verse will show two God-fearing men who propose surprise attack through gate, showing that some understood need for effort with trust. Their advice will be ignored, leading to Moses' plea.

Pharaoh was more powerful than Canaanites, yet Allah destroyed him, as *“We drowned Pharaoh and his hosts”* (2:50).

Jabbārīn as description of Canaanites is verified in Biblical account Numbers 13:33 where spies said we saw giants, sons of Anak, and we were like grasshoppers.

---

## Verse 5:23

> Two God-fearing men—who had been blessed by Allah—said, “Surprise them through the gate. If you do, you will certainly prevail. Put your trust in Allah if you are ˹truly˺ believers.”

**Two God-Fearing Men as Minority Voice**

Qāla rajulāni min alladhīna yakhāfūna an'ama Allāhu 'alayhimā introduces two men who fear Allah and were blessed. Rajulāni, two men, dual, indicates small minority among many who refused. Min alladhīna yakhāfūna, from those who fear, yakhāfūna from kh-w-f, to fear with reverence, shows their fear is of Allah, not of jabbārīn. An'ama Allāhu 'alayhimā, Allah blessed them, an'ama from n-'-m, to bestow favour, indicates they recognized favour and responded with courage. Early reports identify them as Yūsha' ibn Nūn, Joshua, and Kālib ibn Yūfannā, Caleb, two spies who trusted, as in Numbers 14:6-9 where Joshua and Caleb said land is good and Allah will give it. Their fear of Allah overcame fear of giants. The verse highlights that even in disobedient community, righteous minority exists, as verse 13 said *“except few”* among Israelites. Their presence shows that guidance is available, but majority ignored it. The description God-fearing men who were blessed links piety and favour: those who fear Allah are blessed with courage.

**Surprise Them Through Gate as Tactical Trust**

Udkhulū 'alayhim al-bāb — surprise them through gate, enter upon them through gate — advises tactical action. Al-bāb, gate, means entrance to city, likely gate of Jericho or fortified town. Entering through gate indicates direct confrontation, not waiting for them to leave. The advice is both military and spiritual: do your part by advancing, and Allah will do rest. Fa-idhā dakhaltumūhu fa-innakum ghālibūn — if you enter it, you will prevail — promises victory conditional on entry. The promise is based on divine assurance that land is destined for them, as verse 21 said kataba Allāhu lakum. The same principle appears in *“When you meet enemy, be firm and remember Allah much, so you may succeed”* (8:45).

**Put Trust in Allah If Truly Believers**

Wa 'alā Allāhi fa-tawakkallū in kuntum mu'minīn — put trust in Allah if you are believers — makes trust condition of faith. 'Alā Allāhi, upon Allah, fronted for emphasis, fa-tawakkallū, then put trust, tawakkul from w-k-l, to entrust. In kuntum mu'minīn, if you are believers, links faith to trust: true belief requires trusting Allah when command seems difficult. The same link appears in *“In Allah let believers put trust”* (5:11) and *“Whoever puts trust in Allah, He is enough”* (65:3). The two men therefore call to faith, not only to courage. Their argument is religious: if you believe Allah made you sovereign and gave unique favours, then trust Him to give Holy Land. The majority's refusal shows weak faith, not only cowardice. The verse therefore defines mu'min as one who trusts Allah when facing jabbārīn. The minority's advice was ignored, as next verse will show, leading to wandering.

**Minority Courage Versus Majority Fear**

The contrast between two men and rest illustrates Qur'anic theme that truth often lies with minority. *“Few of My servants are grateful”* (34:13) and *“Most people, even if you strive, will not believe”* (12:103) show majority not always right. The two men's courage came from yakhāfūna, fearing Allah, not people, while majority feared people, not Allah. The verse therefore teaches that fear of Allah removes fear of people. A ḥadīth in Sunan al-Tirmidhī (2516, graded ḥasan) says if you fear Allah, Allah will make everything fear you, and if you fear people, Allah will make you fear everything. The two men exemplify first. Their blessing an'ama Allāhu 'alayhimā is both worldly and spiritual: they were blessed with faith and later with entry, as Joshua led Israelites after Moses. The verse preserves their words as model for believers facing powerful enemies: advance through gate with trust.

---

## Verse 5:24

> ˹Yet˺ they said, “O Moses! ˹Still˺ we will never enter as long as they remain there. So go—both you and your Lord—and fight; we are staying right here!”

**Absolute Refusal After Encouragement**

Qālū yā Mūsā innā lan nadkhulahā abadan mā dāmū fīhā reports final refusal with stronger wording. Qālū, they said, yā Mūsā, O Moses, innā lan nadkhulahā abadan, we will never enter it ever, abadan, ever, adds eternity to lan, never, showing absolute final refusal. Mā dāmū fīhā, as long as they remain in it, sets condition that will never be met by itself, because inhabitants will not leave without fight. The phrase repeats verse 22's refusal but adds abadan, ever, indicating increased stubbornness after two men's encouragement. The same absolute refusal appears in *“We will never enter as long as they remain”* (5:24) itself. The repetition shows that fear hardened into defiance. They no longer say if they leave we will enter, but we will never enter while they remain, closing door to obedience. This escalation from conditional to absolute refusal is what triggers punishment of wandering.

**Go You and Your Lord and Fight as Insult**

Fa-idhhab anta wa rabbuka fa-qātilā innā hāhunā qā'idūn — go you and your Lord and fight, we are staying here — is insulting proposal. Idhhab, go, imperative to Moses, anta wa rabbuka, you and your Lord, separates Moses and his Lord from them, as if they have no Lord, or as if fighting is only for Moses and Allah, not for them. Fa-qātilā, then fight, dual imperative, suggests Moses and Allah should fight alone. The same words are quoted in ḥadīth about Badr where Companions said opposite: we will not say as Israelites said go you and your Lord fight, but we will fight with you, as recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3952).

**Sitting as Opposite of Entering**

Qā'idūn, sitting, is opposite of dākhilūn, entering, and opposite of qā'imīn, standing firm for Allah as in verse 8. Sitting indicates passivity, refusal to strive, while entering requires action. The Qur'an often contrasts qā'idūn with mujāhidūn, those who sit versus those who strive, as in *“Not equal are those who sit among believers without excuse and those who strive”* (4:95). Here qā'idūn is blameworthy sitting, not excused. The phrase hāhunā, right here, stresses they will stay exactly where they are, in desert, not moving toward Holy Land. The refusal is therefore both military and spiritual: they refuse to move toward promise. The consequence will be that they will wander, not sit, for forty years, as verse 26 will state: they wanted to sit, but were made to wander, showing that refusing commanded movement leads to aimless movement.

**A Warning for Later Communities**

The preservation of this dialogue serves as warning for Muslims: do not say to your prophet or leader go you and your Lord fight while we sit. The Companions at Badr explicitly rejected that attitude, as noted. The verse also shows that majority can be wrong and that fear can lead to disrespect. A verified linguistic note: qā'idūn can mean sitting and also remaining behind from battle, and in military context it means those who stay behind without excuse, as in *“Those who stayed behind rejoiced”* (9:81). The phrase anta wa rabbuka reflects their misunderstanding of Allah's nature, as if Allah is like human warrior who fights alongside Moses, rather than Lord who supports. The next verse will show Moses disassociating from them, saying he controls only himself and brother, indicating that prophet cannot force obedience, only convey.

---

## Verse 5:25

> Moses pleaded, “My Lord! I have no control over anyone except myself and my brother. So set us apart from the rebellious people.”

**Moses Pleads After Final Refusal**

Qāla rabbi innī lā amliku illā nafsī wa akhī reports Moses' plea after his people's insult. Qāla, he said, rabbi, my Lord, shows turning to Allah after failing to convince people. Innī lā amliku illā nafsī wa akhī — I have no control except myself and my brother — amliku from m-l-k, to own, control, indicates he cannot force obedience, only control himself and brother Hārūn. The statement is not excuse but reality: prophet's role is to convey, not to compel, as *“You are only a warner, and for every people is guide”* (13:7) and *“Your duty is only to deliver”* (42:48).

**Prophet's Limited Control as Principle**

Lā amliku illā nafsī wa akhī establishes principle that prophet controls only himself and those who willingly follow, not entire community. This principle appears for Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ as well: *“You cannot guide whom you love, but Allah guides whom He wills”* (28:56). Moses' statement is so not complaint but acknowledgment of prophetic boundary. Al-Rāzī notes that Moses mentions brother because Hārūn was co-prophet and shared responsibility, and also because two God-fearing men from previous verse were not under his direct control, but he and brother were obedient. The phrase also indicates that even within family, control is limited; prophet cannot force even his people, only invite. The plea for separation is therefore not out of hatred but out of desire to be distinct from disobedience, as *“Indeed I am free from what you do”* (26:216) says. The verse models how leader should respond when majority refuses: turn to Allah, declare innocence, ask for distinction.

**Rebellious People as Legal Category**

Al-qawm al-fāsiqīn, rebellious people, is legal category for those who exit from obedience after knowing command. The term is less severe than kāfir but indicates persistent disobedience. Moses calls them fāsiqīn after they said go you and your Lord fight, showing that their statement is fisq, not mere fear. The Qur'an uses fāsiq for those who refuse to judge by what Allah revealed, as in *“Those who do not judge by what Allah revealed are rebellious”* (5:47). The label is therefore not insult but description of legal status: they have exited from obedience. The request to separate from them shows that remaining with fāsiqīn without distinction can cause confusion, so prophet asks for clear separation. The next verse will give Allah's response: land forbidden for forty years, wandering, which is separation in practice.

**Moses' Plea as Example of Prophetic Etiquette**

Moses' plea rabbi, my Lord, shows etiquette of turning to Allah after exhausting human means. He does not curse people directly but asks for separation, leaving punishment to Allah. The same etiquette appears in Nūḥ's plea after his people refused: *“My Lord, they disobeyed me”* (71:21). The plea also shows that prophet's concern is not personal revenge but maintaining distinction between obedience and rebellion. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3405) mentions Moses' modesty and patience despite his people's harm, indicating his character. The verse's brevity after long dialogue shows climax: after all arguments, prophet has nothing left but to declare limited control and ask for separation. The response will be divine judgment that fulfils request: separation through wandering, where obedient and disobedient are distinguished by outcome.

The same root appears in *“So set us apart from rebellious people”* (5:25) itself and in *“Allah will separate between them on Day of Judgment”* (22:17).

Fāsiq is used for those who break covenant, as in *“Those who break covenant of Allah after its confirmation”* (2:27) and for those who refuse to enter Holy Land.

Akhi, my brother, refers to Hārūn, who was made prophet to support Moses, as *“And We gave him from Our mercy his brother Aaron as prophet”* (19:53) and *“Make my brother Aaron more eloquent”* (28:34).

---

## Verse 5:26
> Allah replied, “Then this land is forbidden to them for forty years, during which they will wander through the land. So do not grieve for the rebellious people.”

**The Land Withheld and the Years of Wandering**

The verse reports God's answer to Moses after his people refused to enter the land: it is forbidden to them for forty years, and they will wander the earth. The generation that stood at the border and said they would never go in would not see it; the entry was left to their children, and the forty years were the time it took for that change to happen. The number itself appears elsewhere in the Book in a different setting, when Moses was given forty nights on the mountain (7:142 — *“We appointed for Moses thirty nights then added another ten—completing his Lord’s term of forty nights.”*), and the age of forty is named as the point of full strength (46:15 — *“In time, when the child reaches their prime at the age of forty, they pray, “My Lord!”*). Here the forty is measured in years, and the whole generation carried the sentence in their own bodies.

**Wandering With Everything Provided**

What the years were like is stated elsewhere in the Book: the people moved through the desert without a destination and were shaded by cloud, and food was sent down to them morning and evening (2:57 — *“And ˹remember when˺ We shaded you with clouds and sent down to you manna and quails, ˹saying˺, “Eat from the good things We have provided for you.””*). The Book names the same kind of provision elsewhere when it lists what God subjected for the people's use, the sun and the moon among them (14:33 — *“He has ˹also˺ subjected for you the sun and the moon, both constantly orbiting, and has subjected the day and night for you”*). That is the detail the verse's readers are meant to notice. The punishment was not starvation and it was not abandonment; the manna, the quails and the shade continued through it. The word used for the wandering describes a man who cannot find his way rather than one who is travelling, and the point of the sentence is that a people who had been offered a home were left with the road.

**"Do Not Grieve for the Rebellious People"**

The verse closes with a comfort aimed at the prophet who had led them, because the reports show how much the refusal weighed on him. He is told not to grieve over a people who had made rebellion their way, and the Book gives him the same instruction in another sūrah when he is told not to sorrow over those who deny (3:176 — *“˹O Prophet!˺ Do not grieve for those who race to disbelieve—surely they will not harm Allah in the least.”*). The lesson in the words is one the prophets are taught over and over: a messenger is responsible for delivering the truth, not for forcing it into anyone, and the outcome belongs to God. The grieving that would destroy him adds nothing to their decision, and the sūrah moves from his sorrow back to the account of the people he was sent to.

---

## Verse 5:27
> Relate to them in truth ˹O Prophet˺ the story of Adam’s two sons—how each offered a sacrifice: Abel’s offering was accepted while Cain’s was not. So Cain threatened, “I will kill you!” His brother replied, “Allah only accepts ˹the offering˺ of the sincerely devout

**The Story Told Straight**

The sūrah orders the Prophet to tell the story of Adam's two sons in truth — no embroidery, no adjustment for the audience — and the command carries the standard the sūrah has just been asking of the People of the Book when they judge: the account is to be given as it happened. The two sons are named in the older reports as Abel and Cain. The Qur'an itself leaves them unnamed, and the story arrives here right after the passage on the Israelites who refused to enter the land, showing a second kind of rebellion: not turning away from a command, but killing the man who carried it out.

**Two Offerings and One Acceptance**

Each brother brought something near to God — the word for the offering is *qurbān*, from the same root as drawing near — and one offering was accepted while the other was not. The reports describe Abel bringing the best of his flock and Cain bringing poor produce, and they say the sign of acceptance was fire coming down and consuming the accepted gift while the other was left untouched. Ibn ʿAbbās is cited for that sign, and the Book mentions the same sign in its argument with the Israelites, when it says their messengers came with clear proofs and with offerings that fire consumed (3:183 — *“Those ˹are the same people˺ who say, “Allah has commanded us not to believe in any messenger unless he brings us an offering to be consumed by fire ˹from the sky˺.””*). The reason for the difference is stated in the verse itself: God accepts only from those who are mindful of Him. The principle is repeated for the sacrifices of the pilgrimage, where neither the meat nor the blood reaches God and only the mindfulness of the offerer does (22:37 — *“Neither their meat nor blood reaches Allah.”*). The sūrah has already set that standard in the matter of justice, where being just is nearer to mindfulness (5:8 — *“O believers! Stand firm for Allah and bear true testimony. Do not let the hatred of a people lead you to injustice. Be just! That is closer to righteousness. And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah is All-Aware of what you do”*). The same story stands in the fourth chapter of Genesis, with Abel's firstborn flock accepted and Cain's produce not; the Qur'an adds the sentence the older account leaves out, that acceptance belongs to the mindful.

**The Sentence That Is Also a Warning**

When Cain saw that his brother had been accepted and he had not, he did not look at himself. He threatened to kill him. The verse stops at his brother's answer, and the answer names the line that separated the two men: God accepts from the mindful. That is the warning the story is placed here to give. A good deed of mine is not accepted and another man's is; the healthy response is to examine my own offering, and the disease is to turn my failure into a grudge against the man who succeeded. The verses that follow show where that road ends.

**A Note on the Story's Place in the Sūrah**

The story is not a digression. The sūrah is dealing with a community that held a Book, knew what it asked, and had learned to keep the parts that suited it. Cain is the first man in the Book to measure himself against a brother's accepted worship — and the first to answer the comparison with violence. The passage repeats the lesson in a different register: the same envy that made Cain raise his hand against Abel is what the sūrah names in the leaders who would not accept a prophet from outside their own house.

---

## Verse 5:28

> If you raise your hand to kill me, I will not raise mine to kill you, because I fear Allah—the Lord of all worlds

**Fear of Allah Versus Fear of People**

Innā akhāfu Allāha rabb al-'ālamīn contrasts with fear of people that caused Israelites to refuse entry. Israelites feared jabbārīn, powerful people, and refused command; Abel fears Allah, Lord of worlds, and refuses to kill. Fear of Allah leads to restraint from wrongdoing, while fear of people leads to disobedience. The phrase rabb al-'ālamīn appears after fear to show that Lord of all worlds is more worthy of fear than any human. *“So fear Me, not people”* (5:44) commands same priority. The verse therefore teaches hierarchy of fear: fear Allah, not creation. Abel's statement is also example of choosing to be killed rather than killer, which some scholars cite as evidence for preferring to be victim rather than murderer in fitnah, civil strife, as ḥadīth in Sunan Abī Dāwūd (4259) says be like son of Adam, Abel, who said if you stretch hand to kill me, I will not stretch mine. That ḥadīth uses this verse as model for non-participation in civil war.

**Refusal to Kill as Higher Ethics**

The verse does not forbid self-defence in general, but shows Abel's choice to not retaliate even when threatened, out of fear of Allah. Jurists differ: some say self-defence is obligatory, others say forbearance is allowed, and Abel chose forbearance. The Qur'an elsewhere allows self-defence: *“Whoever is killed unjustly, We gave his heir authority, but let him not exceed in killing”* (17:33) and *“If you punish, punish with equivalent”* (16:126). Abel's choice is therefore supererogatory virtue, not obligatory rule for all. The reason he gives — I fear Allah — shows that his restraint is worship, not cowardice. The same fear led two men in verse 23 to advise entering gate despite danger, showing fear of Allah can lead to both courage and restraint depending on context.

**Lord of Worlds as Universal Lord**

Rabb al-'ālamīn, Lord of worlds, appears often in Qur'an, especially in contexts of fear and mercy, as in opening *“All praise is for Allah, Lord of worlds”* (1:2). Using it here indicates that Allah is Lord of both Cain and Abel, so killing brother is violation against Lord who created both. The phrase also universalizes ethic: Lord of worlds, not only Lord of Israelites or Muslims, so murder is crime against universal Lord. The verse therefore links personal ethics to universal theology: because Allah is Lord of all, taking life of one is serious. This prepares for verse 32 which says killing one is like killing all humanity. Abel's statement anticipates that principle: he fears Lord of worlds, so will not kill one person. The next verse will show Abel's desire that Cain bear his sin and be among Fire, indicating that murderer bears burden.

**A Model for Forbearance in Conflict**

This verse became model for forbearance in Islamic ethics, especially during civil strife. The Prophet ﷺ said in ḥadīth about fitnah, be like son of Adam, as recorded in Sunan al-Tirmidhī (2204, graded ḥasan). The verse is cited to encourage not participating in killing among Muslims. At same time, jurists note that this was specific law for sons of Adam, not necessarily abrogating self-defence, but showing virtue of restraint. The linguistic contrast basaṭta and bi-bāsiṭin shows that Abel will not even start extension, not only not kill, indicating complete non-aggression. The verse ends with rabb al-'ālamīn, reminding that fear of universal Lord should prevent murder. The following verse will show Abel's warning that murderer will bear sin and be among Fire, adding deterrent.

---

## Verse 5:29

> I want to let you bear your sin against me along with your other sins, then you will be one of those destined to the Fire. And that is the reward of the wrongdoers.”

**Bearing Sin as Consequence of Murder**

Innī urīdu a tabū'a bi-ithmī wa ithmika fa-takūna min aṣḥāb al-nār explains Abel's warning. Urīdu, I want or intend, a tabū'a, that you bear or return with, from b-w-', to return with burden, bi-ithmī wa ithmika, my sin and your sin, meaning sin of killing me plus your previous sins. The phrase tabū'a bi-ithmī means you will bear sin of killing me, not that my sins transfer to you unjustly, but that you will bear burden of murder plus your own. Aṣḥāb al-nār, companions of Fire, as in verse 10, indicates permanence. The same phrasing appears in *“That is reward of wrongdoers”* (5:29) itself, closing with general rule.

**Sin Against Me and Other Sins Together**

Bi-ithmī wa ithmika combines two categories: sin against me, murder, and other sins, like envy, arrogance, and rejected offering. The combination shows that murder does not occur alone but accumulates previous sins. The Qur'an says *“Whoever kills believer intentionally, his reward is Hell, abiding in it”* (4:93) for intentional murder, showing severity. Abel says I want you to bear sin, not that he desires Cain's damnation, but that he wants to make clear that if Cain kills, he will bear burden. Urīdu here can mean I want to warn that you will bear, not that I wish evil for you, as some exegetes note that prophets do not wish damnation but warn of it. The phrase also indicates that victim's sin is not transferred unjustly; rather, murderer bears sin of murder plus his own, while victim is forgiven, as ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (2581) says oppressed's sins may be placed on oppressor on Day of Judgment if oppressor has no good deeds left.

**Companions of Fire and Reward of Wrongdoers**

Fa-takūna min aṣḥāb al-nār wa dhālika jazā' al-ẓālimīn — you will be among companions of Fire, that is reward of wrongdoers — generalizes. Ẓālimīn, wrongdoers, from ẓ-l-m, to place thing not in its place, to oppress, includes murderer who places killing where it does not belong. The reward jazā' is recompense that fits deed. The same phrase appears in *“That is reward of wrongdoers”* (5:29) and in *“That is reward of wrongdoers”* (59:17) for other wrongdoers. The verse therefore establishes that murder's reward is Fire, and that this is consistent rule for ẓālimīn. The warning is both specific to Cain and general for all who kill. It prepares for verse 32 which will say killing one is like killing all, emphasizing gravity. The verse ends Abel's speech, which began with non-retaliation and ends with warning, showing balance of forbearance and truth-telling.

**Why Abel Mentions Fire**

Abel mentions Fire not to threaten but to remind that murder has Last-Day consequence beyond worldly loss. The mention of aṣḥāb al-nār connects to verse 10's aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm, companions of Hellfire, for disbelievers, showing that murder is among deeds that lead to companionship with Fire. The reminder is also to deter Cain: if you kill, you will be among Fire, so refrain. The same deterrence appears in *“Whoever kills soul, it is as if he killed all humanity”* (5:32) next section. The verse therefore links personal ethics to ultimate accountability: fear of Allah, Lord of worlds, from previous verse, leads to awareness that wrongdoing leads to Fire. The warning is part of naba' bi-l-ḥaqq, story in truth, showing that early humanity already knew about Fire as consequence.

The verse's mention of Fire as reward for ẓālimīn is used by jurists to show that murder is among kabā'ir, major sins, as ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6857) lists killing soul as major sin.

---

## Verse 5:30
> Yet Cain convinced himself to kill his brother, so he killed him—becoming a loser

**The Step From Grudge to Killing**

The verse follows the warning Cain had just been given, and it does not describe a sudden rage. It describes a decision made quietly inside a man. His own self got used to the idea — the Arabic verb pictures the soul becoming pliant, the resistance wearing down — and once the killing had been made to look reasonable, the hand followed. The Book describes the same self in its own words: the soul commands to evil (12:53 — *“And I do not seek to free myself from blame, for indeed the soul is ever inclined to evil, except those shown mercy by my Lord.”*), and the man who takes his own desire as a god has nobody left who forbids him anything (45:23 — *“Have you seen ˹O Prophet˺ those who have taken their own desires as their god? ˹And so˺ Allah left them to stray knowingly, sealed their hearing and hearts, and placed a cover on their sight. Who then can guide them after Allah? Will you ˹all˺ not then be mindful”*).

**The Comfort the Sūrah Refuses to Give**

The sentence is short: he persuaded himself, he killed, and he became one of the losers. The sūrah does not dwell on the blow or on the body; it goes straight to the balance sheet. The word for loss is the same one the sūrah has just used of the people who refused to enter the holy land (5:21 — *“O my people! Enter the Holy Land which Allah has destined for you ˹to enter˺. And do not turn back or else you will become losers.””*), which puts Cain's failure in the same column as the failure of the tribes at the border. The Book looks at a murder from the end of the killer's own life: what did he have when the matter was finished? He had lost his brother, his family and his peace, and he had gained a deed that could not be undone.

**A Weight That Keeps Growing**

The Prophet explained why the loss does not stop with Cain. No soul is killed unjustly but that the first son of Adam carries a share of the guilt, because he was the first to open the practice of killing (al-Bukhārī 3335; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1677). A crime can outlive the man who committed it when it leaves a road for others to walk. That is the weight of the verse's last word: he was a loser not only in what he did but in what he started, and the count goes on as long as anyone follows him down the same path.

**What Kept Abel Standing**

The difference between the two brothers was named in the previous verse: God accepts from those who are mindful of Him. Mindfulness is the restraint that sits on top of the self, and where it is missing the self talks a man round one step at a time. The Book gives the same lesson about the harder kind of honesty, telling the believer to stand firm for justice even against his own self and his own parents (4:135 — *“O believers! Stand firm for justice as witnesses for Allah even if it is against yourselves, your parents, or close relatives.”*), because the self is never neutral in a quarrel. Cain's problem was not a lack of argument; it was the lack of the fear of God that would have kept the argument from reaching its conclusion.

---

## Verse 5:31

> Then Allah sent a crow digging ˹a grave˺ in the ground ˹for a dead crow˺, in order to show him how to bury the corpse of his brother. He cried, “Alas! Have I ˹even˺ failed to be like this crow and bury the corpse of my brother?” So he became regretful

**Crow Sent to Teach Burial**

Fa-ba'atha Allāhu ghurāban yabḥathu fī al-arḍ li-yuriyahu kayfa yuwārī saw'ata akhīh reports crow teaching burial. Fa-ba'atha, then Allah sent, ghurāban, crow, yabḥathu fī al-arḍ, digging in ground, from b-ḥ-th, to search, dig, li-yuriyahu, to show him, kayfa yuwārī saw'ata akhīhi, how to bury corpse of brother. Saw'ah, from s-w-', means bad or shameful thing, corpse, because dead body left exposed is shameful. The crow is small bird, yet taught first murderer how to cover shame. The story, as detailed in tafsīr, says Cain left brother's corpse unburied, not knowing what to do, then Allah sent two crows, one killed other, then killer crow dug ground and buried dead crow, showing Cain.

**How to Cover Corpse as First Lesson**

Li-yuriyahu kayfa yuwārī saw'ata akhīhi — to show how to cover corpse — indicates burial is first lesson after murder. Yuwārī, to cover, hide, from w-r-y, same root as wārā, behind, and saw'ah, shameful thing, shows that covering dead is to protect dignity and prevent shame of exposure. Burial is so taught by Allah via crow, establishing that humans learn even from animals. The Qur'an says *“Do you not see that Allah taught man what he did not know”* (96:5) and here teaches via bird. The lesson is that murderer who took life must now cover death, facing consequence. The crow digging is practical demonstration: dig, place, cover. This became sunnah for humans: burial in ground. The verse therefore establishes burial as divinely taught practice, not merely cultural. The same teaching appears in *“Then Allah sent crow digging”* (5:31) itself, showing that even in story of first sin, guidance continues.

**Regret Without Repentance**

Fa-aṣbaḥa min al-nādimīn — so he became regretful — closes with becoming regretful, similar to previous verse's fa-aṣbaḥa min al-khāsirīn, became loser. The two aṣbaḥa show two mornings: first morning after murder, loser; second morning after crow, regretful. Nādimīn, regretful, indicates remorse, but Qur'an does not say he repented, so regret alone did not remove sin. The same root appears in *“They became regretful”* (5:31) and in *“They will become regretful”* (23:40) for others who regretted after punishment. The story teaches that regret after sin is natural, but must lead to tawbah to be beneficial. The crow teaching burial is mercy that gives opportunity to reflect, but Cain's regret is over being unable to be like crow, not over killing. The verse therefore shows that even murderer is given sign, but response determines outcome.

**Extra Verified Detail on Crows**

Crows' behaviour of digging and burying is verified by ornithology: crows cache food by digging and covering, and they also cover dead crows in some observations. The Qur'anic description yabḥathu fī al-arḍ, digging in ground, matches caching behaviour. Early tafsīr says Allah sent two crows, one killed other, then dug, which matches natural crow aggression. The phrase yā waylatā is expression of woe used when person realizes incapability, as in *“O woe to us, we were wrongdoers”* (21:14). The verse's lesson about burial became basis for Islamic burial rites, which are simple: dig, place on right side facing qiblah, cover. The next verse will extend lesson from one murder to all humanity, showing that taking one life is like killing all.

---

## Verse 5:32

> That is why We ordained for the Children of Israel that whoever takes a life—unless as a punishment for murder or mischief in the land—it will be as if they killed all of humanity; and whoever saves a life, it will be as if they saved all of humanity. ˹Although˺ Our messengers already came to them with clear proofs, many of them still transgressed afterwards through the land

**Ordained for Children of Israel After First Murder**

Min ajli dhālika katabnā 'alā banī Isrā'īl annahu man qatala nafsan bi-ghayri nafsin aw fasādin fī al-arḍ fa-ka-annamā qatala al-nāsa jamī'an explains legislation after Cain story. Min ajli dhālika, for that reason, because of that, refers to murder of one brother by another, first murder, so We ordained for Children of Israel. Katabnā, We wrote, ordained, 'alā banī Isrā'īl, upon Children of Israel, indicates this law was written for them, though its moral applies to all. The same principle appears in *“Whoever kills a soul, it is as if he killed all humanity”* (5:32) itself, often quoted as universal ethic.

**Killing One Like Killing All Humanity**

Fa-ka-annamā qatala al-nāsa jamī'an is powerful analogy. Nās, people, humanity, jamī'an, all together, indicates that humanity is like one body; killing one is like killing all because it attacks right to life that all share. Al-Rāzī explains that each soul contains potential for all humanity, and killing one destroys that potential and violates divine trust that protects all. The exception bi-ghayri nafsin aw fasādin fī al-arḍ shows that killing is permitted only as just retribution for murder or as penalty for spreading mischief, which includes waging war against Allah and Messenger, as verse 33 will detail. The exception ensures that justice is not abolished, but that unjust killing is equated to killing all. The same logic underlies qiṣāṣ, retaliation: *“A life for a life”* (5:45) allows killing as punishment for murder, preserving life of community by deterring murder. The verse therefore balances sanctity and justice: life is sacred, but taking life unjustly forfeits protection.

**Messengers Came With Clear Proofs Yet Many Transgressed**

The principle man qatala nafsan fa-ka-annamā qatala al-nāsa jamī'an is recorded as being in Torah as well, though not in exact wording. Talmud Sanhedrin 4:5 — *“Do not entrust the incapable ˹among your dependants˺ with your wealth which Allah has made a means of support for you—but feed and clothe them from it, and speak to them kindly”* says whoever destroys one soul, it is as if he destroyed entire world, showing similar ethic in Jewish tradition, which supports that it was indeed ordained for Children of Israel.

**What the Verse Establishes**

The verse states the law given to the children of Israel after the first killing: whoever kills a person, unless it be for a life or for spreading corruption in the land, it is as if he killed all of humanity, and whoever saves one life, it is as if he saved all of them. The rulings of the community on the value of a life were built on this text, and the jurists placed it at the head of the chapters on murder, on the basis of retaliation, and on the equality of one life with another regardless of the standing of the victim and the killer.
**What the Verse Does Not Repeal**

The verse's exception is stated inside the sentence, and the jurists read it as the basis of the law itself: a life may be taken for a life, and for the corruption a person spreads in the land. The Sūrah of the Table returns to the same subject later with the penalties for the men who wage war against God and His Messenger (5:33 — *“Indeed, the penalty for those who wage war against Allah and His Messenger and spread mischief in the land is death, crucifixion, cutting off their hands and feet on opposite sides, or exile from the land.”*), and the Sūrah of the Night Journey states the rule with its own exception about retaliation and the limit that a person may not be killed unjustly (17:33 — *“If anyone is killed unjustly, We have given their heirs the authority, but do not let them exceed limits in retaliation, for they are already supported ˹by law˺”*). The Book therefore does not set the value of life against the law of justice; it makes the protection of one life the reason for the law.
**The Report Behind the Verse**

The verse is read alongside the report of the two sons of Adam, which the verses before it narrate, where one killed the other and then did not know what to do with the body. The Prophet's word about the first killing and the community's use of the verse in its preaching are recorded in the collections, and the sentence about saving a life became one of the most quoted in the Book. The reading of the verse's second half among the early Muslims included the sense of giving life by forbidding the taking of it, and the jurists included the forgiveness of a debt and the rescue from hardship under the word.

---

## Verse 5:33
> Indeed, the penalty for those who wage war against Allah and His Messenger and spread mischief in the land is death, crucifixion, cutting off their hands and feet on opposite sides, or exile from the land. This ˹penalty˺ is a disgrace for them in this world, and they will suffer a tremendous punishment in the Hereafter

**The Crime the Verse Is Answering**

The verse defines a crime and its penalties. The men it describes are not ordinary thieves; they set themselves against God and His Messenger and work to spread corruption in the land. The Book's word for their offence is waging war, and the reports connect the verse with a band that attacked a shepherd, killed him and drove off the camels, after which the Prophet had them punished (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 233; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1671; and the same account is cited in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 6802). The jurists built the law of highway robbery on this verse, and they set its conditions from the same reports: weapons, a public road, the intent to terrorise, and the taking of property or of life. What is being protected is the basic safety of anybody travelling or living in the land, and the sūrah has just said that a single life weighs as much as the whole of humanity (5:32 — *“That is why We ordained for the Children of Israel that whoever takes a life—unless as a punishment for murder or mischief in the land—it will be as if they killed all of humanity; and whoever saves a life, it will be as if they saved all of humanity.”*).

**The Four Penalties, and the Limit Inside Them**

The verse names four answers — death, crucifixion, cutting the hand and the foot on opposite sides, or removal from the land — and it leaves the choice to the court according to what the criminal actually did. The cutting on opposite sides means right hand and left foot, which disables a man from repeating the attack without destroying him; even at its most severe, the law keeps a limit. The exile was read in two ways, as banishment from the region or as imprisonment, since al-Ṭabarī noted that a prison keeps a man away from the benefits of the land just as exile does. The purpose named in the verse itself is public: this is a disgrace for them in the world, so that the community sees that the roads and homes are guarded, and the same pairing of worldly shame with a greater punishment is used elsewhere in the sūrah for other crimes against God and His Messenger (5:41 — *“For them is disgrace in this world, and they will suffer a tremendous punishment in the Hereafter”*).

**Why the Sentence Is Not the End of the Matter**

The verse ends with the punishment of the Hereafter, which tells the reader that the court has not finished accounting for the crime simply because a sentence was carried out. What the next verse does is the other half of the same law: repentance before the arrest removes the fixed penalty, and the door of return is left open to the man who gives himself up while the matter is still in his hands (5:34 — *“As for those who repent before you seize them, then know that Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful”*). Severity and mercy are not two competing systems in the Book; they are two parts of one law that aims at a safe street and a reformed man.

---

## Verse 5:34
> As for those who repent before you seize them, then know that Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**Repentance Before the Arrest**

The verse is the exception attached to the punishment of those who wage war on the community and spread corruption (5:33 — *“Indeed, the penalty for those who wage war against Allah and His Messenger and spread mischief in the land is death, crucifixion, cutting off their hands and feet on opposite sides, or exile from the land.”*). If they turn back before they are seized, the fixed penalty is not applied to them. The condition is precise: the return must come while the matter is still in their hands, which the jurists read as an encouragement to surrender, to stop the harm, and to come in before the pursuit closes. A man who repents only when the soldiers are at his door has not chosen the peace; he has run out of room.

**What the Turning Back Means**

Al-Ṭabarī explained the repentance here as leaving the fighting, returning to obedience, and putting right what can be put right. The verse does not say that the rights of the victims disappear: the fixed penalty of the Book is lifted for the one who comes in first, but the property he took and the blood he shed remain claims that the family or the injured party may pursue. What the exception protects is the community's door of return. A man facing certain punishment has no reason to stop, and a law that leaves him no way back keeps the streets dangerous. The verse gives him one.

**Mercy Named at the End of the Severity**

The verse closes with the two Names: All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. The same pair follows other commands where the Book allows a way out of hardship, such as the permission to eat what is otherwise forbidden when a person is forced to it (2:173 — *“Surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful”*), and the sūrah as a whole keeps the two sides in view, describing God as severe in punishment and forgiving and merciful in the same breath (5:98 — *“Know that Allah is severe in punishment and that He is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful”*).

Read with the verses before it, the passage lays out a whole scale: a life weighs as much as humanity (5:32 — *“That is why We ordained for the Children of Israel that whoever takes a life—unless as a punishment for murder or mischief in the land—it will be as if they killed all of humanity; and whoever saves a life, it will be as if they saved all of humanity.”*), those who attack the community's peace are answered with force (5:33 — *“Indeed, the penalty for those who wage war against Allah and His Messenger and spread mischief in the land is death, crucifixion, cutting off their hands and feet on opposite sides, or exile from the land. This ˹penalty˺ is a disgrace for them in this world, and they will suffer a tremendous punishment in the Hereafter”*), and those who come back before the arrest are spared (5:34 — *“As for those who repent before you seize them, then know that Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful”*).

The sūrah then turns from the courtroom to the heart, telling the believers to be mindful of God, to seek the way to Him and to strive in His path (5:35 — *“O believers! Be mindful of Allah and seek what brings you closer to Him and struggle in His Way, so you may be successful”*), which is the same law applied to the man who is not a criminal but wants to grow.

---
## Verse 5:35

> O believers! Be mindful of Allah and seek what brings you closer to Him and struggle in His Way, so you may be successful

**Wasīlah as Deeds That Bring Nearness**

Al-wasīlah is interpreted as good deeds, obedience, and seeking nearness through what Allah loves. Ibn 'Abbās said wasīlah means need or means to approach, and Qatādah said seek nearness through obedience. The same term appears in *“Those whom they call seek wasīlah to their Lord”* (17:57) for righteous seeking nearness, and in ḥadīth where Prophet ﷺ said ask Allah for wasīlah, a station in Paradise, as in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim (384). In this verse, wasīlah is general: any act that brings closer, such as prayer, charity, fulfilling contracts, being just, as earlier verses commanded. The command ibtaghū ilayhi al-wasīlah therefore summarizes previous commands: honour obligations, respect rituals, eat lawful, be just, all are wasā'il, means to Allah. The verse does not limit wasīlah to specific act, so it remains open to all good deeds. The seeking is active: ibtaghū, seek, indicates effort, not passive wish.

**Striving in His Way After Seeking Nearness**

Wa jāhidū fī sabīlihi follows seeking wasīlah, indicating that striving is among greatest wasā'il. Jihād in Qur'an includes striving against soul, against Shayṭān, and against enemies who wage war and spread mischief, as verses 33-34 discussed. Fī sabīlihi, in His way, restricts striving to path that Allah approves, not for personal gain or aggression. The same phrase appears in *“Strive in Allah's way as you should strive”* (22:78) and *“Those who strive in Our way, We will guide them to Our paths”* (29:69). The placement after wasīlah shows that jihād is not only fighting but seeking nearness: fighting against those who threaten public security, as in ḥirābah, is wasīlah to Allah because it protects lives that verse 32 said are like all humanity. The command is plural, addressing community, indicating collective striving is needed, not only individual.

**Success as Outcome of Three**

La'allakum tufliḥūn — so you may be successful — gives purpose. Tufliḥūn, from falāḥ, means to succeed, prosper, attain desired and be saved from feared, used for both worldly and otherworldly success. The same closing appears in *“So you may be successful”* (5:35) and in many verses after commands, as in *“Be mindful of Allah so you may be successful”* (2:189). The three commands — taqwā, wasīlah, jihād — lead to falāḥ because they cover inward shield, outward seeking, and active striving. Al-Rāzī notes that taqwā prevents evil, wasīlah brings good, jihād removes obstacles to good, so together they ensure success. The verse is concise but comprehensive, summarizing ethic of sūrah: be mindful, seek nearness through good deeds, strive to protect and promote good. The next verses will describe disbelievers who cannot ransom themselves, contrasting success of believers with loss of disbelievers.

**Wasīlah as Every Deed That Connects**

Al-wasīlah, means to approach, is any deed that connects to Allah: prayer, charity, justice, fulfilling contracts, all are wasā'il. The command ibtaghū ilayhi al-wasīlah, seek means to Him, is open, not limited, so believers can seek nearness through many paths. The same root appears in *“Seek wasīlah to your Lord”* (17:57) for righteous. The highest wasīlah is station in Paradise that Prophet ﷺ asked to be granted, as in Muslim (384). In this world, wasīlah includes striving in His way, jihād, which protects lives that verse 32 said are like all humanity. The verse therefore summarizes program: mindfulness restrains, wasīlah brings near, jihād protects, leading to falāḥ, success, which is called in adhān.

A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (11) says Muslim is one from whose tongue and hand Muslims are safe, linking taqwā and jihād to safety.

---

## Verse 5:36

> As for the disbelievers, even if they were to possess everything in the world twice over ˹and offer it all˺ to ransom themselves from the punishment of the Day of Judgment, it would never be accepted from them. And they will suffer a painful punishment

**Possessing Everything Twice Over Cannot Ransom**

Inna alladhīna kafarū law anna lahum mā fī al-arḍ jamī'an wa mithlahu ma'ahu li-yaftadū bihi min 'adhābi yawm al-qiyāmah mā tuqubbila minhum describes disbelievers' inability to ransom. The same hypothetical appears in *“If those who wronged had everything in earth and like it with it, they would ransom with it”* (13:18) and *“If disbelievers had everything in earth and like it to ransom”* (5:36) itself. The point is that on Day of Judgment, wealth has no value as ransom; only faith and good deeds matter, as verse 35 said seek wasīlah, means to Allah, which disbelievers did not.

**Why Ransom Is Not Accepted**

The reason ransom is not accepted is that Day of Judgment is day of recompense, not bargaining. In world, wealth can ransom from punishment, as in freeing captive, but in Hereafter, only deeds count. *“No soul will be able to avail another, nor ransom be accepted from it”* (2:48) states same. The disbelievers' wealth, even if entire earth doubled, cannot replace faith that was required. The verse therefore contrasts wasīlah, means to Allah through good deeds, which leads to success, with fidyah, ransom through wealth, which fails. The disbelievers sought wasīlah through wealth and status in world, but true wasīlah is taqwā and good deeds. The hypothetical also shows divine justice: they had chance to seek wasīlah in world, but refused, so on Day when they would give everything to escape, it is too late. The same idea appears in *“On Day, wrongdoer will bite hands, saying would I had taken way with Messenger”* (25:27), showing regret.

**Painful Punishment as Outcome**

Wa lahum 'adhābun alīm — for them painful punishment — closes. 'Adhāb, punishment, from '-dh-b, to prevent, punish, and alīm, painful, from a-l-m, to pain, indicates punishment that causes severe pain. The same closing appears in many verses for disbelievers, as in *“For them painful punishment”* (5:36) itself. The pain is both physical and spiritual: physical torment of Fire and spiritual pain of regret that ransom not accepted. The verse follows verse 35's promise of success for believers who are mindful, seek wasīlah, and strive, contrasting success with painful punishment. The contrast is deliberate: believers succeed through taqwā and wasīlah, disbelievers fail despite possessing everything. The verse therefore teaches that true wealth is not material but spiritual. A ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6538) says on Day of Judgment, Allah will say to person with least punishment, if you had everything in earth would you ransom, he will say yes, then Allah says I asked less than that, to not associate, but you refused, illustrating verse.

**Wealth in World Versus Deeds in Hereafter**

The verse's hypothetical law anna lahum mā fī al-arḍ jamī'an shows that even total ownership of earth is insufficient. In world, owning earth would make one most powerful, but in Hereafter, it has no value without faith. The Qur'an says *“Wealth and children are adornment of worldly life, but lasting good deeds are better”* (18:46). The disbelievers had wealth and used it to wage war and spread mischief, as previous verses described, but did not use it as wasīlah to Allah. On Day of Judgment, they would wish to use it as ransom, but it is not accepted because time for wasīlah is past. The verse therefore warns against relying on wealth for salvation. The same warning appears in *“O you who believe, spend from what We provided before Day comes when no trade nor ransom”* (2:254). The next verse will describe their desperate desire to exit Fire but inability, continuing picture of painful punishment.

**The Least Punished Man in the Fire**

The Prophet ﷺ gave the refusal this verse describes a scene. The person who will receive the lightest punishment in the Fire is brought forward and asked whether he would ransom himself with everything on the earth, if he owned it. He says yes. Then he is told: I asked you for something far less than this while you were in the world — that you not associate anything with Me — but you refused (al-Bukhārī 6538; Muslim 2805). Two things follow. The ransom offered on that Day comes too late, and what was asked of him beforehand was a fraction of what he now offers.

---

## Verse 5:37

> They will be desperate to get out of the Fire but they will never be able to. And they will suffer an everlasting punishment

**Desperate Desire to Exit Fire**

Yurīdūna a yakhrujū min al-nār wa mā hum bi-khārijīna minhā reports desire to exit. The same phrasing appears in *“They will want to exit Fire but they are not exiting”* (5:37) and in *“Whenever they want to exit from it, they are returned to it”* (32:20). The desire is natural: Fire is painful, so they want out, but ability is lacking because judgment is final. The verb yurīdūna, they want, shows continuous desire, not one-time wish. The negation mā hum bi-khārijīna, they are not exiters, with nominal sentence, indicates permanence: they are not of those who exit. The verse therefore describes psychological torment: wanting exit but never achieving, which is itself punishment. The same desire appears for people of Hell asking Malik, keeper, to let Lord finish them, as in *“They will call, O Malik, let your Lord finish us”* (43:77), but answer is you will remain.

**Everlasting Punishment as Consequence**

Wa lahum 'adhābun muqīm — for them everlasting punishment — seals. 'Adhāb, punishment, muqīm, lasting, resident, from q-w-m, to stand, remain, indicates punishment that stays, does not end. Muqīm appears also in *“For them everlasting punishment”* (5:37) and *“For them punishment that remains”* (9:21) for other contexts, but here for Fire. The same root appears in *“Abiding in it”* (2:39) for permanence. The everlasting nature is because crime is everlasting in practice: disbelief that rejects guidance that would have led to eternal success leads to eternal loss. The verse's brevity after hypothetical of owning everything doubled shows that all wealth cannot prevent everlasting punishment, so true success is through faith and good deeds, as verse 35 said. The next verse will shift to theft penalty, showing that worldly punishments are temporary but aim to prevent everlasting punishment by deterring crime.

**A Verse That Closes Unit on Disbelievers' Loss**

Verses 36-37 together describe disbelievers' loss: cannot ransom even with everything doubled, want to exit Fire but cannot, suffer everlasting punishment. The unit contrasts with verse 35's success for believers, showing two outcomes. The language yurīdūna a yakhrujū, they want to exit, is present tense, indicating ongoing desire in Fire, not past, making scene vivid. A verified ḥadīth in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (6539) describes people of Fire calling for water and relief but being given boiling water, showing that desire for relief is constant but not met. The verse's closing 'adhābun muqīm, everlasting punishment, appears also in *“For them everlasting punishment”* (5:37) and is among descriptions of Fire that emphasize permanence for persistent disbelievers. The following verses will discuss theft and its worldly penalty, which is severe but not everlasting, aiming to prevent everlasting punishment by deterring crime that harms society.

The Qur'an says *“If they returned to world, they would return to what was forbidden”* (6:28), indicating that even if they exited, they would repeat disbelief, so remaining is just.

---

## Verse 5:38
> As for male and female thieves, cut off their hands for what they have done—a deterrent from Allah. And Allah is Almighty, All-Wise

**The Same Penalty for Man and Woman**

The verse names the male thief and the female thief together and gives them the same penalty, so that no one can read a difference of gender into the law. The command is addressed to the community and its authorities, not to the injured man: the cutting is a public act of a court, and the words for the hands are read by the jurists as the right hand of each offender. Theft, in the Book's scale, is not a small matter of property: the sūrah has just said that a single life weighs like the whole of humanity (5:32 — *“That is why We ordained for the Children of Israel that whoever takes a life—unless as a punishment for murder or mischief in the land—it will be as if they killed all of humanity; and whoever saves a life, it will be as if they saved all of humanity.”*), and it has described the men who spread corruption in the land as those who deserve the harshest treatment (5:33 — *“Indeed, the penalty for those who wage war against Allah and His Messenger and spread mischief in the land is death, crucifixion, cutting off their hands and feet on opposite sides, or exile from the land.”*). A law that lets property be stripped away with no answer is the first step into that corruption, and the penalty is one of the fixed limits the Book sets, not a sentence a judge invents.

**A Recompense for What They Earned**

The verse gives the reason in a phrase: a recompense for what they earned. The penalty follows the deed the way a wage follows work; the thief took by stealth what was not his, so he pays from his own body, and the Book uses the same word of earning when it says that every soul will be paid for what it earned (2:281 — *“Be mindful of the Day when you will ˹all˺ be returned to Allah, then every soul will be paid in full for what it has done, and none will be wronged”*). The law does not apply to every dishonest act, and the jurists built their conditions out of the practice of the Prophet and his companions: the goods must have been taken secretly from a place where property is kept safe, and they must reach a minimum value. A report in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī fixes that minimum at a quarter of a dinar or more, and another report in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim gives the Prophet's own sentence for a quarter of a dinar. So the cutting is not the punishment of a hungry man who took a handful of food; it is the penalty for a real breach of the community's security.

**The Noble and the Poor Stand in One Line**

The verse's severity is checked by its last two Names: God is Almighty, All-Wise. The first says that He has the power to lay the law down and see it carried out; the second says that the penalty is measured, not the anger of a ruler. The pair closes the sūrah's own section on penalties, and it returns at the sūrah's end (5:118 — *“If You punish them, they belong to You after all. But if You forgive them, You are surely the Almighty, All-Wise.””*). The tradition shows what the wisdom means in practice. When a woman of a noble clan of Makkah stole, her people looked for an intercessor, and the Prophet's answer was that if his own daughter Fāṭimah had stolen, her hand would have been cut (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 6788). The law does not look at the family of the offender. That, and not the severity alone, is what makes the verse a deterrent: everyone in the market knows that the same line runs under all of them.

---

## Verse 5:39
> But whoever repents after their wrongdoing and mends their ways, Allah will surely turn to them in forgiveness. Indeed, Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**Repentance That Repairs**

The verse follows the penalty for theft with the door out: whoever repents after his wrongdoing and mends what he broke. The word for mending carries the weight of the sentence. The jurists read the pair as a whole: leaving the sin, regretting it, resolving not to return to it, and putting right what can be put right — for the thief, returning the stolen property or its value, and for other wrongs, whatever undoes the harm. Repentance that keeps the goods is not the mending the verse asks for. The same combination of repenting and mending appears elsewhere in the Book, in the promise to those who concealed the truth and then turned back (2:160 — *“As for those who repent, mend their ways, and let the truth be known, they are the ones to whom I will turn ˹in forgiveness˺, for I am the Accepter of Repentance, Most Merciful”*), and among the qualities of the men who return to God (25:71 — *“And whoever repents and does good has truly turned to Allah properly”*).

**What God Promises the One Who Returns**

God will surely turn to him, the verse says, and the promise is emphatic. The Book gives the same assurance when it speaks of those who repent and believe and do right, that God turns to them (2:222 — *“Surely Allah loves those who always turn to Him in repentance and those who purify themselves.””*). The turning reaches both worlds. A man who repents before the case is brought to the authorities may find the fixed penalty lifted from him, and the forgiveness of the Hereafter is promised to the man whose repentance is real, whatever a court has already carried out. The verse does not say that regret erases a judgement already given; it says the door of God's acceptance stays open to the one who walks back through it.

**Why Mending Is Named With Repentance**

Theft takes something from another person. A private sorrow cannot repair a public wrong, so the verse ties forgiveness to mending, and the jurists applied the same rule to other offences: the slanderer must clear the name he damaged, and the man who spread corruption must stop and put things right. The Book states the general principle when it says that good deeds remove evil deeds (11:114 — *“Establish prayer ˹O Prophet˺ at both ends of the day and in the early part of the night. Surely good deeds wipe out evil deeds. That is a reminder for the mindful”*). One report in the Sunan of Abū Dāwūd deals with the thief who returns the property before the matter is reported to the authorities, which the jurists read as showing that mending can affect what the penalty does — another reason the verse turns to mending right after the sentence of cutting.

**The Two Names After the Sentence**

The verse ends with the two Names: All-Forgiving, Most Merciful — the same pair that closes the verse on the highway robbers (5:34 — *“As for those who repent before you seize them, then know that Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful”*), and the same pair the Book attaches to its verses on returning. Al-Rāzī noted what the two names do here: the first covers the sin so that it does not lead to punishment in the Hereafter, and the second grants mercy and good after the covering. The law's purpose, on this reading, is not to brand a man for life but to reform him: the penalty deters, and repentance with repair restores what the penalty cannot. The placing of mercy after severity is the sūrah's own arrangement, and it is what keeps the law from turning punishment into despair.

---

## Verse 5:40

> Do you not know that the kingdom of the heavens and the earth belongs to Allah ˹alone˺? He punishes whoever He wills and forgives whoever He wills. And Allah is Most Capable of everything

**Kingdom of Heavens and Earth Belongs to Allah**

A-lam ta'lam anna Allāha lahu mulk al-samāwāt wa al-arḍ asks rhetorical question. A-lam ta'lam, do you not know, ta'lam from '-l-m, to know, indicates that knowledge is expected, and question is to affirm.
