Anna Allāha lahu mulk al-samāwāt wa al-arḍ, that to Allah belongs kingdom of heavens and earth, mulk as before, kingdom, dominion, ownership. The same phrase appears in *“To Allah belongs kingdom of heavens and earth”* (5:17, 5:18 — *“To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and everything in between.”*, 5:40) repeated, showing that sovereignty is basis for legislation about theft, ḥirābah, and forgiveness. Lahu, to Him, fronted for emphasis: to Allah alone belongs kingdom, not to thieves or tyrants. The question a-lam ta'lam appears also in *“Do you not know that kingdom belongs to Allah”* (5:40) itself, prompting reflection. The knowledge that kingdom belongs to Allah implies that He can legislate as He wills, as verse 1 said *“Allah commands what He wills”* (5:1), and that He can punish and forgive as He wills, as next clause says.

**He Punishes Whom He Wills and Forgives Whom He Wills**

Yu'adhdhibu man yashā' wa yaghfiru li-man yashā' — He punishes whom He wills and forgives whom He wills — states discretion. The same pairing appears in *“He punishes whom He wills and forgives whom He wills”* (5:40) and in *“He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills”* (5:18) with order reversed, showing both are possible. The order here — punish then forgive — matches context of theft penalty followed by forgiveness for repentant, showing that punishment is mentioned first because verse 38 prescribed cutting, then forgiveness for repentant in verse 39, now general principle that both are by His will.

**Why Question Form Is Used**

A-lam ta'lam, do you not know, is rhetorical question that expects yes, you know, and also rebukes those who act as if they do not know. Those who steal act as if kingdom belongs to them to take, and those who despair of forgiveness act as if Allah cannot forgive, but question reminds that kingdom belongs to Allah, so He can punish theft and forgive repentance. The same question appears in *“Do you not know that Allah has power over everything”* (2:106) and *“Do you not know that Allah knows what is in heavens and earth”* (22:70), always to affirm knowledge that should affect behaviour. Here it affirms that Allah's kingdom includes heavens and earth, so His law covers all, and His will in punishing and forgiving is part of kingdom. The question also invites reflection that leads to taqwā, mindfulness, as earlier verses commanded.

The pairing punish whom He wills and forgive whom He wills is verified as reflecting divine justice and mercy: will is based on wisdom, not arbitrary, as *“He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills, and Allah is Capable”* (48:14) and other verses show. The order punish then forgive here matches context of theft where punishment is prescribed then forgiveness offered, while order forgive then punish in verse 18 matches context of claim of being beloved where forgiveness is hoped then punishment warned. The variation shows rhetorical fit. The verse's closing qadīr, capable, appears also at end of sūrah: *“Allah is Capable of everything”* (5:120), framing sūrah with capability.

---

## Verse 5:41

> O Messenger! Do not grieve for those who race to disbelieve—those who say, “We believe” with their tongues, but their hearts are in disbelief. Nor those among the Jews who eagerly listen to lies, attentive to those who are too arrogant to come to you. They distort the Scripture, taking rulings out of context, then say, “If this is the ruling you get ˹from Muḥammad˺, accept it. If not, beware!” Whoever Allah allows to be deluded, you can never be of any help to them against Allah. It is not Allah’s Will to purify their hearts. For them is disgrace in this world, and they will suffer a tremendous punishment in the Hereafter

**Do Not Grieve for Those Who Race to Disbelief**

Yā ayyuhā al-rasūl lā yaḥzunka alladhīna yusāri'ūna fī al-kufr consoles Messenger. Lā yaḥzunka, do not grieve you, yaḥzun from ḥ-z-n, to grieve, and yusāri'ūna fī al-kufr, race to disbelief, from s-r-', to be quick, race, indicates they hasten to disbelief, competing in it. The phrase appears also in *“Do not grieve for those who race to disbelief”* (5:41) itself and in *“Those who race to disbelief will not harm Allah”* (3:176). The racing shows eagerness, not reluctance. Two groups are described: those who say we believe with tongues but hearts disbelieve, munāfiqūn, hypocrites, and Jews who listen to lies. The first group says āmannā bi-afwāhihim wa lam tu'min qulūbuhum — we believe with mouths but hearts did not believe — showing contradiction between tongue and heart. The second, samā'ūna li-l-kadhib, eager listeners to lies, from s-m-', to listen, and kadhib, lie, indicates they listen to falsehood and accept it. The verse tells Prophet ﷺ not to grieve because their racing does not harm Allah or Messenger, only themselves.

**Distorting Scripture Taking Rulings Out of Context**

Yuḥarrifūna al-kalim min ba'di mawāḍi'ihi — they distort words after they have been placed — similar to verse 13 but with min ba'di, after places, indicating distortion after proper placement. They take rulings out of context, then say in ūtītum hādhā fa-khudhūhu wa in lam tu'tawhu fa-iḥdharū — if you are given this, take it, if not, beware — showing they pick and choose rulings based on desire, not truth. This is precisely taking ruling out of context and following desire, as verse 48 will warn *“Do not follow their desires”* (5:48).

**Hearts Not Purified, Disgrace and Tremendous Punishment**

Ulā'ika alladhīna lam yuridi Allāhu a yuṭahhira qulūbahum — they are those whose hearts Allah did not will to purify — indicates that purification of heart requires divine will plus human effort; when humans persistently choose lies and distortion, Allah does not will to purify. Qulūb, hearts, purification is needed because heart is source of belief, as *“In hearts is disease”* (2:10) for hypocrites. Lahum fī al-dunyā khizyun wa lahum fī al-ākhirati 'adhābun 'aẓīm — for them disgrace in world and tremendous punishment in Hereafter — shows two-level consequence: khizy, disgrace, in world, through exposure of lies and failure, and 'adhāb 'aẓīm, tremendous punishment, in Hereafter. The same closing appears in verse 33 for ḥirābah and here for distortion, showing that distorting scripture and racing to disbelief is also grave. The verse therefore consoles Messenger that their disgrace is already beginning, and tremendous punishment awaits, so grief is unnecessary.

The stoning incident is verified as occasion of revelation: Jewish couple committed adultery, Jewish leaders brought them to Prophet ﷺ hoping for lighter penalty, Prophet asked about Torah ruling, they hid stoning verse, 'Abdullāh ibn Salām exposed it, Prophet ordered stoning, as recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (3635) and Muslim (1699). The verse's description yuḥarrifūna al-kalim min ba'di mawāḍi'ihi matches that hiding. The phrase samā'ūna li-l-kadhib, listeners to lies, is interpreted as listening to false rabbis who permit lies. The verse's consolation lā yaḥzunka is also in *“Do not grieve for those who race to disbelief”* (3:176) for similar context, showing consistent prophetic consolation. The next verse will discuss listening to falsehood and consuming forbidden gain, continuing description of same group.

**What the Verse Exposes**

The verse is addressed to the Prophet, and it tells him not to grieve for the men who race into disbelief, the men who say with their tongues what is not in their hearts, and the people of the Book who trade in the law for a price. The sūrah has just dealt with the judicial system that the tribes had carried to Madinah, and this passage names the men who came to have a verdict arranged rather than a case decided. The Book's description is precise: they listen to falsehood and to a group that never came to the Prophet, and they alter the words after they have been placed in their proper places.
**The Ruling About A Verdict for a Price**

The verse and the passage that follows give the community the rule that a judge may decline to hear a case where the intent is to use him as an instrument, and that the penalty for the men who accept a bribe to alter the law is not the Prophet's to impose. The sūrah states the two men who come to be judged and the difference between them: one brings a case with a claim to a rule, and the other brings a device. The Sūrah of the Cow gives the general rule about the men who conceal what was revealed for a price (2:174 — *“Indeed, those who hide Allah’s revelations, trading them for a fleeting gain consume nothing but fire into their bellies. Allah will neither speak to them on the Day of Judgment, nor will He purify them. And they will suffer a painful punishment”*), and the Sūrah of the Family of ʿImrān states that they sell their covenant for a small sum (3:187 — *“˹Remember, O Prophet,˺ when Allah took the covenant of those who were given the Scripture to make it known to people and not hide it, yet they cast it behind their backs and traded it for a fleeting gain. What a miserable profit”*).
**The Men and the Occasion**

The passage is connected with the men of the Banū Naḍīr and the Banū Qurayẓah, and with a case of adultery brought to the Prophet by men who asked to be judged by their own book rather than by the law of God. The sūrah records the exchange that follows, where the Prophet is told to judge between them by what God has sent down and not to follow their wishes (5:42-49 — *“And judge between them ˹O Prophet˺ by what Allah has revealed, and do not follow their desires.”*). The verse in front of us sets the tone of the whole passage, and it tells the messenger what a judge is likely to meet in any town: a case that has been packaged before it arrives.

---

## Verse 5:42
> They eagerly listen to falsehood and consume forbidden gain. So if they come to you ˹O Prophet˺, either judge between them or turn away from them. If you turn away from them, they cannot harm you whatsoever. But if you judge between them, then do so with justice. Surely Allah loves those who are just

**The Judges Who Crowd Around the Lies**

The verse describes the men the Prophet is dealing with: they press forward to hear falsehood and they live on what is forbidden. Their habits are part of the case, because these were the people asking him to settle a dispute between them while not accepting his judgment as a whole. The verse gives him a real choice — judge between them, or turn away from them — and adds that if he turns away, they cannot harm him at all. The protection is stated again in the same sūrah, where God promises to guard him from the people (5:67 — *“O Messenger! Convey everything revealed to you from your Lord. If you do not, then you have not delivered His message. Allah will ˹certainly˺ protect you from the people. Indeed, Allah does not guide the people who disbelieve”*), so he is left free to take the case or refuse it without fear of what they will say about him afterwards.

**If You Judge, Judge With Justice**

The second half of the verse is the rule for the choice: if he does judge, then he must do it with justice, and God loves those who are just. The Book uses the same sentence for other communities and other dealings, so that the standard is not narrowed to a friendly circle (49:9 — *“Surely Allah loves those who uphold justice”*; 60:8 — *“Allah does not forbid you from dealing kindly and fairly with those who have neither fought nor driven you out of your homes. Surely Allah loves those who are fair”*), and the sūrah has already grounded it in the character of the believers, where being just is said to be closer to mindfulness of God (5:8 — *“O believers! Stand firm for Allah and bear true testimony. Do not let the hatred of a people lead you to injustice. Be just! That is closer to righteousness. And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah is All-Aware of what you do”*). Al-Qurṭubī noted what the word for justice asks of a judge here: a decision built on evidence, not on what he would prefer. The verse's point is that a bad record does not lower the standard of the court. Men who listen to lies all day are still entitled to a truthful ruling.

**Justice That Earns Love**

The verse ends with the promise that God loves those who are just. That is a larger sentence than it looks. It turns fairness from a public duty into a way of drawing near to God, and it puts the judge's motive in his own account rather than in the people standing in front of him. He is just because God is watching and because God loves the quality — not because the parties deserve his kindness, and not because the crowd is watching him.

---

## Verse 5:43

> But why do they come to you for judgment when they ˹already˺ have the Torah containing Allah’s judgment, then they turn away after all? They are not ˹true˺ believers

**Why Seek Judgment When Torah Contains Judgment**

Wa kayfa yuḥakkimūnaka wa 'indahum al-tawrāt fīhā ḥukm Allāh questions inconsistency. The same question appears in *“Why do they come to you for judgment when they have Torah”* (5:43) itself. The Torah's ḥukm Allāh, Allah's judgment, refers to stoning for adultery and other rulings, which they hid. Their coming to Prophet is not out of belief in his prophethood but out of desire for easier ruling, as verse 41 described they said if you are given this, take it, if not, beware. The verse therefore exposes that their seeking judgment is not sincere search for truth but search for desire.

**Inconsistency as Sign of Following Desire**

The inconsistency — having Torah with Allah's judgment yet seeking other judgment, then turning away when judgment matches Torah — is sign of following desire, hawā, not truth. The Qur'an warns *“Do not follow desire, lest it lead you astray from Allah's way”* (38:26) and *“Do not follow their desires over truth that has come to you”* (5:48). The Jews' behaviour exemplifies following desire: they wanted lighter penalty for adultery, so they hid stoning and sought Prophet hoping for lighter, but when Prophet gave stoning, which is true Torah ruling, they turned away, showing they did not want Torah either, only desire. The verse therefore teaches that following desire leads to inconsistency: one has scripture but does not follow, seeks other judge but does not accept. The remedy is to judge by what Allah revealed, as verses 44-48 will command.

**Extra Verified Detail on Torah Judgment**

The Torah's ḥukm Allāh, Allah's judgment, for adultery is stoning, as verified by Torah Deuteronomy 22:22-24 and by ḥadīth in Bukhārī (3635) where Prophet asked Jewish scholars about stoning in Torah, they said we blacken faces and flog, but 'Abdullāh ibn Salām said Torah says stoning, and they found it. The verse's question kayfa yuḥakkimūnaka, how do they make you judge, is also recited with different reading yuḥakkimūnaka, they make you judge, indicating they appoint you judge. The phrase wa mā ulā'ika bi-l-mu'minīn, they are not believers, is used also for those who do not judge by what Allah revealed in verses 44, 45, 47, showing that true belief requires judging by revelation. The following verse will detail Torah's guidance and light and role of prophets, rabbis, scholars as keepers.

---

## Verse 5:44
> Indeed, We revealed the Torah, containing guidance and light, by which the prophets, who submitted themselves to Allah, made judgments for Jews. So too did the rabbis and scholars judge according to Allah’s Book, with which they were entrusted and of which they were made keepers. So do not fear the people; fear Me! Nor trade my revelations for a fleeting gain. And those who do not judge by what Allah has revealed are ˹truly˺ the disbelievers

**The Book That Came Before**

The verse says that God sent down the Torah with guidance and light — the same two words the sūrah uses for the Qur'an when it speaks of a light and a clear Book that has come to them (5:15 — *“There certainly has come to you from Allah a light and a clear Book”*). The praise of the earlier Book is deliberate. The sūrah is not arguing against the revelation; it is arguing against the men who hold it and trade it away.

**Judged by It: Prophets, Rabbis, Scholars**

The prophets who submitted themselves to God — the Book calls them Muslim, submitters, whether they came before Muḥammad or not — judged the Jews by the Torah, and after them the rabbis and the scholars did the same. The verse names the two kinds of teacher: the *rabbāniyyūn*, those who raise people on knowledge, and the *aḥbār*, the learned men of the Book. It also says what their position was: God's Book was placed with them in trust, and they were made its keepers. That arrangement is a responsibility laid on people, not a guarantee. The Qur'an's own preservation runs on a different promise, given by God Himself, to guard the reminder (15:9 — *“It is certainly We Who have revealed the Reminder, and it is certainly We Who will preserve it”*), and the difference is the verse's quiet warning: what is handed to men can be lost by men.

**Do Not Fear People, Do Not Sell the Verses**

The instruction to the keepers is blunt: do not fear people, fear Me; do not trade My verses for a small price. The same warning had already been given to the Israelites, when they were told not to sell God's signs for a little gain and to be mindful of Him (2:41 — *“Believe in My revelations which confirm your Scriptures. Do not be the first to deny them or trade them for a fleeting gain. And be mindful of Me”*). A judge who fears his congregation will find the ruling that suits it; a judge who fears God will read the Book as it stands. The same verse closes with the sentence that has carried weight through the sūrah: those who do not judge by what God has sent down are among the disbelievers. The words fall on the man who has the Book in his hands and lays it aside — not on the community that never received it — and the next verse turns to the law of retribution that the Torah contained, so that the reader can see what the ignored Book actually said.

**The Three Verdicts in the Passage**

The command to judge by the Book is followed in this passage by three verses that end in three different words: those who do not judge by what God sent down are the disbelievers (5:44 — *“And those who do not judge by what Allah has revealed are ˹truly˺ the disbelievers”*), then the wrongdoers (5:45 — *“And those who do not judge by what Allah has revealed are ˹truly˺ the wrongdoers”*), then the transgressors (5:47 — *“So let the people of the Gospel judge by what Allah has revealed in it. And those who do not judge by what Allah has revealed are ˹truly˺ the rebellious”*). The commentators were careful about how to read the sequence. Ibn ʿAbbās and the scholars closest to him read the first verdict as a disbelief short of the disbelief that takes a person out of the faith altogether: the man who denies the ruling as a matter of belief is a denier, while the man who accepts it as God's ruling, bends it for a bribe, and knows what he is doing has done the deed of disbelief, the deed of injustice and the deed of going past the limits in one action. The three words are placed after three different situations on purpose. The first falls on the men who set the revealed ruling aside, the second on the judges who tilt it, and the third on those who go past what the Book contains. What all three have in common is the thing the passage is guarding: the Book in the hands of the people who are paid to keep it.

---

## Verse 5:45
> We ordained for them in the Torah, “A life for a life, an eye for an eye, a nose for a nose, an ear for an ear, a tooth for a tooth—and for wounds equal retaliation.” But whoever waives it charitably, it will be atonement for them. And those who do not judge by what Allah has revealed are ˹truly˺ the wrongdoers

**A Life for a Life**

The verse sets out what God laid down in the Torah: a life for a life, an eye for an eye, a nose for a nose, an ear for an ear, a tooth for a tooth, and equal retaliation for wounds. The law's logic is measure for measure and nothing past it: the answer follows the crime and stops where the crime stopped. The Qur'an gives the community the same instruction in another place — if you punish, punish with the like of what you were made to suffer (16:126 — *“If you retaliate, then let it be equivalent to what you have suffered. But if you patiently endure, it is certainly best for those who are patient”*) — and it tells the believers that retaliation in murder is prescribed for them (2:178 — *“O believers! ˹The law of˺ retaliation is set for you in cases of murder—a free man for a free man, a slave for a slave, and a female for a female.”*), and that in the retaliation there is life for you, so that you may be mindful of God (2:179 — *“There is ˹security of˺ life for you in ˹the law of˺ retaliation, O people of reason, so that you may become mindful ˹of Allah˺”*). The precision is the mercy inside the law: it shields the guilty from a punishment larger than his deed, and it shields the victim's family from a revenge that never stops. The same rule of measure for measure stands in the older books of law (Exodus 21:23-25 — *“But if any harm follows, then you must take life for life, eye for eye, tooth for tooth, hand for hand, foot for foot, burning for burning, wound for wound, and bruise for bruise.”* and Leviticus 24:19-20 — *“If anyone injures his neighbor; as he has done, so shall it be done to him:”*); what the verse fixes is the limit that keeps the measure just.

**The Man Who Waives It**

The verse then opens a wider door: whoever gives his right up as charity, it becomes an atonement for him. He may take what is owed him, and if he releases it for the sake of God, the Book calls the release an expiation that covers something in his own record. The same encouragement is given in the matter of divorce, where pardoning is nearer to mindfulness (2:237 — *“And if you divorce them before consummating the marriage but after deciding on a dowry, pay half of the dowry, unless the wife graciously waives it or the husband graciously pays in full. Graciousness is closer to righteousness. And do not forget kindness among yourselves. Surely Allah is All-Seeing of what you do”*), and a report the scholars grade sound says that no one gives up retaliation for the sake of God without God raising him in honour (al-Tirmidhī). The right remains his; the forgiveness is not demanded of him; but the verse shows him the better market for what he is owed, and the man who forgoes the blow keeps a brotherhood that the blow would have ended.

**Why the Verse Names the Book**

The law had been given to the people of the Torah, and their leaders had long since bent it: unequal penalties for equal wounds were part of what the sūrah is exposing, and the verse begins by naming the original measure they were working from. The verse also prepares for the sentence a few lines later, that God has given each community its law and its road, and that the aim is to compete in good deeds (5:48 — *“So compete with one another in doing good.”*). Its ending, that whoever does not judge by what God has revealed is among the wrongdoers, is not a licence for private vengeance. It is addressed to the men who held the Book and judged by their own preference — and to any reader who would take the law into his own hands while claiming to honour the Book that legislated it.

---

## Verse 5:46

> Then in the footsteps of the prophets, We sent Jesus, son of Mary, confirming the Torah revealed before him. And We gave him the Gospel containing guidance and light and confirming what was revealed in the Torah—a guide and a lesson to the God-fearing

**Gospel Given Containing Guidance and Light**

Wa ātaynāhu al-injīl fīhi hudan wa nūr — We gave him Gospel in it guidance and light — repeats description used for Torah in verse 44: fīhā hudan wa nūr, in it guidance and light, now fīhi hudan wa nūr for Gospel, showing that Gospel also contains guidance and light, not only Torah. Injīl, Gospel, from Greek euangelion, good news, contains guidance for monotheism and ethics and light that illuminates path. The same description appears in *“We gave him Gospel containing guidance and light”* (5:46) itself. The Gospel's guidance includes call to worship Allah alone, to be mindful, and to prepare for coming messenger, as *“And when Jesus said, O Children of Israel, I am messenger confirming Torah and bringing good news of messenger after me, Aḥmad”* (61:6) states. The light in Gospel illuminates, but over time, as verses 13-14 said, portion was forgotten and words distorted, so light dimmed, requiring new light and clear book, Qur'an, as verses 15-16 said.

**Chain of Revelation as Confirmation**

The sequence Torah, Gospel, Qur'an as each confirming previous shows chain of revelation: each confirms truth of previous and adds. The chain is mentioned in *“We sent down Torah, then Gospel, then Qur'an”* (5:44-48) as unit, showing continuity. The confirmation is not that later book copies earlier, but that it confirms its core: monotheism, justice, accountability. The differences are in law, sharī'ah, and way, minhāj, as verse 48 will say to each We ordained law and way. The Gospel's law was somewhat lighter than Torah's, easing some restrictions, as *“I have come to make lawful for you some of what was forbidden”* (3:50) says Jesus said. The Qur'an then confirms both and is protector, muhaymin, over them, as verse 48 will say. The verse therefore places Jesus in prophetic chain, not as divine, but as messenger confirming Torah and bringing Gospel with guidance and light.

**A Verse That Defines Jesus' Role**

This verse defines Jesus' role as messenger in footsteps of prophets, confirming Torah, bringing Gospel with guidance and light, guide and lesson for God-fearing. It counters claim that Allah is Messiah (5:17 — *“Indeed, those who say, “Allah is the Messiah, son of Mary,” have fallen into disbelief.”*) and that Messiah is third of three (5:73 — *“Those who say, “Allah is one in a Trinity,” have certainly fallen into disbelief. There is only One God. If they do not stop saying this, those who disbelieve among them will be afflicted with a painful punishment”*) by showing Jesus as messenger with book, not divine. The same definition appears in *“Messiah, son of Mary, was only messenger”* (5:75). The verse also shows that Gospel, like Torah, contains guidance and light, so People of Book have had light, but neglected portion, leading to enmity and distortion, as earlier verses said. The remedy is new light and clear book, Qur'an, that guides to ways of peace, as verses 15-16 said. The next verse will command people of Gospel to judge by what Allah revealed in it, showing that they too must judge by revelation, not desire. Injīl, Gospel, from euangelion, good news, is good news of monotheism and of coming messenger Aḥmad, as *“Bringing good news of messenger after me, Aḥmad”* (61:6) says. The Gospel confirming Torah means it confirms monotheism and justice, not abrogating core. The verse therefore places Jesus as messenger in chain, not divine, countering claim Allah is Messiah.

The same phrasing appears for Qur'an confirming previous scriptures in *“Confirming what was before it”* (3:3) and for Jesus here.

The same phrase hudan wa maw'iẓah appears for Qur'an as *“Guide and admonition for God-fearing”* (3:138).

---

## Verse 5:47

> So let the people of the Gospel judge by what Allah has revealed in it. And those who do not judge by what Allah has revealed are ˹truly˺ the rebellious

**People of Gospel Must Judge by What Allah Revealed**

Wa li-yaḥkum ahl al-injīl bi-mā anzala Allāhu fīh commands people of Gospel to judge by revelation. Li-yaḥkum, let them judge, or so that they judge, ahl al-injīl, people of Gospel, Christians, bi-mā anzala Allāhu fīh, by what Allah revealed in it, indicates that Gospel contains Allah's judgment that they must follow, just as Torah contained Allah's judgment that Jews must follow, as verse 44 said. The command is parallel to previous commands to judge by Torah, showing that each community must judge by its own book as long as it is preserved and not abrogated. The Gospel's judgments include monotheism, justice, and preparation for coming messenger. The verse therefore establishes that People of Gospel have responsibility to judge by what Allah revealed in Gospel, not by distorted interpretations that claim divinity for Messiah. The same principle appears in *“Let people of Gospel judge by what Allah revealed”* (5:47) itself, making it explicit.

**Judging by Gospel Means Following Its Monotheism**

Judging by what Allah revealed in Gospel means following its call to worship Allah alone and to follow messenger who confirms Torah, not following later doctrines that claim Allah is Messiah or third of three. The Gospel's true judgment includes *“Hear, O Israel, Lord our God is One”* (Mark 12:29) and Jesus saying *“I am going to my Father and your Father”* (John 20:17) indicating distinction, and *“Worship Lord your God”* (Matthew 4:10). The Qur'an says *“They did not follow Gospel as it should be followed”* (57:27) and *“They neglected portion”* (5:14). Judging by Gospel therefore means returning to its monotheistic core and to its prophecy of coming messenger, Aḥmad, as in *“Bringing good news of messenger after me, Aḥmad”* (61:6). The command li-yaḥkum ahl al-injīl is therefore call to follow true Gospel, not distorted. Verses 44-47 form parallel: verse 44 Torah with guidance and light, prophets and rabbis judged by it, do not fear people, fear Me, do not trade revelations, those who do not judge are disbelievers. Verse 45 ordained life for life etc., waiving is atonement, those who do not judge are wrongdoers; verse 46 Jesus confirming Torah, Gospel with guidance and light, guide and lesson for God-fearing; verse 47 let people of Gospel judge by what Allah revealed, those who do not are rebellious.

Ahl al-injīl, people of Gospel, appears only here in Qur'an, showing specific address to Christians to judge by Gospel. The command li-yaḥkum can be read as imperative, let them judge, or as purpose, so that they judge, both meaning they must judge by it. The phrase fa-ulā'ika hum al-fāsiqūn, they are rebellious, appears also in *“Those who do not judge are rebellious”* (5:47) itself. Is among verses used to discuss whether ruling by other than revelation is major or minor kufr, with Ibn 'Abbās explaining it can be kufr dūna kufr, lesser disbelief, if done while believing revelation is better but following desire. The next verse will introduce Qur'an as confirmation and protector, commanding Prophet to judge by it.

---

## Verse 5:48

> We have revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ this Book with the truth, as a confirmation of previous Scriptures and a supreme authority on them. So judge between them by what Allah has revealed, and do not follow their desires over the truth that has come to you. To each of you We have ordained a code of law and a way of life. If Allah had willed, He would have made you one community, but His Will is to test you with what He has given ˹each of˺ you. So compete with one another in doing good. To Allah you will all return, then He will inform you ˹of the truth˺ regarding your differences

**Book With Truth as Confirmation and Protector**

Wa anzalnā ilayka al-kitāb bi-l-ḥaqq muṣaddiqan li-mā bayna yadayhi min al-kitāb wa muhayminan 'alayh introduces Qur'an. Anzalnā ilayka al-kitāb, We revealed to you Book, bi-l-ḥaqq, with truth, indicates Qur'an is revealed with truth, containing truth, and for truth. Muṣaddiqan li-mā bayna yadayhi min al-kitāb, confirming what was before it of Book, muṣaddiq as before, confirming Torah and Gospel's truth, and muhayminan 'alayh, protector, guardian, trustee over them, muhaymin from h-y-m-n, to protect, watch over, also name of Allah al-Muhaymin, Guardian. Muhaymin means Qur'an is guardian that testifies to truth of previous books and preserves and judges over them, so what agrees with Qur'an is true, what contradicts is distorted. The same description appears in *“Confirming previous and protector over them”* (5:48) itself. The Book with truth is Qur'an, which confirms previous scriptures' monotheism and justice, and protects by clarifying what was hidden and distorted, as verses 15 and 41 described.

**Judge Between Them by What Allah Revealed**

Fa-uḥkum baynahum bi-mā anzala Allāh commands Prophet to judge between People of Book by what Allah revealed, i.e., Qur'an, not by their distorted desires. Fa-uḥkum, so judge, baynahum, between them, bi-mā anzala Allāh, by what Allah revealed, indicates that when they come for judgment, as in verses 42-43, he should judge by Qur'an, which contains true judgment that matches Torah and Gospel's original. The same warning appears in *“Do not follow their desires”* (5:48, 5:49 — *“And judge between them ˹O Prophet˺ by what Allah has revealed, and do not follow their desires.”*) repeated, showing that following desire is main danger when judging between People of Book who seek desire-based rulings, as verse 41 said they said if you are given this, take it, if not, beware.

**If Allah Willed He Would Make You One Community**

Wa law shā'a Allāhu la-ja'alakum ummatan wāḥidah — if Allah willed, He would make you one community — states that diversity is by divine will, not inability. The same counterfactual appears in *“If Allah willed, He would make you one community”* (5:48) and in *“If Allah willed, He would make you one community, but He leads astray whom He wills and guides whom He wills”* (16:93) and *“Had Allah willed, He would have made you one community”* (42:8). The will not to make one community is to test, as next clause says. The test is not to create division for its own sake but to test obedience within diversity, as *“We made you into nations and tribes to know one another”* (49:13) explains diversity as means to know, not to despise.

The same command appears in *“Compete in good deeds”* (2:148) and *“Compete in good deeds”* (5:48) itself.

**The Ruling the Schools Took From It**

The command to judge by what God has sent down is the base text of the sūrah's legal passage, and the jurists used the phrase *shirʿatan wa minhājan* — a law and a clear road — to settle a question about the earlier communities. Their laws were given for their times, and where this Book states a rule, the earlier rule is not the Muslim's law. The verse explains the differences among the communities as something God intended: if He had willed, He would have made them one people, so that the variation is a test of what each was given. The sentence then tells the believers what to do with the differences, and the instruction is not to argue about them but to compete in doing good.
**The Two Errors the Verse Corrects**

The verse has been read in the sūrah as a statement against two positions. The first is the rejection of judging by the revelation, which the verses after it address directly (5:49-50 — *“And judge between them ˹O Prophet˺ by what Allah has revealed, and do not follow their desires. And beware, so they do not lure you away from some of what Allah has revealed to you. If they turn away ˹from Allah’s judgment˺, then know that it is Allah’s Will to repay them for some of their sins, and that many people are indeed rebellious Is it the judgment of ˹pre-Islamic˺ ignorance they seek? Who could be a better judge than Allah for people of sure faith”*). The second is the idea that the arrival of this Book cancels the value of the earlier ones, and the verse refuses that as well, calling what came before guidance and light and keeping the demand that the People of the Book hold to what God gave them (5:46-47 — *“And We gave him the Gospel containing guidance and light and confirming what was revealed in the Torah—a guide and a lesson to the God-fearing”*; 5:68 — *“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “O People of the Book!”*). The community is given the last law and told that the earlier ones were real. The Book's argument is about the final authority of the messenger, not about the worth of what was sent before him.
**Where It Sits in the Sūrah**

The sūrah's first verses bind the believers to fulfil their contracts, and this passage sets out what they are to judge by. The two stand together: the community's promises and its courts. The verse before it explains that the Book was sent with the truth, confirming what came before it and standing guard over it, and the verse after it warns the Prophet not to follow the desires of people who have already been taught the truth and have left it. The law in the middle is therefore stated to the people who will carry it, and the sūrah tells them at the same time what they are not allowed to bend.

---

## Verse 5:49
> And judge between them ˹O Prophet˺ by what Allah has revealed, and do not follow their desires. And beware, so they do not lure you away from some of what Allah has revealed to you. If they turn away ˹from Allah’s judgment˺, then know that it is Allah’s Will to repay them for some of their sins, and that many people are indeed rebellious

**The Command Repeated for Emphasis**

The verse repeats the order given a moment earlier: judge between them by what God has revealed, and do not follow their desires. The repetition is deliberate, and the commentators read it as a warning about the exact situation of a judge sitting between two parties who badly want a particular ruling. The Book had already given the Prophet the same instruction (5:48 — *“So judge between them by what Allah has revealed, and do not follow their desires over the truth that has come to you.”*), and it gives the principle in its plainest form when it tells David not to follow his own desire, since desire leads a man away from the way of God (38:26 — *“And do not follow ˹your˺ desires or they will lead you astray from Allah’s Way.”*). The sūrah is not discussing a hypothetical court; it is describing a community in which people brought their cases to the Prophet in the hope of a ruling that suited them.

**The Warning About Being Drawn Off Course**

The verse adds a caution aimed at the judge himself: be careful that they do not turn you away from part of what God has revealed. The Book records how far such attempts went, when it says that the men of Makkah almost pulled the Prophet away from what had been revealed to him, hoping he would compromise and that they would then take him as a friend (17:73 — *“They definitely ˹thought they˺ were about to lure you away from what We have revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺, hoping that you would attribute something else to Us falsely—and then they would have certainly taken you as a close friend”*). The temptation is not always an open bribe. A judge can be worn down into dropping a rule he knows, and the verse tells him exactly where the danger lies: in pieces, not in the whole.

**What Their Turning Away Means**

If they turn away from the ruling, the verse says, then know that God intends to strike them for some of what they have done, and that many people are indeed rebellious. The sentence gives the reader a way to read the refusal: it is not proof that the message has failed. It is the ordinary behaviour of people whose chiefs the sūrah has already described, and the same verdict is repeated for them a few verses later (5:81 — *“Had they believed in Allah, the Prophet, and what has been revealed to him, they would have never taken those ˹pagans˺ as allies. But most of them are rebellious”*). The verse leaves the Prophet with a court that must be fair, a rule that must not be trimmed, and a clear-eyed expectation about the people who will walk out of it.

**The Judge and the Book He Judges By**

The command to judge by what God has revealed is repeated here from the verse before it, and the passage it belongs to runs from 5:42 — *“They eagerly listen to falsehood and consume forbidden gain. So if they come to you ˹O Prophet˺, either judge between them or turn away from them. If you turn away from them, they cannot harm you whatsoever. But if you judge between them, then do so with justice. Surely Allah loves those who are just”* to 5:50 — *“Is it the judgment of ˹pre-Islamic˺ ignorance they seek? Who could be a better judge than Allah for people of sure faith”*, where the Prophet is asked about the People of the Book who brought their disputes to him. The sūrah records the irony in one of those verses: they have the Torah, in which God's ruling stands, and they turn away from it (5:43 — *“But why do they come to you for judgment when they ˹already˺ have the Torah containing Allah’s judgment, then they turn away after all? They are not ˹true˺ believers”*). The Prophet's practice shows how he handled the cases that came to him from the other communities. When the matter of the Jewish couple taken in adultery was brought to him, he asked for the Torah and had the passage on stoning read out, and he judged by what God had revealed (al-Bukhārī and Muslim). The jurists drew the rule from the two verses together: the judge of the community judges by the Book of God for everyone who comes to him, and the community's courts are not run on the private preferences of the parties in front of them. The warning the verse gives is aimed at the judge himself, and the danger it names is piecework — accepting the rule that suits the room and dropping the one that does not.

**The Desire That Wears Down a Judge**

The verse calls the thing the judge must resist *their desires*, and the Book uses the same word for the pressure that bends a person away from a clear text. It warns the Prophet in another sūrah that the people of the Book and the idolaters would have liked him to follow their desire until they could turn him from part of what was revealed (17:73-74 — *“They definitely ˹thought they˺ were about to lure you away from what We have revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺, hoping that you would attribute something else to Us falsely—and then they would have certainly taken you as a close friend”*), and it names the man who takes his own desire as a god (45:23 — *“Have you seen ˹O Prophet˺ those who have taken their own desires as their god?”*; and see 2:120 — *“And if you were to follow their desires after ˹all˺ the knowledge that has come to you, there would be none to protect or help you against Allah”*, 28:50 — *“So if they fail to respond to you, then know that they only follow their desires.”*). The verse shows what the pressure looks like in practice. It is rarely a single open demand; it is the slow wearing down of a man who wants to keep the peace, the trimming of one rule to hold a room together, the quiet agreement that a particular text need not be pressed too hard this time. The Book answers it by putting the command first and the caution second — judge by what God has revealed, and be careful.

---

## Verse 5:50

> Is it the judgment of ˹pre-Islamic˺ ignorance they seek? Who could be a better judge than Allah for people of sure faith

**Is It Judgment of Ignorance They Seek**

A-fa-ḥukm al-jāhiliyyah yabghūn questions whether they seek judgment of ignorance. A-fa-, is it, interrogative with fa-, so, ḥukm al-jāhiliyyah, judgment of ignorance, jāhiliyyah from j-h-l, ignorance, era before Islam marked by ignorance of divine guidance, tribal custom, following desire. Yabghūn, they seek, from b-gh-y, to seek, desire, indicates they desire judgment of ignorance instead of Allah's judgment. The question is rhetorical, expressing disapproval: do they seek jāhiliyyah judgment after having Torah, Gospel, and Qur'an with guidance and light. The same phrase appears in *“Is it judgment of ignorance they seek”* (5:50) itself and in *“They sought judgment of ṭāghūt”* (4:60) for similar seeking of non-divine judgment.

**Jāhiliyyah as System Versus Islam as System**

Al-jāhiliyyah, ignorance, is not only lack of knowledge but system of values: tribalism, following desire, inequality, as in *“They sought judgment of ignorance”* (5:50) and *“Do not display as in former jāhiliyyah”* (33:33) and *“Ḥamiyyat al-jāhiliyyah, pride of ignorance”* (48:26). Islam is presented as system of knowledge, justice, equality, as verses 8, 42, 44-48 showed: be just, judge with qisṭ, judge by what Allah revealed. The contrast is therefore between two systems: jāhiliyyah and Islam, ignorance and guidance. The verse asks which system they seek, implying that seeking jāhiliyyah after guidance is regression. The same contrast appears in *“Is it judgment of ignorance they seek, who is better than Allah”* (5:50) itself, showing that seeking jāhiliyyah is seeking worse. The verse therefore completes argument that began with Torah, Gospel, Qur'an: each contains guidance and light, and judging by them is best, while seeking other is ignorance.

**For People of Sure Faith as Qualifier**

Li-qawmin yūqinūn, for people who have certainty, qualifies who recognizes best judgment. Without yaqīn, person may doubt and seek jāhiliyyah. The same qualifier appears in *“For people who are certain”* (5:50) and in *“For people who have certainty”* (45:4) for signs. The phrase suggests that judgment of Allah appears best to those with certainty because they have knowledge of Allah's wisdom and mercy, while those without certainty may be swayed by desire. The verse therefore links account of knowledge and law: certainty leads to accepting divine judgment, doubt leads to seeking ignorance. The next section will discuss alliances and not taking Jews and Christians as protectors to exclusion, continuing theme of following guidance versus following desire.

Jāhiliyyah is defined as period before Islam and also as attribute that can return, as ḥadīth says four matters of jāhiliyyah remain in community. Lisān al-'Arab defines jahl as opposite of 'ilm, knowledge, and jāhiliyyah as ignorance, but in Qur'anic usage it also means following desire and tribal custom. The phrase aḥsanu ḥukman, better in judgment, appears also in *“Who is better than Allah in judgment”* (5:50) itself, and is used to show superiority of divine law. The verse's closing li-qawmin yūqinūn, for people of certainty, indicates that certainty is needed to appreciate divine judgment, and certainty comes from knowledge and reflection, as *“We made from them leaders guiding by Our command when they were patient and certain of Our signs”* (32:24) shows patience and certainty lead to guidance.

---

## Verse 5:51

> O believers! Take neither Jews nor Christians as guardians—they are guardians of each other. Whoever does so will be counted as one of them. Surely Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people

**Prohibition of Taking Jews and Christians as Guardians**

Yā ayyuhā lladhīna āmanū lā tattakhidhū al-yahūda wa al-naṣārā awliyā' prohibits taking Jews and Christians as awliyā'. Ba'ḍuhum awliyā' ba'ḍ — they are guardians of each other — explains that Jews and Christians are allies of each other in opposing Muslims, though they differ among themselves, as verses 13-14 showed enmity among Christians and hard hearts among Jews, but against Muslims they ally. The prohibition is not against kind dealings, as *“Allah does not forbid you from being kind and just to those who did not fight you”* (60:8) allows kindness. Against taking them as protectors to exclusion of believers, as tafsīr clarifies: taking them as allies in war against Muslims or relying on them for protection instead of Allah and believers. The same prohibition appears in *“Do not take disbelievers as allies instead of believers”* (4:144).

**Allah Does Not Guide Wrongdoing People**

Inna Allāha lā yahdī al-qawm al-ẓālimīn — Allah does not guide wrongdoing people — seals with lack of guidance for ẓālimīn. Ẓālimīn, wrongdoers, from ẓ-l-m, to place thing not in its place, indicates those who place alliance where it does not belong, taking disbelievers as guardians instead of believers. Lā yahdī, does not guide, means does not guide to success or right path, because they chose wrong alliance. The same seal appears in many verses after warnings about alliances, as in *“Allah does not guide wrongdoing people”* (5:51) and *“Allah does not guide disbelieving people”* (5:67) for similar context. The lack of guidance is consequence of wrongdoing, not arbitrary denial. The verse therefore warns that taking Jews and Christians as exclusive guardians is ẓulm that prevents guidance. The next verse will describe those with sickness in hearts racing for guardianship, fearing turn of fortune, illustrating motive for prohibited alliance.

**Context of Madinan Alliances**

The verse was revealed when some Muslims, particularly hypocrites and some with weak faith, sought alliances with Jewish tribes and Christians for protection, fearing that Muslims might be defeated, as verse 52 says *“We fear turn of fortune will strike us”* (5:52). The prohibition is therefore about political alliance that compromises loyalty to Muslim community, not about personal kindness or business. Classical jurists distinguish between muwālāt, alliance of loyalty and protection, which is prohibited, and mu'āmalah, dealings like trade and kindness, which is allowed, as *“Be kind and just”* (60:8) permits. The verse's phrase ba'ḍuhum awliyā' ba'ḍ, they are guardians of each other, indicates that Jews and Christians, despite differences, ally against Muslims, so taking them as guardians to exclusion of believers is unwise. The next verse will describe racing for guardianship out of fear, and that Allah may bring victory that makes them regret hidden intentions.

**Guardianship Belongs to Allah, Messenger, Believers**

The prohibition is clarified by verse 55 which says your only guardians are Allah, His Messenger, and fellow believers who establish prayer and pay alms with humility. That verse defines true awliyā': Allah, Messenger, and believers. The prohibition in verse 51 is therefore to take Jews and Christians as guardians instead of those three, not to forbid all interaction. The same definition appears in *“Your guardians are Allah, His Messenger, and believers”* (5:55). The verse therefore establishes loyalty hierarchy: Allah first, then Messenger, then believers. Taking others as guardians to exclusion violates hierarchy. A verified ḥadīth in Sunan Abī Dāwūd (3052) says Muslim community is one hand against others, indicating internal alliance. The verse ends with Allah does not guide wrongdoing people, indicating that choosing wrong guardians is ẓulm that blocks guidance. The following verse will describe sickness in hearts as motive.

---

## Verse 5:52
> You see those with sickness in their hearts racing for their guardianship, saying ˹in justification˺, “We fear a turn of fortune will strike us.” But perhaps Allah will bring about ˹your˺ victory or another favour by His command, and they will regret what they have hidden in their hearts

**The Sick at Heart and Their Haste**

The verse shows a group whose hearts carry a sickness running ahead to make alliances with the enemies of the community. The sickness is the disease of doubt: they are not sure the Muslims will win, so they prepare a way out and look for protection on the other side. The Book uses the same word of the hypocrites in the Sūrah of the Cow, where the disease in the heart is named and then allowed to grow (2:10 — *“There is sickness in their hearts, and Allah ˹only˺ lets their sickness increase. They will suffer a painful punishment for their lies”*). The verse before this one had forbidden the believers to take such men as patrons (5:51 — *“O believers! Take neither Jews nor Christians as guardians—they are guardians of each other.”*); the verse in front of us shows what the forbidden step looks like from the inside, and how quickly men who are supposed to be inside the community go looking for a door.

**The Excuse: A Turn of Fortune**

Their reason is quoted in their own words: we are afraid a change of fortune will reach us — a defeat, a famine, a shift of power that leaves us exposed. The fear itself is not the crime. What the verse exposes is where the fear drove them: instead of taking the matter to God, who had promised the community His help, they took cover with the side they expected to win. The same weakness is described in the Sūrah of the Women, where a group feared people as they should have feared God, or more (4:77 — *“Then once the order came to fight, a group of them feared those ˹hostile˺ people as Allah should be feared—or even more.”*). The men in front of us have made a calculation about the future of the religion and left God out of the sum.

**What They Hid, and What Comes Out**

The last words of the verse give the end of the story. When God brings victory or some other command, the men who arranged their cover will regret what they hid in their hearts. The regret is not Cain's regret over his brother (5:31 — *“Then Allah sent a crow digging ˹a grave˺ in the ground ˹for a dead crow˺, in order to show him how to bury the corpse of his brother. He cried, “Alas! Have I ˹even˺ failed to be like this crow and bury the corpse of my brother?” So he became regretful”*); the same word is used, and the cause is different — they will discover that the door they kept quietly open was written down. The next verse shows the believers looking at them and asking whether these were the very men who swore solemn oaths that they stood with them (5:53 — *“˹Only then,˺ the believers will ask ˹one another˺, “Are these the ones who swore solemn oaths by Allah that they were with you?””*). The Sūrah of Muḥammad gives the same picture of the sick at heart staring at the Prophet when fighting is mentioned (47:20 — *“Yet when a precise sûrah is revealed, in which fighting is ˹explicitly˺ mentioned, you see those with sickness in their hearts staring at you like someone in the throes of death.”*). What the community is asked to remember is that the promise of help stands, and that God's party is the one that will prevail (5:56 — *“Whoever allies themselves with Allah, His Messenger, and fellow believers, then it is certainly Allah’s party that will prevail”*). The men who read the future off the strength of the day had misread which side was already decided.

---

## Verse 5:53
> ˹Only then,˺ the believers will ask ˹one another˺, “Are these the ones who swore solemn oaths by Allah that they were with you?” Their deeds have been in vain, so they have become losers

**The Question the Believers Ask**

The verse shows the moment the truth comes out. When the help of God arrives and the men who had been playing both sides are exposed, the believers look at them and ask one another: are these the ones who swore by God their strongest oaths that they were with us? The question is not for information; it is the sound of a community taking in what it is looking at. The Book gives the same picture of the hypocrites who use their oaths as a cover and then turn people away from the path of God (63:2 — *“They have made their ˹false˺ oaths as a shield, hindering ˹others˺ from the Way of Allah. Evil indeed is what they do”*), and of those who swear to the believers that they are among them when they are not (9:56 — *“They swear by Allah that they are part of you, but they are not. They only say so out of fear”*). The same formula of the strongest oath is put in their mouths elsewhere, when they swear that they would believe if a sign came down (6:109 — *“They swear by Allah their most solemn oaths that if a sign were to come to them, they would certainly believe in it.”*), which is why the Book treats their swearing as a symptom rather than a guarantee. The men who made the loudest promises are the first ones shown to have been somewhere else.

**Oaths Do Not Build a Record**

The verse then says what happened to their work: it came to nothing, and they became the losers. The word for deeds coming to nothing is used in the sūrah for any work separated from real faith (5:5 — *“And whoever rejects the faith, all their good deeds will be void ˹in this life˺ and in the Hereafter they will be among the losers”*): the deed may have been done, but it carries no weight in the account, because the man was not with the community he was serving. The Book had warned the same people how much better it would have been for them if they had simply done what they were advised (4:66 — *“Had they done what they were advised to do, it would have certainly been far better for them and more reassuring”*). What the verse establishes is a rule about promises: the strength of an oath is not evidence of loyalty, and the man who swears hardest is not necessarily the man who will be standing there when the fighting starts. A community that judges its members by their volume will be surprised; one that judges by their conduct will not.

**What Comes Next**

The verse stands at the hinge of the passage: the end of the men who covered their disloyalty with oaths, and the beginning of the promise that God will bring people in their place. The next verse describes the community that replaces them — people who love God and are loved by Him, gentle with the believers and firm against the aggressors, working in His way and afraid of no one's blame (5:54 — *“O believers! Whoever among you abandons their faith, Allah will replace them with others who love Him and are loved by Him.”*). The sūrah puts the two portraits side by side so that the reader can see exactly what the difference is: not the words a man says, but the direction of his life. The tradition records, in a report the scholars grade sound, that the first three men to be judged on the Day of Judgement are men whose good deeds were done for display, and whose work therefore comes to nothing (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2985). That report is the same rule as this verse, stated at the scale of a whole life.

---

## Verse 5:54
> O believers! Whoever among you abandons their faith, Allah will replace them with others who love Him and are loved by Him. They will be humble with the believers but firm towards the disbelievers, struggling in the Way of Allah; fearing no blame from anyone. This is the favour of Allah. He grants it to whoever He wills. And Allah is All-Bountiful, All-Knowing

**A Promise of Replacement**

The verse answers the possibility of desertion with a promise: if any of you turns back from his faith, God will bring a people in his place. The Book states the same law elsewhere in the plainest terms — if you turn away, He will replace you with another people (47:38 — *“If you ˹still˺ turn away, He will replace you with another people.”*) — and the sūrah of the Family of ʿImrān gives the test of that love: if you love God, then follow His messenger, and God will love you (3:31 — *“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “If you ˹sincerely˺ love Allah, then follow me; Allah will love you and forgive your sins. For Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.””*). The community is not dependent on any individual, however long his record; the religion belongs to God and moves on without the man who walks out.

**The Portraits of the Replacement**

The people described next have four qualities, and they are placed in two pairs. They are gentle among the believers and firm against those who wage war on them — the same balance the sūrah of Triumph uses for the Prophet and his companions, severe with the disbelievers and merciful among themselves (48:29 — *“And those with him are firm with the disbelievers and compassionate with one another.”*). Then they struggle in the way of God and are not afraid of anyone's blame. That last quality is the practical one: a man can be generous in private and silent in public, but a person who will not be moved by the criticism of his neighbours is a person who can be counted on when a position becomes unpopular. The Book allows kindness and justice towards those who did not fight the community (60:8 — *“Allah does not forbid you from dealing kindly and fairly with those who have neither fought nor driven you out of your homes. Surely Allah loves those who are fair”*), so the firmness named here is aimed at aggression, not at every neighbour of another faith.

**Love That Runs Both Ways**

The verse's centre is a sentence about love: God loves them and they love Him. Only a few places in the Book speak so plainly of love going in both directions, and the verse puts this mutual love at the heart of the community's identity. The verse then closes by naming the whole arrangement a favour from God, which He gives to whom He wills, and by naming Him the Bountiful, the All-Knowing. The reports connect the promise with the generations that came after the Prophet: a report graded sound says that when this verse came down he pointed to Salmān al-Fārisī and said that his people were the ones meant (al-Tirmidhī 3841, graded ḥasan), and the community that carried the religion into Persia and beyond became one of the answers to the verse's promise. What the passage leaves with the reader is the standard the promise set: love of God first, and a life that shows it in how a person treats his own people and how he stands when he is blamed for what is right.

---

## Verse 5:55
> Your only guardians are Allah, His Messenger, and fellow believers—who establish prayer and pay alms-tax with humility

**Who the Believers' Guardians Are**

The verse answers the question of loyalty that runs through this part of the sūrah. After forbidding the wrong alliances, it names the true ones: God, His Messenger, and the believers who establish the prayer and give the alms while they are humble. The word used for guardian carries protection, support and nearness. The Book uses it of God when it says He is the guardian of the believers (2:257 — *“Allah is the Guardian of the believers—He brings them out of darkness and into light.”*), and of the community when it says that the believing men and believing women are guardians of one another (9:71 — *“The believers, both men and women, are guardians of one another.”*). What the verse does is define where a believer's protection ultimately lies — not with powerful neighbours, and not with the men who offered cover in exchange for compromise.

**The People Described by Their Practice**

The believers named here are not described by their wealth or their tribe, but by two acts: they pray, and they give. The qualifier at the end, that they do this humbly, is read by the commentators in two ways — as a description of the posture of prayer itself and as a description of the manner in which the giving is done. Some of the early reports connect the words to a man who gave his ring in charity while bowing in prayer, and the story is used to show the two acts joined as one way of life. The Book uses the same pair, the prayer and the alms, again and again for the believers (2:277 — *“Indeed, those who believe, do good, establish prayer, and pay alms-tax will receive their reward from their Lord, and there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve”*), and it makes them the mark of the people whose guardianship can be trusted.

**Loyalty That Does Not Close the Door on Kindness**

The verse's restriction is about where a believer's ultimate loyalty rests, and the commentators were careful to say what it does not mean. The Book permits kindness and fair dealing with those who have not fought the community or driven it from its homes (60:8 — *“Allah does not forbid you from dealing kindly and fairly with those who have neither fought nor driven you out of your homes. Surely Allah loves those who are fair”*), and the sūrah will promise that the party of God is the one that will prevail. What the verse forbids is the arrangement of the frightened man in the earlier passage: a believer who settles his affairs by promising a share of himself to the side he thinks will win. The community's protection is God, His Messenger and the people who pray and give; the rest of the world may be dealt with kindly, and that is the difference the verse draws.

---

## Verse 5:56

> Whoever allies themselves with Allah, His Messenger, and fellow believers, then it is certainly Allah’s party that will prevail

**Whoever Allies With Allah, Messenger, Believers Prevails**

Wa man yatawalla Allāha wa rasūlahu wa alladhīna āmanū fa-inna ḥizb Allāhi hum al-ghālibūn promises victory for true alliance. Fa-inna ḥizb Allāhi hum al-ghālibūn — Allah's party will prevail — ḥizb, party, group, from ḥ-z-b, to gather, group, and ghālibūn, prevailing, victorious, from gh-l-b, to overcome. The promise is that those who ally with true guardians will be part of Allah's party, ḥizb Allāh, which will prevail over ḥizb al-shayṭān, party of Satan, as in *“Indeed party of Satan are losers”* (58:19) and *“Indeed party of Allah are successful”* (58:22). The same promise appears in *“Allah's party will prevail”* (5:56) itself. The prevailing is both worldly, victory over enemies, and otherworldly, success in Hereafter, as *“They are successful”* (58:22) for ḥizb Allāh.

**Alliance With True Guardians Versus False**

The verse contrasts with verse 51's prohibition of taking Jews and Christians as exclusive guardians and verse 52's racing for their guardianship out of fear. Those who take false guardians race for protection but end up regretful and losers, as verses 52-53 said deeds void, become losers. The same contrast appears in *“Your guardians are Allah, Messenger, believers”* (5:55) then *“Whoever allies with them, Allah's party prevails”* (5:56), showing that true guardianship leads to prevailing. The certainty contrasts with hypocrites' fear of turn of fortune, dā'irah, in verse 52: they feared turn against Muslims, but Allah promises His party will prevail, so fear is unfounded.

**Prevailing as Both Worldly and Otherworldly**

Al-ghālibūn, prevailing, indicates victory over enemies in world and success in Hereafter. In world, prevailing includes conquest, security, and being able to practice faith, as Muslims prevailed after alliances with true guardians. In Hereafter, prevailing includes Paradise, as *“They are successful”* (58:22) for ḥizb Allāh indicates. The same root appears in *“Allah will prevail”* (58:21) and *“We will surely prevail, I and My messengers”* (58:21). The prevailing is promised for those who ally with Allah, Messenger, believers, not for those who ally with disbelievers out of fear. The verse therefore gives confidence to believers: do not fear turn of fortune, do not race for false guardianship, ally with true guardians and you will prevail. The next verse will warn not to take those who mock faith as guardians, continuing theme of choosing guardians.

**A Verse That Defines Party of Allah**

This verse defines ḥizb Allāh, party of Allah, as those who ally with Allah, Messenger, and believers. The definition appears also in *“Party of Allah are successful”* (58:22) where qualities include not allying with those who oppose Allah and Messenger, even if kin. The party of Allah is therefore defined by loyalty, walā', to Allah, Messenger, believers, and disavowal, barā', from those who oppose. A verified ḥadīth in Sunan al-Tirmidhī (2516) says if you are with Allah, Allah will be with you, supporting that alliance with Allah brings prevailing.

---

## Verse 5:57

> O believers! Do not seek the guardianship of those given the Scripture before you and the disbelievers who have made your faith a mockery and amusement. And be mindful of Allah if you are ˹truly˺ believers

**Do Not Take Mockers of Faith as Guardians**

Yā ayyuhā lladhīna āmanū lā tattakhidhū alladhīna ittakhadhū dīnakum huzuwan wa la'iban mina alladhīna ūtū al-kitāb min qablikum wa al-kuffār awliyā' prohibits taking mockers as guardians. Lā tattakhidhū, do not take, alladhīna ittakhadhū dīnakum huzuwan wa la'iban, those who made your religion mockery and amusement, huzuw from h-z-', to mock, and la'ib from l-'-b, to play, amusement, indicates they treat faith as joke and play, not serious. The prohibition is similar to verse 51 but adds reason: they mock faith, so taking them as guardians is unwise. The same phrasing appears in *“Do not take those who made your religion mockery and amusement as guardians”* (5:57) itself.

**Mockery and Amusement as Attitude Toward Faith**

Huzuwan wa la'iban, mockery and amusement, describes attitude of disrespect: faith is taken as joke and play, not as serious guidance. The same pair appears in *“They made religion mockery and amusement”* (5:57, 5:58 — *“When you call to prayer, they mock it in amusement. This is because they are a people without understanding”*) and in *“They took religion as amusement and play”* (6:70) for disbelievers. Mockery includes laughing at prayer, at rulings, at Prophet, as Quraysh did. Amusement indicates they treat faith as entertainment, not commitment. Taking such people as guardians, awliyā', means seeking protection from those who do not respect your faith, which is contradictory: how can protector mock what you seek protection for. The verse therefore gives reason for prohibition: they mock faith, so they cannot be trusted as guardians. The same reason appears for not taking disbelievers as allies when they mock, as *“Do not take disbelievers as allies while they mock”* (4:140) warns.

**Why Mockers Cannot Be Guardians**

Mockers cannot be guardians because guardianship, walāyah, requires respect and protection, while mockery, huzuw, shows disrespect and intent to harm. A guardian who mocks faith will not protect it but undermine. The verse's prohibition is therefore logical: do not seek protection from those who mock what you want protected. Classical jurists distinguish between taking disbelievers as allies in war, which is prohibited, and dealing kindly, which is allowed, as *“Be kind and just”* (60:8) permits. The remedy is to take guardians who respect faith: Allah, Messenger, believers, as verse 55 defined.

**A Verse That Defines Loyalty Based on Respect**

This verse defines loyalty based on respect for faith: do not take as guardians those who mock faith. The principle is general: loyalty should be to those who respect what you hold sacred. The same principle appears in *“Do not take disbelievers as allies instead of believers, do you want to give Allah clear proof against you”* (4:144). The verse's reason — they made your religion mockery and amusement — is also used to forbid attending gatherings where revelations are mocked, as *“When you see those who engage in mocking Our revelations, turn away”* (6:68). The prohibition therefore includes both taking as guardians and participating in mockery. The next verse will give example: when you call to prayer, they mock it in amusement, because they lack understanding.

The pair appears for disbelievers who treat religion as play, as in *“They took religion as amusement and play”* (7:51).

---

## Verse 5:58

> When you call to prayer, they mock it in amusement. This is because they are a people without understanding

**Because They Are People Without Understanding**

Dhālika bi-annahum qawmun lā ya'qilūn — that is because they are people without understanding — gives reason: lack of 'aql, understanding, intellect. Lā ya'qilūn, do not understand, from '-q-l, to bind, intellect that binds, indicates they lack intellect that would make them respect prayer. The same phrase appears in *“They are people without understanding”* (5:58) and in many verses for those who mock, as in *“Most of them do not understand”* (5:103) for similar. The lack of understanding is not lack of intelligence but lack of using intellect to reflect on purpose of prayer, which is remembrance of Allah and seeking peace, as verses 15-16 said Qur'an guides to ways of peace. Mocking prayer shows they do not understand its purpose, so they treat it as amusement. The verse therefore links mockery to lack of understanding, indicating that understanding would prevent mockery.

**Mockery of Prayer as Sign of Disrespect for Faith**

Mocking call to prayer is sign of disrespect for entire faith, because prayer is pillar. The Qur'an says *“When you call to prayer, they mock”* (5:58) and also *“Do not take those who made your religion mockery”* (5:57) previous verse, linking mockery of prayer to making religion mockery. The same mockery appears for other acts: *“They took religion as amusement and play”* (6:70). The remedy is not to take such mockers as guardians, as previous verse commanded, and to be mindful of Allah if truly believers. The verse's reason — they are people without understanding — also appears for those who forbid good things that Allah made lawful, as verse 87 will say do not forbid good things, Allah does not like transgressors, indicating that understanding would prevent forbidding lawful and mocking prayer. The verse therefore teaches that lack of understanding leads to both mocking and forbidding, both forms of disrespect.

Adhān, call to prayer, is verified as being instituted in Madinah after Prophet ﷺ consulted about how to call to prayer, and 'Abdullāh ibn Zayd saw dream of adhān, as recorded in Sunan Abī Dāwūd (499, graded ḥasan). The phrases Allāhu akbar, Allāh is Greatest, and ḥayya 'alā al-ṣalāh, come to prayer, and ḥayya 'alā al-falāḥ, come to success, are in adhān, linking to verse 35's call to success, falāḥ, and verse 58's mockery of call. The mockery of adhān is reported in Sīrah that some polytheists mocked Bilāl's call, and some Jews mocked. The verse's reason lā ya'qilūn, they do not understand, is used also for those who consume forbidden gain and do not forbid evil, as verses 62-63 will show, indicating that lack of understanding leads to many sins. The following verse will question resentment.

---

## Verse 5:59

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “O People of the Book! Do you resent us only because we believe in Allah and what has been revealed to us and what was revealed before—while most of you are rebellious?”

Qul yā ahl al-kitāb hal tanqimūna minnā illā a āmannā bi-Llāhi wa mā unzila ilaynā wa mā unzila min qabl questions resentment. The same question appears in *“Do you resent us only because we believe”* (5:59) itself. The question is rhetorical, expecting answer: you resent us only for believing, which is not valid reason for resentment.

**While Most of You Are Rebellious**

Wa anna aktharakum fāsiqūn — while most of you are rebellious — adds that most of People of Book are fāsiqūn, rebellious, exiting obedience, as earlier verses said many are rebellious (5:49, 81 — *“If they turn away ˹from Allah’s judgment˺, then know that it is Allah’s Will to repay them for some of their sins, and that many people are indeed rebellious”*). Aktharakum, most of you, indicates majority, not all, acknowledging that some among them are upright, as verse 66 will say some are upright, and verse 82-85 will praise some Christians. Fāsiqūn, rebellious, from f-s-q, to exit, indicates they exit from obedience to Torah and Gospel by distorting, forgetting portion, and mocking. The phrase appears in *“Most of you are rebellious”* (5:59) itself. The contrast is: you resent us for believing, while most of you are rebellious, so if anyone deserves resentment, it is rebellious, not believers. The verse therefore turns table: who deserves resentment is not believers who believe, but rebellious who exit obedience. The next verse will ask shall I inform you of those who deserve worse punishment than rebellious, those condemned and turned into apes and pigs.

**A Question That Exposes Motive**

Hal tanqimūna minnā illā a āmannā, do you resent us only because we believed, exposes motive: resentment is for faith, not for wrong. The same question appears for Pharaoh's magicians who were resented only for believing, as in *“You resent us only because we believed in signs of our Lord”* (7:126). The pattern shows that disbelievers often resent believers precisely for believing, not for harming. The verse's inclusion of wa mā unzila min qabl, what was revealed before, indicates that Muslims believe in previous revelations too, so People of Book should not resent them for believing in what was revealed to them and before, because they themselves claim to believe in previous revelations. The resentment is therefore inconsistent: they claim to believe in previous revelations, yet resent those who believe in those plus Qur'an. The inconsistency shows that resentment is based on desire, not principle.

---

## Verse 5:60

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Shall I inform you of those who deserve a worse punishment from Allah ˹than the rebellious˺? It is those who earned Allah’s condemnation and displeasure—some being reduced to apes and pigs and worshippers of false gods. These are far worse in rank and farther astray from the Right Way.”

**Those Who Earned Condemnation and Displeasure**

Man la'anahu Allāhu wa ghaḍiba 'alayh describes those who earned la'nah, condemnation, curse, and ghaḍab, displeasure, wrath. La'anahu Allāhu, Allah cursed him, la'ana from l-'-n, to expel from mercy, and ghaḍiba 'alayh, was angry with him, ghaḍab from gh-ḍ-b, wrath, indicates divine curse and wrath for persistent disobedience, such as breaking covenant, distorting, and killing prophets, as earlier verses described for Israelites. The same pair appears in *“Those who earned condemnation and displeasure”* (5:60) itself. The curse and wrath are for those who broke covenant and distorted, as verse 13 said We cursed them and hardened hearts, and for those who said Allah is tight-fisted, as verse 64 will say they are condemned. The verse therefore identifies those who truly deserve resentment and worse punishment: those cursed and under wrath, not believers who believe.

**Reduced to Apes and Pigs and Worshippers of False Gods**

Wa ja'ala minhum al-qiradah wa al-khanāzīr wa 'abada al-ṭāghūt — made some apes and pigs and worshippers of false gods — lists transformations. Ja'ala minhum, made from them, al-qiradah, apes, plural of qird. Al-khanāzīr, pigs, plural of khinzīr, and 'abada al-ṭāghūt, worshippers of false gods, ṭāghūt from ṭ-gh-y, to exceed, tyrant, false god, indicates that some Israelites were transformed into apes and pigs as punishment for violating Sabbath, as in *“We said to them be apes despised”* (2:65) and *“We said be apes despised”* (7:166). Some became pigs, and some worshipped ṭāghūt, false gods. The transformation is both literal, as some exegetes say they were physically turned into apes and pigs, and moral, as they became like apes and pigs in behaviour. The inclusion of worshippers of ṭāghūt shows that following false gods is worse than being transformed, because it is choice. The verse therefore lists three categories of worst: cursed and under wrath, transformed into apes and pigs, and worshippers of false gods.

**A Verse That Lists Worst as Warning**

This verse lists worst categories as warning not to be among them. The listing of apes and pigs refers to Sabbath violators who were transformed, as *“Be apes despised”* (2:65) and *“We made them apes despised”* (7:166). The worshippers of ṭāghūt refers to those who worship false gods, exceeding limits. The verse's purpose is to show who truly deserves resentment and punishment, not believers who believe in Allah and previous revelations. The question hal unabbi'ukum, shall I inform you, is method to draw attention to important information, as in *“Shall I inform you of worse”* (5:60). The verse ends with sharrun makānan wa aḍallu, worse in rank and farther astray, indicating that rank and path are both worse. The following verses will describe those who come saying we believe but hide disbelief, and who race to sin and consume forbidden gain, showing that some among People of Book are among worst described.

---

## Verse 5:61
