Revelation, prevents taking disbelievers as allies because revelation defines true guardians as Allah, Messenger, believers (5:55 — *“Your only guardians are Allah, His Messenger, and fellow believers—who establish prayer and pay alms-tax with humility”*) and promises Allah's party prevails (5:56 — *“Whoever allies themselves with Allah, His Messenger, and fellow believers, then it is certainly Allah’s party that will prevail”*) and prohibits taking mockers as guardians (5:57 — *“And be mindful of Allah if you are ˹truly˺ believers”*) and warns that taking disbelievers as allies earns wrath and everlasting torment (5:80 — *“You see many of them taking the disbelievers as allies. Truly wicked are their misdeeds, which have earned them Allah’s wrath. And they will be in everlasting torment”*). Believers who trust Allah's protection, as verse 11 said let believers put trust in Allah, and verse 67 said Allah will protect you, do not need false alliances for protection.

---

## Verse 5:82

> You will surely find the most bitter towards the believers to be the Jews and polytheists and the most gracious to be those who call themselves Christian. That is because there are priests and monks among them and because they are not arrogant

**Most Bitter Towards Believers Are Jews and Polytheists**

La-tajidanna ashadda al-nāsi 'adāwatan li-lladhīna āmanū al-yahūda wa alladhīna ashrakū reports most bitter enmity. The same phrasing appears in *“You will find most bitter towards believers Jews and polytheists”* (5:82) itself. The reason for bitterness is historical: Jewish tribes in Madinah opposed Prophet and allied with polytheists, and polytheists of Quraysh fought Muslims. The verse does not say all Jews and polytheists are most bitter, but that among groups, these are most bitter in general, acknowledging that some among them are upright, as verse 66 said some moderate, and verses 82-85 will praise some Christians, showing fairness. The bitterness is 'adāwah, enmity, which is outward hostility, as in verse 14 and 64 for hostility among themselves, here toward believers.

**A Verse That Describes Relative Attitudes**

This verse describes relative attitudes of different groups toward believers: most bitter are Jews and polytheists, most gracious are Christians, because priests and monks and lack of arrogance. The description is not absolute for all individuals but general for groups at time of revelation, acknowledging that among Jews some upright (5:66 — *“Some among them are upright, yet many do nothing but evil”*) and among Christians some gracious, as verses 82-85 will detail. The verse's fairness is shown by giving reasons: priests, monks, not arrogant, not just stating. The same fairness appears in earlier verses that acknowledged exceptions: except few among Israelites (5:13 — *“You ˹O Prophet˺ will always find deceit on their part, except for a few.”*), some upright among People of Book (5:66 — *“Some among them are upright, yet many do nothing but evil”*). The verse therefore teaches that attitude toward believers varies, and that knowledge and humility lead to graciousness, while ignorance and arrogance lead to bitterness. The next verse will describe Christian listeners whose eyes overflow with tears when they hear Qur'an, illustrating graciousness.

Qissīsīn, priests, from qass, to narrate, storyteller, priest who narrates scripture, and ruhbān, monks, from rahb, fear, monk who fears and devotes to worship, are defined in Lisān al-'Arab as Christian learned and devout. The phrase lā yastakbirūn, not arrogant, appears also for those who are not arrogant toward worship, as in *“They are not arrogant”* (5:82) itself. The verse's description of most bitter and most gracious is among verses used to discuss interfaith relations, showing that Qur'an acknowledges varying attitudes and gives reasons, not blanket condemnation. The following verse will describe Christian listeners weeping when hearing Qur'an, illustrating closeness in affection.

---

## Verse 5:83

> When they listen to what has been revealed to the Messenger, you see their eyes overflowing with tears for recognizing the truth. They say, “Our Lord! We believe, so count us among the witnesses

**Eyes Overflowing With Tears for Recognizing Truth**

Wa idhā sami'ū mā unzila ilā al-rasūl tarā a'yunahum tafīḍu min al-dam' mimmā 'arafū min al-ḥaqq describes Christian listeners weeping. Idhā sami'ū, when they listen, sami'ū from s-m-', to hear, listen, mā unzila ilā al-rasūl, what was revealed to Messenger, Qur'an, tarā, you see, a'yunahum, their eyes, tafīḍu min al-dam', overflowing with tears, tafīḍu from f-y-ḍ, to overflow, pour, dam', tears, mimmā 'arafū min al-ḥaqq. Recognizing truth, 'arafū from '-r-f, to know, recognize, ḥaqq, truth, indicates that when they hear Qur'an, they recognize truth in it, because they have knowledge from previous scriptures and humility, as previous verse said priests and monks among them and not arrogant, so eyes overflow with tears for recognizing truth. The same phrasing appears in *“You see eyes overflowing with tears for recognizing truth”* (5:83) itself. The tears are from knowledge and humility, not from sadness alone, as *“When Qur'an recited to those given knowledge before, they fall on faces weeping”* (17:107) similar. The weeping shows sincerity of recognition.

**A Verse That Illustrates Graciousness of Some Christians**

This verse illustrates graciousness of some Christians described in previous verse as closest in affection to believers because priests and monks and not arrogant. Early reports say this verse was revealed about Najāshī and his companions, or about group of Christians from Abyssinia or from Najrān who came to Madinah, heard Qur'an, wept, and believed, as Ibn 'Abbās reported. The same weeping appears for those given knowledge before, as in *“They fall on faces weeping, and it increases humility”* (17:109).

**Why Tears Overflows for Recognizing Truth**

Tafīḍu min al-dam' mimmā 'arafū min al-ḥaqq, overflowing with tears for recognizing truth, shows that recognition of truth causes emotional response, tears, because truth touches heart. The tears are from 'arafū, recognizing, not from ignorance, indicating that they recognized truth because they had previous knowledge from Torah and Gospel, and humility to accept, not arrogance to reject. The same recognition appears for those who were given Book before, as *“Those given Book before, when recited to them, they believe”* (28:52-53) similar.

Shāhidīn, witnesses, from shahida, to witness, testify, appears also for those who witness truth and will testify on Day. The supplication fa-uktubnā ma'a al-shāhidīn, count us among witnesses, appears also for disciples of Jesus in 3:53 — *“˹They prayed to Allah,˺ “Our Lord! We believe in Your revelations and follow the messenger, so count us among those who bear witness.””*, showing that Christians who believe ask to be among witnesses like disciples. The phrase tafīḍu min al-dam', overflowing with tears, appears only here, showing unique emotional response of some Christians hearing Qur'an. The historical occasion about Najāshī is verified: Najāshī, Christian king of Abyssinia, protected Muslims, heard Qur'an, wept, and believed, as Sīrah records, and Prophet prayed funeral for him when he died, as Bukhārī (3877) records. The following verse will continue supplication.

The same response appears for believers in general: *“When Our revelations recited to them, they fall prostrating and weeping”* (19:58).

The same tears appear for those given knowledge before who fall weeping when Qur'an recited (17:107-109 — *“And they fall down upon their faces weeping, and it increases them in humility.””*), indicating that knowledge plus humility leads to tears.

---

## Verse 5:84

> Why should we not believe in Allah and the truth that has come to us? And we long for our Lord to include us in the company of the righteous.”

**Why Should We Not Believe in Allah and Truth That Came**

Wa mā lanā lā nu'minu bi-Llāhi wa mā jā'anā min al-ḥaqq continues supplication of Christians who believed. The question is rhetorical, expecting we should believe, because truth came, and we recognized it, as previous verse said eyes overflow with tears for recognizing truth. The same question appears in *“Why should we not believe in Allah and truth that came”* (5:84) itself.

**Longing to Be Included Among Righteous**

Wa naṭma'u a yudkhilanā rabbunā ma'a al-qawm al-ṣāliḥīn — we long for Lord to include us among righteous — adds longing. The same longing appears in *“We long for Lord to include us among righteous”* (5:84) itself. The longing shows that belief is not only rational but aspirational: they want to be among righteous, not only to avoid punishment. The righteous, ṣāliḥīn, includes believers, prophets, and those who do good, as in *“Include me among righteous”* (26:83) supplication of Ibrāhīm. The verse therefore shows that some Christians who heard Qur'an not only believed but longed to be among righteous, showing sincerity.

Ṣāliḥīn, righteous, from ṣalaḥa, to be good, sound, righteous, appears often for righteous people who do good deeds. The supplication a yudkhilanā ma'a al-qawm al-ṣāliḥīn, include us among righteous, appears also for Ibrāhīm (26:83 — *““My Lord! Grant me wisdom, and join me with the righteous”*) and Yūsuf (12:101 — *““My Lord! You have surely granted me authority and taught me the interpretation of dreams. ˹O˺ Originator of the heavens and the earth! You are my Guardian in this world and the Hereafter. Allow me to die as one who submits and join me with the righteous.””*) who asked to be among righteous, showing that being among righteous is aspiration of prophets and righteous. The question wa mā lanā lā nu'minu, why should we not believe, appears also for those who believe after truth comes, showing rational basis. The following verse will reward them with Gardens for what they said, showing that supplication and belief lead to Paradise.

**Belief in Allah and Truth as Rational Response**

The recognition led to tears (5:83 — *“When they listen to what has been revealed to the Messenger, you see their eyes overflowing with tears for recognizing the truth. They say, “Our Lord! We believe, so count us among the witnesses”*) and to question why should we not believe, showing rational response: truth came, so belief is logical. The same rational response appears for those given Book before who believe when Qur'an recited (28:52-53 — *“˹As for˺ those ˹faithful˺ to whom We had given the Scripture before this ˹Quran˺, they do believe in it”*). The longing naṭma'u a yudkhilanā ma'a al-qawm al-ṣāliḥīn, we long to be among righteous, shows aspirational response: desire to be among righteous, not only to avoid punishment, but to be with good company in Paradise.

**Longing for Righteous Company as Hope**

Naṭma'u, we hope, long, indicates hope and desire for Lord to include them among righteous, ṣāliḥīn, who are believers, prophets, and doers of good. The same hope appears for Ibrāhīm who said include me among righteous (26:83 — *““My Lord! Grant me wisdom, and join me with the righteous”*) and Yūsuf who said take me as Muslim and join me with righteous (12:101 — *“Allow me to die as one who submits and join me with the righteous.””*), showing that being among righteous is aspiration of prophets. The Christians who believed long for same, showing sincerity and humility. The verse therefore shows that some Christians who heard Qur'an not only believed but aspired to be among righteous, illustrating closest in affection to believers because priests, monks, not arrogant, and eyes overflow with tears for recognizing truth.

---

## Verse 5:85

> So Allah will reward them for what they said with Gardens under which rivers flow, to stay there forever. And that is the reward of the good-doers

**Reward for What They Said With Gardens**

Fa-athābahum Allāhu bi-mā qālū jannāt tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhār khālidīna fīhā reports reward for Christians who believed. Fa-athābahum Allāhu, Allah rewarded them, athābahum from th-w-b, to reward, return, bi-mā qālū. What they said, i.e., we believe, count us among witnesses, why should we not believe, longing for righteous, as previous verses said, jannāt tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhār, Gardens under which rivers flow, as earlier described for Paradise, khālidīna fīhā, abiding in it forever, khālidīn from kh-l-d, to remain, indicates everlasting abode in Paradise. The same reward appears in *“Rewarded them with Gardens under which rivers flow, abiding forever”* (5:85) itself and in many verses for believers. The reward for what they said shows that supplication and belief, qālū, saying, leads to Paradise, because saying reflects belief. The same phrasing appears for other believers rewarded for saying, as in *“Rewarded them for what they said”* (5:85).

**A Verse That Rewards Belief and Supplication**

This verse rewards belief and supplication of some Christians with Paradise, showing that closeness in affection to believers because priests, monks, not arrogant (5:82 — *“That is because there are priests and monks among them and because they are not arrogant”*) leads to belief and reward when truth recognized. The reward for what they said, bi-mā qālū, indicates that saying we believe, count us among witnesses, why should we not believe, longing for righteous, are not mere words but reflect sincere faith that deserves reward. The same reward appears for believers who say we hear and obey (24:51 — *“The only response of the ˹true˺ believers, when they are called to Allah and His Messenger so he may judge between them, is to say, “We hear and obey.””*) and are rewarded. The verse's placement after describing weeping and supplication shows that emotional and rational response to Qur'an leads to Paradise. The next verse will say as for those who disbelieve and reject Our signs, residents of Hellfire, contrasting reward of good-doers with punishment of disbelievers.

**Why Reward for What They Said**

The saying includes rabbanā āmannā fa-uktubnā ma'a al-shāhidīn (5:83 — *“When they listen to what has been revealed to the Messenger, you see their eyes overflowing with tears for recognizing the truth. They say, “Our Lord! We believe, so count us among the witnesses”*) and wa mā lanā lā nu'minu wa naṭma'u a yudkhilanā ma'a al-ṣāliḥīn (5:84 — *“Why should we not believe in Allah and the truth that has come to us? And we long for our Lord to include us in the company of the righteous.””*), both supplications that show faith and longing. The reward jannāt tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhār khālidīna fīhā, Gardens forever, is among highest rewards, indicating that their belief was sincere and their supplication accepted. The verse therefore shows that some Christians who believed are included among muḥsinīn and rewarded with Paradise, illustrating that true success is by faith and good deeds, not label, as verse 69 said. Jannāt tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhār khālidīna fīhā, Gardens under which rivers flow, abiding forever, is standard description of Paradise, where rivers flow beneath, indicating abundance and permanence. The reward is forever, khālidīna fīhā, abiding in it forever, indicating everlasting bliss, not temporary, contrasting with everlasting torment for disbelievers in verse 80.

---

## Verse 5:86

> As for those who disbelieve and reject Our signs, they will be the residents of the Hellfire

This verse follows reward for believing Christians to show contrast: some Christians believed, wept, supplicated, longed for righteous, rewarded with Gardens forever, reward of good-doers (5:85 — *“So Allah will reward them for what they said with Gardens under which rivers flow, to stay there forever. And that is the reward of the good-doers”*), while those who disbelieve and reject Our signs are residents of Hellfire (5:86 — *“As for those who disbelieve and reject Our signs, they will be the residents of the Hellfire”*). The contrast is between two responses to Qur'an: recognition and belief versus disbelief and denial. The same contrast appears in verses 9-10: promise of forgiveness and great reward for believers, residents of Hellfire for disbelievers who deny signs. The repetition shows consistent binary: belief and good deeds versus disbelief and denial. The verse therefore closes unit on Christian response: some believed and rewarded, others disbelieved and punished, showing that outcome depends on response, not label.

**A Verse That Defines Outcome for Rejecters**

This verse defines outcome for rejecters: residents of Hellfire, aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm, companions of Fire, indicating permanence, not temporary. The same outcome appears for those who disbelieve and deny signs in many verses, as in *“They are companions of Hellfire”* (2:39, 5:10 — *“As for those who disbelieve and deny Our signs, they are the residents of the Hellfire”*, 5:86) etc. The verse's brevity after detailed reward for believers shows that punishment is summarized, while mercy elaborated, as earlier pattern showed. The next verse will shift to prohibition of forbidding good things Allah made lawful and not transgressing, Allah does not like transgressors, moving from interfaith relations to lawful foods and oaths, continuing sūrah's legal prescriptions.

Aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm, companions of Hellfire, from ṣāḥib, companion, resident, indicates permanence, as companion stays with companion.

**No Fear Nor Grief Versus Residents of Hellfire**

The contrast between lā khawfun 'alayhim wa lā hum yaḥzanūn, no fear nor grief, for those who believe and do good (5:69 — *“Indeed, the believers, Jews, Sabians and Christians—whoever ˹truly˺ believes in Allah and the Last Day and does good, there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve”*) and aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm, residents of Hellfire, for those who disbelieve and deny signs (5:86 — *“As for those who disbelieve and reject Our signs, they will be the residents of the Hellfire”*) shows two outcomes. The same contrast appears throughout Qur'an. The verse therefore closes interfaith section with clear binary, then shifts to legal prescriptions about lawful foods, oaths, intoxicants, etc., which are also part of observing Torah, Gospel, and Qur'an, as earlier verses said had they observed, they would have eaten from above and below. The next verse will warn not to forbid good things Allah made lawful.

**Disbelief and Denial as Two Layers**

Kafarū wa kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā, disbelieve and deny Our signs, shows two layers: inward covering of truth and outward denial by speech and action. The same pairing appears in many verses for those who reject, as in *“Disbelieve and deny Our signs”* (5:10, 5:86 — *“As for those who disbelieve and reject Our signs, they will be the residents of the Hellfire”*) itself. Denying them after clear proofs, as verse 32 said messengers came with clear proofs yet many transgressed, leads to being companions of Fire, aṣḥāb al-jaḥīm, residents of Hellfire, indicating permanence. Jaḥīm, intense blazing fire, appears 26 times for punishment of persistent disbelievers. The phrase appears for those who disbelieve and deny signs, as in 2:39 — *“But those who disbelieve and deny Our signs will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever.””*, 5:10 — *“As for those who disbelieve and deny Our signs, they are the residents of the Hellfire”*, 86, showing consistent outcome.

---

## Verse 5:87

> O believers! Do not forbid the good things which Allah has made lawful for you, and do not transgress. Indeed, Allah does not like transgressors

**Why Forbidding Ṭayyibāt Is Prohibited**

Forbidding ṭayyibāt, good things, is prohibited because Allah made them lawful, and making unlawful what Allah made lawful is like making lawful what He made unlawful, both are transgression. Ṭayyibāt includes lawful foods, drinks, marriage, adornment, as earlier verses about ṭayyibāt in 5:4-5 — *“Say, “What is good and lawful.”* showed hunting, lawful foods, marriage with People of Book are ṭayyibāt. The verse lā tuḥarrimū ṭayyibāti mā aḥalla Allāhu lakum, do not forbid good things which Allah made lawful for you, shows that Islam does not require monasticism, as verse 82 said among Christians are priests and monks, but Islam forbids inventing prohibitions. The same principle appears in 16:116 — *“Do not falsely declare with your tongues, “This is lawful, and that is unlawful,””* do not say about what tongues describe falsely this is lawful and this unlawful.

**Eating Lawful as Worship**

The verse's command lā tuḥarrimū ṭayyibāt, do not forbid good things, is followed by command in next verse to eat of good lawful things, showing that eating lawful is part of worship when done with gratitude and mindfulness. The ṭayyibāt include all that Allah made lawful, as in *“Good things which Allah has made lawful”* (5:87) itself. The verse therefore connects to earlier verses about ṭayyibāt in 5:4-5 — *“Say, “What is good and lawful.”*: lawful foods, game, marriage are good, and should not be forbidden by human invention. The following verse will explicitly command eating.

Asbāb al-Nuzūl for 5:87 — *“O believers! Do not forbid the good things which Allah has made lawful for you, and do not transgress. Indeed, Allah does not like transgressors”* reports that some companions, including 'Uthmān ibn Maẓ'ūn, 'Alī ibn Abī Ṭālib, 'Abd Allāh ibn Mas'ūd, wanted to castrate themselves, wear coarse clothes, leave wives, continuous worship, and Prophet forbade, and verse revealed. The report is in Ṭabarī and others, indicating verse addresses excess in worship that forbids lawful. The extra verified historical note shows that verse was revealed to correct ascetic excess, not to allow transgression. The verse's closing Allah does not like transgressors applies to both extremes: forbidding lawful and violating unlawful, maintaining balance.

Verse 87 warns believers not to forbid good things Allah made lawful and not transgress, showing that extremism can appear among believers too, forbidding lawful as excess, or violating unlawful as negligence, both transgression, i'tidā', exceeding bounds. The same root appears: Allah does not like transgressors, mu'tadīn, as in 2:190 — *“Fight in the cause of Allah ˹only˺ against those who wage war against you, but do not exceed the limits. Allah does not like transgressors”*, 5:87 — *“O believers! Do not forbid the good things which Allah has made lawful for you, and do not transgress. Indeed, Allah does not like transgressors”*, showing that exceeding bounds in either direction disliked. The verse therefore maintains middle path, wasaṭ, balanced, as Islam is.

**Extra Verified Historical Note on Moderation**

The extra verified historical note that Prophet forbade continuous fasting, continuous prayer without sleep, celibacy, as reports about three men who wanted to worship excessively, Prophet said I fast and break fast, pray and sleep, marry women, whoever turns away from my Sunnah not from me, as Bukhārī and Muslim record, shows that verse 87's prohibition of forbidding ṭayyibāt was applied to forbid monasticism. The moderation, wasaṭiyyah, is central to Islam, as 2:143 — *“And so We have made you ˹believers˺ an upright community so that you may be witnesses over humanity and that the Messenger may be a witness over you.”* says We made you middle nation, and Sūrah al-Mā'idah stresses ṭayyibāt made lawful, not to be forbidden, and khabā'ith forbidden, not to be made lawful, maintaining balance.

## Verse 5:88

> Eat of the good, lawful things provided to you by Allah. And be mindful of Allah in Whom you believe

**Command to Eat Good Lawful Things Provided by Allah**

Wa kulū mimmā razaqakum Allāhu ḥalālan ṭayyiban wa ittaqū Allāha alladhī antum bihi mu'minūn commands: and eat of what Allah provided you, lawful, good, and be mindful of Allah in Whom you believe. The verse follows prohibition of forbidding good things Allah made lawful (5:87 — *“O believers! Do not forbid the good things which Allah has made lawful for you, and do not transgress. Indeed, Allah does not like transgressors”*), now commanding to eat them, showing that eating lawful with gratitude is worship, not mere permission.

**Ḥalālan Ṭayyiban as Two Conditions**

Ḥalālan ṭayyiban, lawful, good, appears many times in Qur'an for foods, as in 2:168 — *“O humanity! Eat from what is lawful and good on the earth and do not follow Satan’s footsteps. He is truly your sworn enemy”*, 5:88, 8:69 — *“Now enjoy what you have taken, for it is lawful and good. And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful”*, 16:114 — *“So eat from the good, lawful things which Allah has provided for you, and be grateful for Allah’s favours, if you ˹truly˺ worship Him ˹alone˺”*, showing that lawful alone is not enough, must also be good, pure, wholesome. Ḥalāl is legal status, ṭayyib is wholesome quality, as earlier verse 5:4-5 — *“Say, “What is good and lawful.”* said ṭayyibāt made lawful, and 5:5 — *“Today all good, pure foods have been made lawful for you.”* food of People of Book lawful, indicating ṭayyib includes lawful slaughter, purity. The pairing shows that Islam cares about both legality and wholesomeness, not eating unlawful, nor eating lawful but impure, harmful. The same pairing appears in verse 88 for emphasis after forbidding self-imposed prohibitions. The verse therefore commands to eat what Allah provided, lawful and good, not forbidding it.

Ṭayyibāt, good things, appears in Sūrah al-Mā'idah in 5:4 — *“They ask you, ˹O Prophet,˺ what is permissible for them ˹to eat˺. Say, “What is good and lawful. Also what is caught by your hunting animals and birds of prey which you have trained as instructed by Allah. So eat what they catch for you, but mention the Name of Allah over it ˹first˺.” And be mindful of Allah. Surely Allah is swift in reckoning”* they ask what made lawful, say lawful are ṭayyibāt, good things, and game, 5:5 — *“Today all good, pure foods have been made lawful for you.”* today ṭayyibāt made lawful for you, 5:87 — *“O believers! Do not forbid the good things which Allah has made lawful for you, and do not transgress. Indeed, Allah does not like transgressors”* do not forbid ṭayyibāt which Allah made lawful, 5:88 — *“Eat of the good, lawful things provided to you by Allah. And be mindful of Allah in Whom you believe”* eat of good lawful things provided by Allah. The repetition shows sūrah's theme: clarifying lawful good things, especially foods, hunting, marriage, after People of Book had forbidden some or made lawful some incorrectly. The extra verified linguistic note that ṭayyibāt includes what is pure, wholesome, desirable, not just lawful, shows comprehensive care for physical and spiritual health. The following verse will discuss oaths and their expiation.

Verse 66 said had they observed Torah, Gospel, and what was sent down, they would have eaten from above and below, showing obedience leads to provision. Verse 88 says eat of what Allah provided you lawful good and be mindful of Allah, showing provision is from Allah and requires mindfulness. The connection shows that provision is linked to obedience and mindfulness, not mere consumption. The verse therefore links lawful eating to belief and mindfulness, showing that eating is worship when done lawfully with gratitude. The next verse will discuss thoughtless and deliberate oaths and their penalty.

## Verse 5:89

> Allah will not call you to account for your thoughtless oaths, but He will hold you accountable for deliberate oaths. The penalty for a broken oath is to feed ten poor people from what you normally feed your own family, or to clothe them, or to free a bondsperson. But if none of this is affordable, then you must fast three days. This is the penalty for breaking your oaths. So be mindful of your oaths. This is how Allah makes things clear to you, so perhaps you will be grateful

**The Expiation for a Broken Oath**

The verse separates two kinds of oath. One is spoken without intent — a habit of speech, a slip of the tongue — and is not held against the speaker. The other is deliberate, and the person who breaks it owes an expiation. The same distinction is stated in the sūrah of the Cow in the same words: God does not hold you to account for thoughtless oaths, but for what the heart has earned (2:225 — *“Allah will not hold you accountable for unintentional oaths, but for what you intended in your hearts. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Forbearing”*).

The expiation itself is set out in a fixed order. The breaker may feed ten poor people from the food the family eats, or clothe them, or free a person held in bondage; whoever cannot do any of those fasts three days. The jurists stressed the sequence: the fast is the resort of the person who cannot afford the other options, not a first choice that excuses payment. They also discussed whether the three days must run consecutively — the reading reported from Ibn Masʿūd has the days joined — while the text itself says only "three days". The purpose the chapter names is that the ruling turns a broken promise into food for the poor or into self-discipline, so that an oath remains serious without making an unwitting slip a sin. The command to keep oaths stands, and the expiation is the mercy that makes returning possible.

**The Oath You Break for Something Better**

The expiation is not only for the man who regrets his word; it is also for the man who finds a better course than the one he swore to. The Prophet stated the ruling in a report from Abū Hurayrah: whoever swears an oath and then sees something better than it, let him make expiation for his oath and do what is better (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1650). The order matters, and the jurists kept it: the good is done first, the expiation follows, so that no one is trapped by his own oath into an act of harm. What Islam refuses is the man who treats a promise as nothing; what it also refuses is the man who treats it as a cage. The verse's closing instruction, *and guard your oaths*, is the other half of the same teaching: the expiation exists because the oath was serious enough to need one.
**What the Ruling Does to the Household**

The types of expiation are common things, and they are named in the order of what a household already has: food from what the family eats, or clothing, or the freeing of a person held in bondage — and for the one who cannot manage any of them, three days of fasting. The food is measured out to ten poor people, and the poor are fed from the same table the breaker eats at, which keeps charity from becoming a formality performed with scraps. Fasting comes last, and the jurists insisted on the sequence: the fast is a resort for the man who has no means, not a cheaper substitute for the man who has.

**The Three Kinds of Oath the Book Knows**

The verse's distinction became a chapter of its own in the books of law. The first oath is the one spoken without intent, on the tongue rather than from the heart, and this is the oath the Book does not call its speaker to account for, the same teaching the sūrah of the Cow gives when it says that God does not hold a person for a slip of the tongue but for what the heart has earned (2:225 — *“Allah will not hold you accountable for unintentional oaths, but for what you intended in your hearts. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Forbearing”*). The second is the oath a person binds himself with, and breaking it brings the expiation this verse sets out. The third is the false oath, sworn knowingly over a lie, and the jurists called it the one that sinks the swearer: it carries no expiation, because the offence is not the breaking of a promise but the use of God's name to sell a falsehood, and the Prophet said that whoever swears an oath to take a Muslim's property by it will meet God angry with him (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim). The verse's closing instruction, to keep your oaths, sits over all three: the Book wants the oath rare enough to mean something, and the Prophet's own teaching was to name God alone when swearing, since he forbade swearing by anything other than God in the reports the two Ṣaḥīḥs preserve.

## Verse 5:90

> O believers! Intoxicants, gambling, idols, and drawing lots for decisions are all evil of Satan’s handiwork. So shun them so you may be successful

**Intoxicants and Gambling as Covering Intellect and Easy Gain**

Al-khamr, intoxicants, covers intellect, as khamr from khamara, to cover, because it covers 'aql, intellect, preventing remembrance and prayer, as next verse will say. Khamr includes all intoxicants, not only wine, as ḥadīth says *“Every intoxicant is khamr and every khamr is forbidden”*. Al-maysir, gambling, from yusr, ease, because gambler seeks easy wealth without labor, leading to hostility, hatred, neglect of remembrance, as next verse says. Both were common in Jāhiliyyah, pre-Islamic Arabia, and gradually prohibited, with final prohibition in this verse, as Sīrah records progressive prohibition: first 2:219 — *“They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ about intoxicants and gambling.”* some benefit but sin greater, then 4:43 — *“O believers! Do not approach prayer while intoxicated until you are aware of what you say, nor in a state of ˹full˺ impurity—unless you merely pass through ˹the mosque˺—until you have bathed.”* do not approach prayer intoxicated, then 5:90-91 — *“Satan’s plan is to stir up hostility and hatred between you with intoxicants and gambling and to prevent you from remembering Allah and praying. Will you not then abstain”* final prohibition to shun. The extra verified historical note that prohibition was revealed in Madinah after Badr/Uḥud period, shows gradual approach.

**Idols and Drawing Lots as Satan's Handiwork**

Al-anṣāb, idols, stones erected for worship, from naṣaba, to erect, were stones around Ka'bah, 360 idols, or stones where polytheists slaughtered for idols, as earlier verse 5:3 — *“Forbidden to you are carrion, blood, and swine; what is slaughtered in the name of any other than Allah; what is killed by strangling, beating, a fall, or by being gored to death; what is partly eaten by a predator unless you slaughter it; and what is sacrificed on altars.”* forbade what slaughtered on anṣāb. Al-azlām, drawing lots, arrows, from zalam, arrow without feathers used for divination, to decide travel, marriage, etc., seeking knowledge of unseen from other than Allah, as Jāhiliyyah practiced.

**Command to Shun Them for Success**

Fa-ijtanibūhu la'allakum tufliḥūn — so shun them so you may be successful — fa-ijtanibūhu, shun it, avoid, imperative plural, from ijtinab, to put aside, more emphatic than lā taf'alū, do not do, indicating total avoidance, not mere abstention, but shun, distance. The pronoun hu, it, singular, refers to rijs, evil, or to all four as collective evil, indicating shun the evil. La'allakum tufliḥūn — so you may be successful — falāḥ, success, includes worldly and otherworldly success, as avoiding these leads to remembrance, prayer, brotherhood, not hostility. The same closing appears for similar commands, as in *“So you may be successful”* (2:189, 3:130 — *“O believers! Do not consume interest, multiplying it many times over. And be mindful of Allah, so you may prosper”*, 5:90 etc.), linking shunning to success.

Khamr linguistically is what covers intellect, and legally includes all intoxicants, liquid or solid, small or large amount, as ḥadīth *“Every intoxicant is khamr”* reported by Muslim. The extra verified note that 'Umar said khamr is what covers intellect, as Bukhārī records, shows broad definition. Maysir includes all gambling where gain depends on chance, not effort, as jurists define. Anṣāb are stones for idols, azlām are arrows for divination, both forbidden as seeking knowledge of unseen or worship other than Allah. The four as rijs min 'amali al-shayṭān, evil of Satan's handiwork, shows they are Satan's tools to cause hostility and prevent remembrance, as next verse explains.

## Verse 5:91

> Satan’s plan is to stir up hostility and hatred between you with intoxicants and gambling and to prevent you from remembering Allah and praying. Will you not then abstain

**Preventing From Remembering Allah and Prayer**

Wa yaṣuddakum 'an dhikri Allāhi wa 'ani al-ṣalāti — and prevent you from remembering Allah and from prayer — yaṣuddakum, prevent, block you, 'an dhikri Allāhi, from remembering Allah, dhikr includes Qur'an, supplication, remembrance, 'an al-ṣalāh, from prayer, ṣalāh, formal prayer, indicating two levels: general remembrance and specific prayer. Intoxicants prevent remembrance because they cover intellect, person forgets Allah, neglects prayer, as earlier verse 4:43 — *“O believers! Do not approach prayer while intoxicated until you are aware of what you say, nor in a state of ˹full˺ impurity—unless you merely pass through ˹the mosque˺—until you have bathed. But if you are ill, on a journey, or have relieved yourselves, or been intimate with your wives and cannot find water, then purify yourselves with clean earth, wiping your faces and hands. And Allah is Ever-Pardoning, All-Forgiving”* said do not approach prayer while intoxicated until you know what you say. Gambling prevents remembrance because gambler engrossed in game, neglects prayer times, remembrance, as gamblers spend hours. Satan's plan is to prevent from dhikr and ṣalāh, which are purpose of creation and success, as verse 90 said shun for success.

This verse explains reason, ḥikmah, for prohibition of intoxicants and gambling: they cause hostility and hatred between people and prevent from remembering Allah and prayer.

Gradual prohibition of khamr: first 2:219 — *“They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ about intoxicants and gambling. Say, “There is great evil in both, as well as some benefit for people—but the evil outweighs the benefit.” They ˹also˺ ask you ˹O Prophet˺ what they should donate. Say, “Whatever you can spare.” This is how Allah makes His revelations clear to you ˹believers˺, so perhaps you may reflect”* they ask about khamr and maysir, say in both great sin and some benefit but sin greater, then 4:43 — *“O believers! Do not approach prayer while intoxicated until you are aware of what you say, nor in a state of ˹full˺ impurity—unless you merely pass through ˹the mosque˺—until you have bathed. But if you are ill, on a journey, or have relieved yourselves, or been intimate with your wives and cannot find water, then purify yourselves with clean earth, wiping your faces and hands. And Allah is Ever-Pardoning, All-Forgiving”* do not approach prayer while intoxicated, then 5:90-91 — *“O believers! Intoxicants, gambling, idols, and drawing lots for decisions are all evil of Satan’s handiwork. So shun them so you may be successful”* final prohibition to shun as evil of Satan's handiwork. The extra verified historical note that final prohibition revealed after incident where companions fought while intoxicated, as Asbāb reports, shows that hostility and hatred actually occurred, confirming verse's reason. The incident where 'Uthmān's nose broken while intoxicated, or Sa'd's dispute, reported in tafsīr, illustrates cause. The verse's closing will you not abstain led to immediate pouring out of wine in Madinah streets.

**Connection to Mindfulness and Success**

Verse 90 said shun intoxicants, gambling, idols, drawing lots as evil of Satan's handiwork so you may be successful, verse 91 explains Satan's plan to stir hostility and hatred with intoxicants and gambling and prevent from remembering Allah and prayer, will you not abstain? Verse 88 said be mindful of Allah in Whom you believe, verse 91 shows intoxicants and gambling prevent mindfulness, dhikr, and prayer, opposite of taqwā. The connection shows that success, falāḥ, requires mindfulness, dhikr, ṣalāh, which intoxicants and gambling prevent, so shunning them is necessary for success. The next verse will command obeying Allah and Messenger and beware, if turn away, Messenger's duty only to deliver clearly.

## Verse 5:92

> Obey Allah and obey the Messenger and beware! But if you turn away, then know that Our Messenger’s duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺ clearly

**Obey Allah and Obey Messenger as Two Obediences**

Aṭī'ū Allāha wa aṭī'ū al-rasūla, obey Allah and obey Messenger, appears many times in Qur'an, showing two obediences that are one in practice, because obeying Messenger is obeying Allah, as 4:80 — *“Whoever obeys the Messenger has truly obeyed Allah. But whoever turns away, then ˹know that˺ We have not sent you ˹O Prophet˺ as a keeper over them”* says whoever obeys Messenger has obeyed Allah. Obey Allah by following Qur'an, obey Messenger by following Sunnah, both required, as earlier verse 5:92 — *“Obey Allah and obey the Messenger and beware! But if you turn away, then know that Our Messenger’s duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺ clearly”* shows after prohibiting intoxicants, gambling, etc., obedience required to implement prohibitions. Wa iḥdharū, and beware, indicates caution against disobeying, or against returning to intoxicants, gambling, etc., after prohibition, or beware of Satan's plan. The same triad appears in other verses: obey Allah, obey Messenger, beware.

**If Turn Away Then Messenger's Duty Only Clear Delivery**

The phrase indicates Messenger's duty is to deliver message clearly, not to compel, not to guide hearts, which is Allah's, and if people turn away after clear delivery, consequence is theirs, not Messenger's fault, as earlier verse 5:67 — *“If you do not, then you have not delivered His message.”* said O Messenger deliver what was sent down, and Allah will protect you. The same phrase appears in 5:99 — *“The Messenger’s duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺. And Allah ˹fully˺ knows what you reveal and what you conceal”* Messenger's duty only to deliver, and Allah knows what you reveal and conceal.

**A Verse That Defines Messenger's Role**

This verse defines Messenger's role: al-balāghu al-mubīn, clear delivery, conveying message clearly, not coercion, not controlling hearts, as earlier verses about People of Book showed messengers came with clear proofs yet many transgressed, and responsibility is on rejecters. The same definition appears in 5:99 — *“The Messenger’s duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺. And Allah ˹fully˺ knows what you reveal and what you conceal”*, 16:35 — *“So did those before them. Is not the messengers’ duty only to deliver ˹the message˺ clearly”*, 24:54 — *“The Messenger’s duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺ clearly.””*, 29:18 — *“If you ˹Meccans˺ persist in denial, so did ˹many˺ communities before you. The Messenger’s duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺ clearly.””*, 36:17 — *“And our duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺ clearly.””*, etc., showing consistent definition. The verse therefore clarifies that after clear delivery, if people turn away, they bear consequence, Messenger has fulfilled duty. The next verse will say no blame on those who believe and do good for what they consumed before prohibition, as long as they fear, believe, do good, then fear and believe, then fear and do good, Allah loves good-doers.

Balāgh mubīn, clear delivery, from balagha, to reach, convey, mubīn from abāna, to clarify, indicates Messenger conveys in clear Arabic, with explanation, evidence, not ambiguous, so that proof established. The extra verified note that balāgh mubīn includes conveying Qur'an and explaining it via Sunnah, as 16:44 — *“˹We sent them˺ with clear proofs and divine Books.”* says We sent down Reminder so you may explain to people what was sent down, shows that Sunnah is part of clear delivery. The verse's closing that Messenger's duty only clear delivery shows that guidance of hearts is Allah's, not Messenger's, as 28:56 — *“You surely cannot guide whoever you like ˹O Prophet˺, but it is Allah Who guides whoever He wills, and He knows best who are ˹fit to be˺ guided”* you do not guide whom you love but Allah guides.

## Verse 5:93

> There is no blame on those who believe and do good for what they had consumed before ˹the prohibition˺, as long as they fear Allah, have faith, and do what is good; then they believe and act virtuously, then become fully mindful ˹of Allah˺ and do righteous deeds. For Allah loves the good-doers

**Why No Blame for Past Consumption**

Laysa 'alā alladhīna āmanū wa 'amilū al-ṣāliḥāti junāḥun fīmā ṭa'imū — no blame on those who believe and do good for what they consumed — fīmā ṭa'imū, in what they tasted, includes intoxicants, foods, etc., consumed before prohibition, as Asbāb reports companions worried about those who died before prohibition of khamr, had consumed it, would they be blamed? Verse revealed to say no blame if they believed, did good, feared, etc. The same principle appears for other prohibitions: what was done before prohibition with belief and good deeds is forgiven, no retroactive blame, as Islam erases past after repentance and obedience. The verse therefore shows mercy for past after prohibition revealed.

**Allah Loves Good-Doers as Closing**

Wa Allāhu yuḥibbu al-muḥsinīn — Allah loves good-doers — muḥsinīn, good-doers, those who do good with excellence, from aḥsana, to do good, with iḥsān. The closing appears many times for similar contexts, as in *“Allah loves good-doers”* (2:195, 3:134 — *“˹They are˺ those who donate in prosperity and adversity, control their anger, and pardon others. And Allah loves the good-doers”*, 5:93). The muḥsinīn are those who combine taqwā, īmān, ṣāliḥāt, iḥsān, as verse describes. The verse therefore ends with love for muḥsinīn, encouraging excellence, not mere minimum. The next verse will discuss test with game within reach of hands and spears while on pilgrimage, to distinguish those who fear Him in secret, transgressor suffers painful punishment.

**A Verse That Shows No Retroactive Punishment**

Iḥsān, doing good with excellence, defined in ḥadīth Jibrīl: to worship Allah as if you see Him, if not seeing Him, He sees you. The verse's progression from ṣāliḥāt to iḥsān shows ascent from good deeds to excellence. The extra verified note that muḥsinīn are highest level after mu'min, believer, as Qur'an describes levels: islām, īmān, iḥsān, shows that verse encourages reaching iḥsān, not just īmān. Allah loves muḥsinīn indicates that excellence is beloved, not just minimum obedience. The following verse will discuss test with game while on pilgrimage. The verse answers men who were worried about what they had done before the rules came down. The sūrah goes on to give the rules about hunting while on pilgrimage and about what a man owes if he kills game by mistake (5:94 — *“O believers! Allah will surely test you with game within the reach of your hands and spears to distinguish those who fear Him in secret.”* states the test and 5:95 — *“O believers! Do not kill game while on pilgrimage.”* sets the compensation), and it names the food that remains lawful to the community. The order of the passage shows the Book's habit: it forbids something and then tells the men who slipped before the prohibition that their past is not held against them.

## Verse 5:94

> O believers! Allah will surely test you with game within the reach of your hands and spears to distinguish those who fear Him in secret. Whoever transgresses from now on will suffer a painful punishment

**To Distinguish Those Who Fear Him in Secret**

Li-ya'lama Allāhu man yakhāfuhu bi-al-ghaybi — to know who fears Him in secret — li-ya'lama, so Allah may know, man yakhāfuhu, who fears Him, bi-al-ghaybi, in unseen, secret, when not seen by people, only Allah sees. The phrase indicates test reveals who has true taqwā, fear of Allah even when unseen, not just when seen by people. The same phrase appears for similar tests, as in *“Who fears Him in secret”* (5:94, 67:12 — *“Indeed, those in awe of their Lord without seeing Him will have forgiveness and a mighty reward”*). The test with game within reach while in iḥrām distinguishes those who fear Allah in secret, abstaining from easy game, versus those who transgress. The knowledge is not for Allah who already knows, but to manifest, make known, who fears.

**Whoever Transgresses After That Painful Punishment**

Fa-mani i'tadā ba'da dhālika fa-lahu 'adhābun alīm — whoever transgresses after that, for him painful punishment — mani i'tadā, whoever transgresses, exceeds bounds, from '-d-w, ba'da dhālika, after that, after clear prohibition and test, fa-lahu 'adhābun alīm, for him painful punishment, 'adhāb alīm, painful, from '-dh-b, a-l-m. The punishment is for transgressing after knowledge, after test, after prohibition, indicating severity for intentional violation after clarity. The same warning appears for other violations, as in *“Painful punishment”* (5:94) itself. The verse therefore warns that after test and prohibition, transgression leads to painful punishment. This verse shows test in pilgrimage: Allah tests pilgrims with game within reach of hands and spears, easy to hunt, while hunting forbidden in iḥrām, to distinguish those who fear Him in secret. The verse's placement after discussion of lawful foods and hunting (5:4-5 — *“Today all good, pure foods have been made lawful for you.”*) returns to hunting prohibition while on pilgrimage, which was mentioned in 5:1-2 — *“When pilgrimage has ended, you are allowed to hunt.”*.

Ṣayd, game, hunting, while muḥrim, pilgrim in iḥrām, is forbidden, as 5:1 — *“O believers! Honour your obligations. All grazing livestock has been made lawful to you—except what is hereby announced to you and hunting while on pilgrimage. Indeed, Allah commands what He wills”* said do not permit hunting while on pilgrimage, and 5:95-96 — *“But hunting on land is forbidden to you while on pilgrimage.”* detail penalty. The extra verified juristic note that ṣayd includes land game, not sea game, as verse 96 says lawful for you to hunt and eat seafood, but hunting on land forbidden while on pilgrimage, shows distinction: sea game lawful even in iḥrām, land game forbidden. The test with game within reach of hands and spears indicates land game, as sea game not reached by hands/spears in same way. The verse therefore introduces penalty section for hunting while muḥrim.

---

## Verse 5:95

> O believers! Do not kill game while on pilgrimage. Whoever kills game intentionally must compensate by offering its equivalence—as judged by two just men among you—to be offered at the Sacred House, or by feeding the needy, or by fasting so that they may taste the consequences of their violations. Allah has forgiven what has been done. But those who persist will be punished by Allah. And Allah is Almighty, capable of punishment

**Killing Game While in Pilgrimage State**

The verse forbids the pilgrim to kill land game and then sets out what the offender owes. The first option is a sacrificial animal equivalent to what was killed, chosen by two just men from the community and driven to the Kaʿbah as an offering — a gazelle matched with a sheep, an ostrich with a camel, each case weighed by two arbitrators. The Companions practised exactly this: ʿUmar, ʿUthmān and ʿAlī are all reported to have judged such equivalences. The alternative to the animal is expiation: feeding the poor, or its equivalent in fasting, "so that he tastes the consequence of his deed."

The graded options follow the same pattern as the oath expiation earlier in the sūrah (5:89 — *“Allah will not call you to account for your thoughtless oaths, but He will hold you accountable for deliberate oaths.”*): recompense first, then feeding, then fasting for the person who cannot manage either. The verse closes with the warning that God is Mighty and capable of retribution, together with the forgiveness of what is past, so the rule applies from the moment it is given. The animals are named as *al-anʿām* — camels, cattle, sheep and goats — the livestock the sūrah declared lawful for food at its opening (5:1 — *“All grazing livestock has been made lawful to you—except what is hereby announced to you and hunting while on pilgrimage.”*). The prohibition belongs to the state of *iḥrām*, in which the ordinary permissions of travel and food are narrowed to test obedience, and the next verse grants the exception that makes the rule bearable: sea catch stays lawful (5:96 — *“It is lawful for you to hunt and eat seafood, as a provision for you and for travellers. But hunting on land is forbidden to you while on pilgrimage. Be mindful of Allah to Whom you all will be gathered”*).

**The Test That Comes Within Arm's Reach**

The rule in this verse is preceded by a verse that says why the rule was given: God tests you with the game that your hands and your spears can reach, to make clear who fears Him in secret (5:94 — *“O believers! Allah will surely test you with game within the reach of your hands and spears to distinguish those who fear Him in secret.”*). A pilgrim in *iḥrām* is a traveller with time on his hands, walking country where animals are plentiful, and the test is designed so that obedience is chosen when nobody is watching. From that comes the graded penalty the verse sets out: the equivalent animal, judged by two just men from the community and taken to the Kaʿbah as an offering; or feeding the poor to the value of the animal; or fasting in place of the food, so that the offender tastes what his hand did. The verses that follow keep the arrangement workable — sea catch stays lawful, and the rule belongs to the state of sacred dress, not to the journey's food (5:96 — *“It is lawful for you to hunt and eat seafood, as a provision for you and for travellers.”*).
**What the Schools Drew From It**

The jurists read *mithluhu*, the equivalent, in two ways, and both are recorded from the Companions: the animal matched by value, or matched by size and kind. ʿUmar, ʿUthmān and ʿAlī are all reported to have judged such cases, which is why the passage is studied as a living example of community arbitration rather than as a fixed tariff. One point the commentators draw from the wording of the offering is that the animal is sent to the Sanctuary and not kept: the man who killed the game does not eat it, because the whole arrangement is a repair and not a licence to hunt.

## Verse 5:96
> It is lawful for you to hunt and eat seafood, as a provision for you and for travellers. But hunting on land is forbidden to you while on pilgrimage. Be mindful of Allah to Whom you all will be gathered

**Sea Game Lawful, Land Game Forbidden**

The verse completes the hunting rules of the pilgrimage with a permission and a prohibition: fish and sea catch are lawful, eaten at home or carried by travellers, while land game remains forbidden as long as the pilgrim is in the sacred state — *ḥurum*, the plural of *ḥarām*, meaning a person under the restrictions of pilgrim sanctity. The jurists extended the ban beyond the act of killing to hunting itself and to helping another hunt, since a prohibition covers the means to what it forbids.

The verses form a single unit. It begins with the animals made lawful at the opening of the sūrah (5:1 states it — *“All grazing livestock has been made lawful to you—except what is hereby announced to you and hunting while on pilgrimage.”*), then the sacred months and the offerings (5:2 states them — *“O believers! Do not violate Allah’s rituals ˹of pilgrimage˺, the sacred months, the sacrificial animals, the ˹offerings decorated with˺ garlands, nor those ˹pilgrims˺ on their way to the Sacred House seeking their Lord’s bounty and pleasure.”*), then the trained hunting animals and what they catch (5:4 permits it — *“Also what is caught by your hunting animals and birds of prey which you have trained as instructed by Allah.”*), then the test of game brought within reach of the pilgrims' hands (5:94 describes it — *“O believers! Allah will surely test you with game within the reach of your hands and spears to distinguish those who fear Him in secret.”*), then the penalty for killing it deliberately (5:95 states it — *“O believers! Do not kill game while on pilgrimage.”*), and here the exception for the sea, with the reminder that everyone will be gathered to God.

The prohibition and the permission together show what the sacred state does: it narrows ordinary permissions for a time, and it also leaves a way to live. The sanction behind the whole set is the account, not only the penalty, which is why the verse ends with the gathering.

**How the Jurists Handled the Sea**

The permission was read to cover everything that lives in the water, and the sūrah of the Bee names the same gift in the same terms: it is God who subjected the sea so that you may eat fresh meat from it (16:14 — *“And He is the One Who has subjected the sea, so from it you may eat tender seafood and extract ornaments to wear.”*). The differences among the schools are on the details — whether all water creatures are lawful or only the fish-shaped ones, and how to understand the crawling things of the sea — and on the far side of the question the verse is unambiguous about the purpose: a traveller on the pilgrimage road eats from the sea, and the rule that forbade the land game was never meant to make life impossible. The jurists also drew from the two halves of the verse that the sea's permission covers what is caught, sold, and eaten, while the land's prohibition covers the hunting, the killing, and the helping of another man's hunt, since the means to a forbidden act are held to the same rule as the act.
**What the Closing Clause Adds**

The verse ends with the line that runs through the whole passage: *and be mindful of God, to whom you will be gathered*. The prohibition on land game lasts as long as the pilgrim's sacred state, and the state ends with the pilgrimage; the mindfulness named in the last phrase does not end with anything, and the gathering is where the accounting happens. The four verses on hunting — the lawful animals at the sūrah's opening, the training of the hunting dogs, the game brought within reach, the penalty for killing it, and this exception for the sea — are all built so that the reader understands the rules as a test with a purpose, and the purpose is stated in the last four words of the last of them.

## Verse 5:97

> Allah has made the Ka’bah—the Sacred House—a sanctuary of well-being for all people, along with the sacred months, the sacrificial animals, and the ˹offerings decorated with˺ garlands. All this so you may know that Allah knows whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth, and that He has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of everything

**Ka'bah as Sacred House Sanctuary of Wellbeing**

Ja'ala Allāhu al-Ka'bata al-bayta al-ḥarāma qiyāman li-al-nāsi wa al-shahra al-ḥarāma wa al-hadya wa al-qalā'ida dhālika li-ta'lamū anna Allāha ya'lamu mā fī al-samāwāti wa mā fī al-arḍi wa anna Allāha bi-kulli shay'in 'Alīm declares: Allah made Ka'bah, Sacred House, support for people. Sacred month, offering, garlands, so you may know Allah knows what in heavens and earth and has knowledge of everything. Ja'ala Allāhu, Allah made, from j-'-l, al-Ka'bata, Ka'bah, from k-'-b, cube, al-bayta al-ḥarāma, Sacred House, bayt, house, ḥarām, sacred, forbidden, qiyāman li-al-nāsi, support, standing, for people, qiyām from q-w-m, to stand, support, li-al-nāsi, for people, nās.

Qiyāman li-al-nāsi, support for people, indicates Ka'bah is qiyām, support, establishment, by which people's religious and worldly affairs stand, as they gather for ḥajj, trade, security, as earlier verse 5:2 — *“O believers! Do not violate Allah’s rituals ˹of pilgrimage˺, the sacred months, the sacrificial animals, the ˹offerings decorated with˺ garlands, nor those ˹pilgrims˺ on their way to the Sacred House seeking their Lord’s bounty and pleasure.”* said do not violate rites of Allah, sacred month, sacrificial animals, garlands. The Ka'bah as Sacred House is sanctuary, secure, where people find wellbeing, safety, as Ibrahim prayed make this city secure, and verse 14:37 — *“So make the hearts of ˹believing˺ people incline towards them and provide them with fruits, so perhaps they will be thankful”*. Qiyāman also means source of uprightness, religion stands by it, as ḥajj is pillar of Islam. The same root q-w-m appears for qiyām, standing, supporting.

**A Verse That Shows Ka'bah's Role**

The verse also shows sacred months, hady, qalā'id as support, providing security and rites. The purpose clause so you may know Allah knows what in heavens and earth shows that legislation of these rites is based on Allah's perfect knowledge of what benefits people, not arbitrary, and His knowledge encompasses everything, so He knows what supports people.

Ka'bah, cube, named for cubic shape, built by Ibrāhīm and Ismā'īl, as 2:127 — *“And ˹remember˺ when Abraham raised the foundation of the House with Ishmael, ˹both praying,˺ “Our Lord! Accept ˹this˺ from us. You are indeed the All-Hearing, All-Knowing”* says, and is bayt al-ḥarām, Sacred House, first house for people, as 3:96 — *“Surely the first House ˹of worship˺ established for humanity is the one at Bakkah—a blessed sanctuary and a guide for ˹all˺ people”* says. Qalā'id, garlands, were collars of fiber, leather, put on sacrificial animals to mark them as hady, offering, so people know not to harm them, providing security, as Jāhiliyyah and Islam practiced, as 5:2 — *“O believers! Do not violate Allah’s rituals ˹of pilgrimage˺, the sacred months, the sacrificial animals, the ˹offerings decorated with˺ garlands, nor those ˹pilgrims˺ on their way to the Sacred House seeking their Lord’s bounty and pleasure.”* mentions. The extra verified historical note that Arabs respected sacred months, hady with garlands, providing safe travel for ḥajj, and Islam continued, shows continuity. The verse's closing Allah knows what in heavens and earth and has knowledge of everything shows that He legislated these based on knowledge of benefits.

## Verse 5:98

> Know that Allah is severe in punishment and that He is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**Command to Know Allah Severe in Punishment and Forgiving Merciful**

I'lamū anna Allāha shadīdu al-'iqābi wa anna Allāha Ghafūrun Raḥīm commands: know that Allah severe in punishment and that Allah All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. The verse combines two attributes: severity in punishment for those who transgress, persist, and forgiveness, mercy for those who repent, believe, do good, as earlier verses showed painful punishment for transgressors (5:94 — *“Whoever transgresses from now on will suffer a painful punishment”*) and Allah is Almighty capable of punishment (5:95 — *“And Allah is Almighty, capable of punishment”*), and forgiveness for past (5:93, 95 — *“For Allah loves the good-doers”*).

**Severe in Punishment for Transgressors**

Shadīdu al-'iqāb, severe in punishment, indicates Allah's punishment severe, strong, not light, for those who deserve, as earlier verses said painful punishment for transgressors after test with game, and Allah is 'Azīz Dhū Intiqām, Almighty Possessor of vengeance (5:95 — *“And Allah is Almighty, capable of punishment”*). The severity is for those who persist after clear proofs, transgress after prohibition, as verse 95 said whoever returns, Allah will punish him, Allah Almighty capable of punishment. The same attribute appears in many verses, balancing mercy, showing that Allah's punishment severe for those who deserve, not to be taken lightly, as earlier verse 33-34 showed severe punishment for waging war against Allah and Messenger, but forgiveness for repentance.

Shadīd al-'iqāb appears 14 times, often paired with Ghafūr Raḥīm or similar, showing that Allah's severity and mercy coexist, severity for those who deserve, mercy for those who repent. The extra verified note that Qur'an often pairs severity and mercy to maintain balance, not despair nor complacency, as in 40:3 — *“the Forgiver of sin and Accepter of repentance, the Severe in punishment, and Infinite in bounty. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him. To Him ˹alone˺ is the final return”* Forgiver of sin, Accepter of repentance, Severe in punishment, Possessor of bounty, shows consistent pattern.

I'lamū, know, imperative, indicates obligation to know Allah's attributes, not mere belief but knowledge, as earlier verse 97 said so you may know Allah knows what in heavens and earth. Verse 98 says know Allah severe in punishment and Forgiving Merciful, showing that knowledge of Allah's attributes required for proper worship, fear, hope.

## Verse 5:99

> The Messenger’s duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺. And Allah ˹fully˺ knows what you reveal and what you conceal

**Messenger's Duty Only to Deliver**

Mā 'alā al-rasūli illā al-balāghu wa Allāhu ya'lamu mā tubdūna wa mā taktumūn declares: not upon Messenger except delivery, and Allah knows what you reveal and what you conceal. The phrase mā 'alā al-rasūli illā al-balāghu appears in 5:99 and similar in 5:92 — *“Obey Allah and obey the Messenger and beware! But if you turn away, then know that Our Messenger’s duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺ clearly”*, 24:54 — *“Say, “Obey Allah and obey the Messenger.”*, 29:18 — *“If you ˹Meccans˺ persist in denial, so did ˹many˺ communities before you. The Messenger’s duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺ clearly.””*, etc., defining Messenger's role as delivery, not compulsion, not guiding hearts, not controlling provision, etc. The delivery is al-balāgh, conveying Qur'an and explaining, as 16:44 — *“And We have sent down to you ˹O Prophet˺ the Reminder, so that you may explain to people what has been revealed for them, and perhaps they will reflect”* says.

**Allah Knows What You Reveal and Conceal**

Wa Allāhu ya'lamu mā tubdūna wa mā taktumūn — Allah knows what you reveal and what you conceal — ya'lamu, knows, mā tubdūna, what you reveal, show openly, wa mā taktumūn, what you hide, conceal inwardly, indicates Allah's knowledge encompasses outward and inward, as earlier verse 97 said Allah knows what in heavens and earth and has knowledge of everything. Verse 98 know Allah severe in punishment and Forgiving Merciful, and verse 99 Allah knows what you reveal and conceal, showing that accountability based on His knowledge of outward and inward, so beware, as verse 92 said beware.

The verse therefore after discussing Ka'bah, sacred months, hady, qalā'id, severity and forgiveness, reminds that Messenger delivered, and Allah knows what you reveal and conceal, so obedience required, as earlier verse 92 commanded obey Allah and obey Messenger and beware, if turn away, know Messenger's duty only clear delivery.

Balāgh, delivery, from balagha, to reach, indicates conveying message so it reaches, with clarity, as mubīn, clear, in 5:92 — *“Obey Allah and obey the Messenger and beware! But if you turn away, then know that Our Messenger’s duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺ clearly”*. The extra verified note that balāgh includes conveying Qur'an verbatim and explaining via Sunnah, as Prophet explained ḥajj rites, hunting prohibitions, etc., shows that Sunnah part of balāgh. The verse's closing Allah knows what you reveal and conceal shows that even if you conceal violation of hunting prohibition, or reveal obedience, Allah knows both, so fear Him in secret, as verse 94 said to know who fears Him in secret.

Mā 'alā al-rasūli illā al-balāghu — not upon Messenger except delivery — is mercy, as Messenger not responsible for guiding hearts, which is Allah's, as 28:56 — *“You surely cannot guide whoever you like ˹O Prophet˺, but it is Allah Who guides whoever He wills, and He knows best who are ˹fit to be˺ guided”* you do not guide whom you love but Allah guides, and not responsible for provision, punishment, etc., only delivery. The same phrase shows that after delivery, people have no excuse, proof established, as earlier verses about People of Book showed messengers came with clear proofs. The verse therefore after discussing penalties, forgiveness, Ka'bah, etc., reminds that Messenger delivered, so obey.

## Verse 5:100

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Good and evil are not equal, though you may be dazzled by the abundance of evil. So be mindful of Allah, O people of reason, so you may be successful.”

**Be Mindful of Allah O People of Reason So Successful**

Fa-ittaqū Allāha yā ulī al-albābi la'allakum tufliḥūn — be mindful of Allah O people of reason so you may be successful — ittaqū, be mindful, from w-q-y, yā ulī al-albābi, O possessors of understanding, reason, albāb, hearts, minds, lubb, core, la'allakum tufliḥūn, so you may be successful, falāḥ. Ulū al-albāb, people of reason, indicates those who have sound intellect, core understanding, not deceived by abundance, able to distinguish khabīth and ṭayyib, good and evil. The command to be mindful of Allah and success shows that distinguishing good and evil and choosing good with mindfulness leads to success, as earlier verse 90 said shun evil so you may be successful.

**A Verse That Summarizes Ṭayyibāt Versus Khabā'ith Theme**

Khabīth and ṭayyib appear as opposite categories in Qur'an: 8:37 — *“so Allah may separate the evil from the good. He will pile up the evil ones all together and then cast them into Hell. They are the ˹true˺ losers”* to distinguish khabīth from ṭayyib, 24:26 — *“Wicked women are for wicked men, and wicked men are for wicked women. And virtuous women are for virtuous men, and virtuous men are for virtuous women. The virtuous are innocent of what the wicked say. They will have forgiveness and an honourable provision”* khabīth women for khabīth men, ṭayyib women for ṭayyib men, etc., showing that categories distinct, not equal, even if abundant. The extra verified note that ṭayyib includes lawful provision, good deeds, good persons, pure, while khabīth includes unlawful, evil deeds, impure, shows broad application. The verse's closing O people of reason, ulū al-albāb, indicates that distinguishing requires reason, intellect, not mere numbers. Verses 87-88 forbade forbidding good things and commanded eating lawful good, 89 oath expiation, 90-91 prohibited intoxicants, gambling, idols, divination as evil of Satan's handiwork, explained Satan's plan, 92-93 obedience and no blame for past, 94-96 test with game, prohibition, penalty, lawful seafood, 97 Ka'bah support, sacred months, hady, qalā'id, Allah knows, 98 severe punishment and Forgiving Merciful, 99 Messenger's duty only delivery, Allah knows reveal and conceal, 100 good and evil not equal though abundance of evil may dazzle, be mindful O people of reason so successful.

**People of Reason as Those Who Distinguish**

The same address appears for similar commands, as in *“O people of reason”* (5:100) itself. The address shows that distinguishing good and evil requires reason, intellect, not mere imitation, and that people of reason are those who fear Allah, as fa-ittaqū Allāha, be mindful of Allah, precedes, linking taqwā to reason. The verse therefore calls people of reason to be mindful and not be dazzled by abundance of evil.

**Success Linked to Mindfulness and Choosing Good**

La'allakum tufliḥūn — so you may be successful — falāḥ, success, cultivation, indicates that success, in worldly and otherworldly, requires mindfulness of Allah and choosing ṭayyib over khabīth, not being dazzled by abundance of evil. The same closing appears for similar commands, as in 5:90 — *“O believers! Intoxicants, gambling, idols, and drawing lots for decisions are all evil of Satan’s handiwork. So shun them so you may be successful”* shun evil so you may be successful, and 5:100 — *“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Good and evil are not equal, though you may be dazzled by the abundance of evil. So be mindful of Allah, O people of reason, so you may be successful.””* be mindful O people of reason so you may be successful, linking success to shunning evil and mindfulness.

## Verse 5:101

> O believers! Do not ask about any matter which, if made clear to you, may disturb you. But if you inquire about what is being revealed in the Quran, it will be made clear to you. Allah has forgiven what was done ˹in the past˺. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Forbearing

**But If Ask While Qur'an Revealed It Will Be Made Clear**

Wa in tas'alū 'anhā ḥīna yunazzalu al-Qur'ānu tubda lakum — but if you ask about them while Qur'an being revealed, it will be made clear to you — indicates that if you ask about matters that are being revealed in Qur'an. Revelation descending, it will be clarified, tubda lakum, made clear, as Qur'an clarifies what needed. The distinction: do not ask about things whose clarification would trouble you, but if you ask about what is being revealed, it will be clarified, as Qur'an's purpose to clarify. The phrase ḥīna yunazzalu al-Qur'ānu, when Qur'an being revealed, indicates time of revelation, when rulings being sent down, questions about them will be answered. Unnecessary questions not being revealed should not be asked. 'Afā Allāhu 'anhā wa Allāhu Ghafūrun Ḥalīm — Allah forgave it and Allah Forgiving Forbearing — 'afā, forgave, pardoned, 'anhā, about it, what was done in past, such as excessive questioning before prohibition, wa Allāhu Ghafūrun Ḥalīm, Allah Forgiving, covering sins, Ḥalīm, Forbearing, not hastening punishment, forbearing with questioning.

The extra verified note that individual responsibility, 'alaykum anfusakum, upon you is yourselves, does not cancel collective duty of enjoining good and forbidding evil, as earlier verses about not forbidding evil as cause of curse (5:79 — *“They did not forbid one another from doing evil. Evil indeed was what they did”*) showed that failure to forbid evil leads to curse. Verse 105 says deviation of others not harm guided as long as guided, but guidance includes enjoining good and forbidding evil as much as possible, as Abū Bakr clarified, and if people see evil and do not change it, Allah may punish all, as ḥadīth says. The verse therefore balances individual and collective, showing that after doing duty of calling to revelation and Messenger and enjoining good, if others still deviate, their deviation not harm guided, as each returns to Allah and informed of deeds, and Allah knows what you reveal and conceal.

## Verse 5:102

> Some people before you asked such questions then denied their answers

Qad sa'alahā qawmun min qablikum thumma aṣbaḥū bihā kāfirīn reports: indeed some people before you asked such questions then became disbelievers because of them. The verse follows prohibition of asking about matters that if made clear would trouble you (5:101 — *“O believers! Do not ask about any matter which, if made clear to you, may disturb you.”*), explaining that some before asked such troubling questions then denied answers, becoming disbelievers, showing consequence of excessive questioning.

**A Verse That Warns Against Excessive Questioning**

This verse warns against excessive questioning by citing example of previous people who asked then denied and became disbelievers, as deterrent. The warning aligns with ḥadīth *“Those before you were destroyed by excessive questioning and disagreeing with prophets”*, showing that excessive questioning leads to destruction, disbelief. The verse therefore after prohibiting asking about troubling matters (5:101 — *“O believers! Do not ask about any matter which, if made clear to you, may disturb you.”*), says some before asked such then became disbelievers, so do not follow their path, ask beneficial questions about what being revealed, which will be clarified, not troubling.

Banī Isrā'īl, Children of Israel, asked Moses about cow: *“Call upon your Lord to make clear to us what it is”* (2:68), then color, then details, increasing hardship, then slaughtered, showing excessive questioning. They also asked *“Show us Allah plainly”* (4:153), then thunderbolt seized them. The extra verified historical note that these questions led to increased obligations and punishments, shows that verse 102's qawmun min qablikum refers to such, and warns believers not to repeat. The next verse will discuss baḥīrah, sā'ibah, waṣīlah, ḥām camels, which Allah never ordained but disbelievers fabricate lies, most lack understanding, showing another form of inventing religion without knowledge.

**Connection to Forgiveness and Forbearing**

Verse 101 said Allah forgave what past, Allah Forgiving Forbearing, after prohibiting asking about troubling matters. Verse 102 says some before asked such then became disbelievers, showing that past excessive questions forgiven for believers, but previous nations who asked then denied became disbelievers, so forgiveness for this Ummah if stops, but warning that denial after asking leads to disbelief. The connection shows mercy for this Ummah: past forgiven, but warning not to follow previous path. The next verse will discuss fabricated livestock prohibitions that Allah never ordained, another form of inventing without knowledge.

## Verse 5:103

> Allah has never ordained the ˹so-called˺ baḥîrah, sâ'ibah, waṣîlah, and ḥâm camels. But the disbelievers just fabricate lies about Allah, and most of them lack understanding

**Disbelievers Fabricate Lies About Allah**

Wa lākinna alladhīna kafarū yaftarūna 'alā Allāhi al-kadhiba — but disbelievers fabricate lies about Allah — yaftarūna, fabricate, invent, 'alā Allāhi al-kadhiba, lies about Allah, claiming Allah ordained these prohibitions. He did not, as earlier verse 87 said do not forbid good things Allah made lawful, and 5:103 — *“But the disbelievers just fabricate lies about Allah, and most of them lack understanding”* says Allah never ordained these. The fabrication is attributing to Allah what He did not say, as earlier verses about People of Book distorting, hiding, inventing. The same phrase appears for other fabrications, as in *“Fabricate lies about Allah”* (5:103) itself. The fabrication shows that forbidding lawful and attributing to Allah is lie.

**Connection to Forbidding Good Things**

Verses 87-88 forbade forbidding good things Allah made lawful and commanded eating lawful good. Verse 103 says Allah never ordained baḥīrah, sā'ibah, waṣīlah, ḥām, but disbelievers fabricate lies about Allah, most lack understanding, showing that forbidding good things and attributing to Allah is fabrication, lie, lacking understanding, as Jāhiliyyah did with camels. The connection shows that forbidding lawful is not only transgression (5:87 — *“O believers! Do not forbid the good things which Allah has made lawful for you, and do not transgress. Indeed, Allah does not like transgressors”*) but also fabrication of lies about Allah (5:103 — *“But the disbelievers just fabricate lies about Allah, and most of them lack understanding”*), both prohibited. The next verse will say when told come to Allah's revelations and Messenger, they say what forefathers practiced enough, even if forefathers had no knowledge or guidance, showing blind following. This verse connects to earlier themes of knowledge and guidance that run through Sūrah al-Mā'idah, as sūrah stresses 'ilm, knowledge. Hudā, guidance, versus lack of knowledge and misguidance, as in verse 77 do not follow desires of those astray before and led many astray and strayed from straight path, and verse 104 even if forefathers had no knowledge or guidance, and verse 103 most lack understanding, lā ya'qilūn, and verse 77.
