## Verse 5:104

> When it is said to them, “Come to Allah’s revelations and to the Messenger,” they reply, “What we found our forefathers practicing is good enough for us.” ˹Would they still do so,˺ even if their forefathers had absolutely no knowledge or guidance

**Blind Following of Forefathers**

Ḥasbunā mā wajadnā 'alayhi ābā'anā — enough for us what we found forefathers upon — indicates blind following, taqlīd, of forefathers, without knowledge, saying forefathers' practice enough, no need for revelation and Messenger. The same response appears for disbelievers throughout Qur'an, as in 2:170 — *“We ˹only˺ follow what we found our forefathers practicing.””*, 5:104, 31:21 — *“We ˹only˺ follow what we found our forefathers practicing.””*, etc., showing that disbelievers reject revelation and Messenger and follow forefathers blindly. The response shows lack of understanding, as previous verse said most lack understanding, and following forefathers who had no knowledge or guidance is irrational.

Forefathers who instituted baḥīrah, sā'ibah, etc., had no knowledge, lā ya'lamūna shay'an, nor guidance, lā yahtadūn, as they invented without revelation, following desires, as earlier verse 77 said do not follow desires of those astray before and led many astray and strayed from straight path. The extra verified historical note that 'Amr ibn Luḥayy, first to institute sā'ibah, had no knowledge, changed religion of Ibrāhīm, brought idols, instituted taboos without knowledge, and following him is following one with no knowledge or guidance, shows example. The verse's rhetorical question even if forefathers had no knowledge or guidance? Indicates they indeed had none, so following them irrational.

**Connection to Fabrication and Understanding**

Verse 104 says when told come to Allah's revelations and Messenger, they say forefathers enough, even if forefathers had no knowledge or guidance, connecting fabrication to blind following: they fabricate lies about Allah, attributing inventions to Allah. When called to revelation and Messenger, they reject and follow forefathers who invented without knowledge or guidance, showing lack of understanding. The next verse will discuss individual responsibility, that each person is charged with self and others' deviation does not injure the guided, and that all return to the Lord who will inform of deeds, showing personal accountability.

## Verse 5:105

> O believers! You are accountable only for yourselves. It will not harm you if someone chooses to deviate—as long as you are ˹rightly˺ guided. To Allah you will all return, and He will inform you of what you used to do

**Each Person Answers for Himself**

The verse tells the believers that they are responsible for their own conduct, and that the deviation of other people will not harm them as long as they are on the right road themselves. Then it says that all of them will return to God, who will tell them what they used to do. The verse comes at the end of a passage that has dealt with the questions and the false claims of the earlier communities, and it puts the responsibility back on the reader.

**What the Verse Does Not Mean**

The verse has been misread as permission to stop caring about other people. The Book does not allow that reading: the community is commanded to call people to good and to forbid what is wrong, and the Book calls the community that does this work the best of communities (3:104 — *“Let there be a group among you who call ˹others˺ to goodness, encourage what is good, and forbid what is evil—it is they who will be successful”* names the duty and 3:110 — *“Had the People of the Book believed, it would have been better for them.”* gives the description). The verse is about the limit of responsibility: a person does what he can to call others and to set an example, and after that he is not answerable for the choice they make. The Prophet put the same rule in words the community remembers: keep enjoining good and forbidding wrong until you see greed obeyed, appetites followed, the world preferred, and every man impressed with his own opinion, and then look to yourself.

**What the Command to Take Care of Yourself Means**

The instruction to take care of yourself is not an instruction to be selfish. It is the order of priority: a man who is not on the right road cannot lead anybody else to it. The sūrah has been telling the believers to keep their promises, to be fair in their dealings, and to remember God in secret and in the open, and the verse says that this work begins at home. The account of the earlier communities in the same passage shows what happens when a people leaves its own conduct and argues about other people's instead.

**The Rest of the Verse**

The verse ends by saying that everyone returns to God and that He will inform them of what they did. The same reminder runs through the passage: the sūrah says that God is mindful of what people do (5:96 — *“But hunting on land is forbidden to you while on pilgrimage.”* continues the instruction about the sea and the land), that God knows what is in the heavens and the earth and what people reveal and conceal (5:97 — *“All this so you may know that Allah knows whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth, and that He has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of everything”* and 5:99 — *“The Messenger’s duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺. And Allah ˹fully˺ knows what you reveal and what you conceal”* state the knowledge in the same passage), and it tells the reader that the accounting is not settled here. The verse therefore closes the section on questions and arguments by handing the reader his own file and telling him where it will be opened. The sūrah says in the same passage that God knows what people reveal and what they conceal (5:99 states the knowledge — *“The Messenger’s duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺. And Allah ˹fully˺ knows what you reveal and what you conceal”*), which is why the accounting of the hidden things is left to Him.

---

## Verse 5:106

> O believers! When death approaches any of you, call upon two just Muslim men to witness as you make a bequest; otherwise, two non-Muslims if you are afflicted with death while on a journey. If you doubt ˹their testimony˺, keep them after prayer and let them testify under oath ˹saying˺, “By Allah! We would never sell our testimony for any price, even in favour of a close relative, nor withhold the testimony of Allah. Otherwise, we would surely be sinful.”

**Witnesses to a Bequest**

The verse legislates for a death that comes on a journey. When death approaches, two just men from the community are to witness the bequest; if the dying person is travelling and no Muslim witnesses can be found, two men from outside the community may serve. The permission is practical: a traveller who dies abroad must be able to leave a record, and the rights of the heirs depend on that record being made.

The occasion reported for the ruling concerns two Christian travellers, Tamīm al-Dārī and ʿAdī ibn Baddāʾ, who had charge of a Muslim's property when he died and whose oath was taken after a silver vessel went missing from it. The verses that follow allow the heirs to stand in place of the witnesses and swear when the first oath is shown to be false (5:107 — *“If they are found guilty ˹of false testimony˺, let the deceased’s two closest heirs affected by the bequest replace the witnesses and testify under oath ˹saying˺, “By Allah!”*). This verse gives the form the testimony takes: the witnesses swear by Allah that they have not sold the testimony for a price, even for a relative, and that they will not hide the testimony of Allah. The general rule behind the closing clause is stated in the sūrah of the Cow: do not conceal testimony, for whoever conceals it has a sinful heart (2:283 — *“And do not conceal the testimony, for whoever conceals it, their hearts are indeed sinful.”*). What begins as a rule for a deathbed on the road ends as a rule protecting the people whose only defence is an accurate record.

**What the Following Verses Add**

The verse after this one gives the procedure if the witnesses turn out to have lied: two others from among the heirs stand up and swear by God that their testimony is truer than the first testimony, and that they have not exceeded the limits (5:107 — *“Our testimony is truer than theirs.”*). Then comes the sentence that explains why the whole passage is here: *that is more likely to make them give the testimony correctly, or fear that an oath will be taken after their own* (5:108 — *“In this way it is more likely that witnesses will give true testimony or else fear that their oaths could be refuted by those of the heirs.”*). The rule was given in a case that had already happened — two travellers entrusted with a dying man's goods, and a silver vessel that disappeared from what they carried. The apparatus of oaths exists because the dying man on a road may have only strangers at his side, and because the heirs, kept far away, will have nothing but a record.
**The Ruling the Jurists Took From It**

The majority of the jurists read the permission for two non-Muslim witnesses as belonging to the traveller's case, in a place where no Muslim could be found, and some held the verse to have been settled by the later rule that testimony is taken from the trustworthy regardless of community. The point the passage itself insists on is simpler: witnessing is a trust, and the men who take charge of another man's estate at his death are answering to a weight they will carry in the open. The verse's closing words say so — the oath is taken over a share of the inheritance, and the inheritance is only the visible part of it.

## Verse 5:107

> If they are found guilty ˹of false testimony˺, let the deceased’s two closest heirs affected by the bequest replace the witnesses and testify under oath ˹saying˺, “By Allah! Our testimony is truer than theirs. We have not transgressed. Otherwise, we would surely be wrongdoers.”

**Replacement Witnesses From Heirs Affected**

Fa-ākharāni yaqūmāni maqāmahumā mina alladhīna istaḥaqqa 'alayhim al-awlayāni — two others stand in their place from those entitled, the two closest — indicates that if first two witnesses found guilty of false testimony, sin, then two others from heirs, those who have right, al-awlayān, two closest, most entitled, affected by gift, stand in their place and testify with oath. The replacement ensures that false testimony corrected by heirs' testimony, which is more likely true, as heirs know deceased and gift, and have interest, but oath ensures truth. The procedure shows justice: if witnesses guilty, heirs replace. Verse 106 said when death approaches, two just Muslim witnesses for gift, or two non-Muslims if traveling and death afflicts, if doubt detain after prayer and let them swear not selling testimony for price even for relative nor hiding Allah's testimony otherwise sinful.

This verse connects to earlier themes of knowledge and guidance that run through Sūrah al-Mā'idah, as sūrah stresses 'ilm, knowledge. Hudā, guidance, versus lack of knowledge and misguidance, as in verse 77 do not follow desires of those astray before and led many astray and strayed from straight path, and verse 104 even if forefathers had no knowledge or guidance, and verse 103 most lack understanding, lā ya'qilūn, and verse 77. The connection shows that following forefathers without knowledge or guidance leads to fabrication, as in baḥīrah etc., and following revelation and Messenger leads to knowledge and guidance, as revelation has knowledge and guidance. The verse therefore reinforces that true guidance comes from what Allah sent down and Messenger, not from forefathers who had no knowledge.

---

## Verse 5:108
> In this way it is more likely that witnesses will give true testimony or else fear that their oaths could be refuted by those of the heirs. Be mindful of Allah and obey. For Allah does not guide the rebellious people

**Why the Rule Is Made This Way**

The verse states the wisdom of the procedure just given: witnesses who know that their oath can be challenged by the heirs are more likely to tell the truth, or at least will fear that their oath will be overturned after them. The law works by making false testimony risky rather than by inspecting consciences — a principle the sūrah applies more than once. The command that closes the verse, "be mindful of Allah and listen," is the chapter's usual seal on a ruling: the law has been given, and obedience is owed. The same command to obey God and His Messenger was given earlier in the sūrah (5:92 — *“Obey Allah and obey the Messenger and beware! But if you turn away, then know that Our Messenger’s duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺ clearly”*). The three verses together handle the practical side of a bequest: witnesses at the deathbed (5:106 — *“If you doubt ˹their testimony˺, keep them after prayer and let them testify under oath ˹saying˺, “By Allah!”*), the challenge of a false oath (5:107 — *“If they are found guilty ˹of false testimony˺, let the deceased’s two closest heirs affected by the bequest replace the witnesses and testify under oath ˹saying˺, “By Allah!”*), and the reason the procedure is weighted this way. They stand inside the passage on individual responsibility that runs through the sūrah, where the believers are told to look after their own souls and are assured that the one who goes astray cannot harm them when they are guided (5:105 — *“It will not harm you if someone chooses to deviate—as long as you are ˹rightly˺ guided.”*). A person's last act, done properly, is part of the duty.

From here the sūrah turns to the Day when God gathers the messengers and asks how they were received (5:109 — *“˹Consider˺ the Day Allah will gather the messengers and say, “What response did you receive?””*).

**Why the Rule Is Weighted This Way**

The verse gives the reason for the oath procedure in the plainest terms of policy: the witnesses who know their word can be challenged will be more careful, or will at least fear being answered by an oath after them. The law works on the risk of being caught rather than on an inspection of anyone's conscience, and the sūrah uses the same method in its other rulings — the punishment of the false accuser, the requirement of witnesses, the public witnessing of the penalty. What the verse adds is the limit of any legal system: the procedure makes perjury costly, and it cannot make a man honest. The command that closes it — be mindful of God and listen — is the chapter's standard seal, and it is aimed at the part of the matter no court can reach.
**Where the Verses Sit**

The three verses on the deathbed bequest stand inside a passage about responsibility that runs through the sūrah: the believers are told to look after their own souls, and assured that one who goes astray cannot harm them when they are guided (5:105 — *“It will not harm you if someone chooses to deviate—as long as you are ˹rightly˺ guided.”*). The passage is practical because a man dies on a road, and the people with him may be strangers; the community's answer is a witnessed record and a challenge for the heirs. The Book has been building the same idea since the sūrah's opening verse on fulfilling contracts, and the closing words of these verses come back to the same place: the law is a trust, and the One who gave it is the One the witnesses are answering to.

---
## Verse 5:109

> ˹Consider˺ the Day Allah will gather the messengers and say, “What response did you receive?” They will reply, “We have no knowledge ˹compared to You˺! You ˹alone˺ are indeed the Knower of all unseen.”

**The Day the Messengers Are Asked**

The verse describes a scene on the day of judgment: God gathers the messengers and asks them what answer they received from the people they were sent to. They reply that they have no knowledge, and that He alone knows everything hidden. The verse follows the passage about the community's duties and the warnings given to the earlier nations, and it shows the reader how the accounts of the nations will be settled.

**Why the Messengers Answer That Way**

The messengers' answer does not mean that they forgot what happened. The Book shows the same modesty in the sūrah of the Heights, where God says that He will question the people who were sent to and question the messengers themselves (7:6 states both — *“We will surely question those who received messengers and We will question the messengers ˹themselves˺”*). The messengers hand the matter to God because the question is not about the facts, which are known, but about the inner response of the people they delivered the message to: what they believed, what they hid, and what they intended. Only God knows that, and the verse names Him in the strongest form of the word for knowing, twice over: the Knower of every kind of unseen thing.

**What the Scene Establishes**

The scene establishes that the message was delivered. The community's own duty in this sūrah is described as delivery and nothing more, since the Prophet was told that his duty was to convey the message clearly, and that if the people turned away, his responsibility was done (5:92 — *“Obey Allah and obey the Messenger and beware! But if you turn away, then know that Our Messenger’s duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺ clearly”* and 5:99 — *“The Messenger’s duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺. And Allah ˹fully˺ knows what you reveal and what you conceal”* state the duty in the same passage). The gathering in front of the reader is where that delivery is confirmed, and the response of each nation is known to the God who knows the unseen. The next verse turns to Jesus and the favours given to him and to his mother, and the questioning of the messengers leads into the questioning of one of them in particular.

**What the Verse Says to the Reader**

The verse tells the reader that the message he has received is not a private matter between him and the person who carried it. It has a sender, it was delivered, and it will be accounted for. The same sūrah has reminded the reader that God knows what people reveal and what they conceal (5:99 states it — *“The Messenger’s duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺. And Allah ˹fully˺ knows what you reveal and what you conceal”*), and the verse before this one says that all of them will return to God and be told what they did (5:105 gives the sentence — *“O believers! You are accountable only for yourselves. It will not harm you if someone chooses to deviate—as long as you are ˹rightly˺ guided. To Allah you will all return, and He will inform you of what you used to do”*). The scene in front of the reader puts the two together: the messenger answers for the delivery, and the people answer for what they did with it.

---

## Verse 5:110

> And ˹on Judgment Day˺ Allah will say, “O Jesus, son of Mary! Remember My favour upon you and your mother: how I supported you with the holy spirit so you spoke to people in ˹your˺ infancy and adulthood. How I taught you writing, wisdom, the Torah, and the Gospel. How you moulded a bird from clay—by My Will—and breathed into it and it became a ˹real˺ bird—by My Will. How you healed the blind and the lepers—by My Will. How you brought the dead to life—by My Will. How I prevented the Children of Israel from harming you when you came to them with clear proofs and the disbelievers among them said, “This is nothing but pure magic.”

**The Favours Listed One by One**

*Idh qāla Allāhu yā ʿĪsā ibna Maryam udhkur niʿmatī ʿalayka wa-ʿalā wālidatik* — when God said: O Jesus son of Mary, remember My favour to you and to your mother. The verse is spoken on the Day of Judgment, and it begins a list of gifts rather than a verdict. The first is the support of the holy spirit, which the Sūrah of the Cow names among the gifts given to Jesus and to the line of prophets before him (2:87 — *“And We gave Jesus, son of Mary, clear proofs and supported him with the holy spirit.”*; 2:253 — *“To Jesus, son of Mary, We gave clear proofs and supported him with the holy spirit.”*). The second is his speech in the cradle and in maturity, which the Sūrah of Maryam narrates as a baby speaking from the cradle to clear his mother's name (19:29-33 — *“and to be kind to my mother. He has not made me arrogant or defiant”*). The third is the teaching: the Book, the wisdom, the Torah and the Gospel.
**The Miracles and the Word That Limits Them**

The list continues with the clay bird that became a living bird, the blind and the leper healed, and the dead raised. The Book repeats one phrase with every miracle: by My permission. The Sūrah of Āl-ʿImrān gives the same catalogue to the same man, and it inserts the same clause at each item (3:49 — *“I will heal the blind and the leper and raise the dead to life—by Allah’s Will.”*). The repetition is the answer to the claim that grew up around him later. A man who heals the blind and raises the dead is still a man acting on a permission, and the verse says so in the middle of the honour being conferred. The next sentence adds the protection given to him from the children of Israel when they came to kill him (5:110 — *“How you healed the blind and the lepers—by My Will.”*).
**Where the Verse Sits in the Sūrah**

The passage is the dialogue of the Day: God gathers the messengers and asks what response they received (5:109 — *“˹Consider˺ the Day Allah will gather the messengers and say, “What response did you receive?””*), and this verse begins the account of Jesus and what was given to him. The disciples' request for the table, the question about whether he told people to worship him and his mother, and his answer leaving the matter to God all follow (5:111-118 — *“Did you ever ask the people to worship you and your mother as gods besides Allah?””*). The sūrah's last speech from God is therefore about the honours heaped on a servant who never claimed more than servanthood, and the favours are listed so that the reader can see where the line is drawn.

**The Miracles and the Limit Attached to Them**

The list of favours is a list of wonders. A shape of clay that became a bird by permission. A man born blind given sight. A leper made clean. The dead brought out of their graves. Every one of them is in the Book as a gift and a proof, and every one of them is closed with the same words: by God's permission. The repetition is not decoration. It is the verse's answer to everything that would later be said about the man, and it is why the same catalogue appears in Āl ʿImrān with the same clause attached to each item (3:49 — *“I will heal the blind and the leper and raise the dead to life—by Allah’s Will.”*). A servant who does astonishing things by permission is still a servant, and the Book says so while honouring him.
**How the Community Was Taught to Speak of Him**

The Prophet himself gave the community the words and the ranking. Bukhārī reports him saying that he is the closest of all people to Jesus son of Mary, in this world and the next, and that the prophets are brothers of the same father with different mothers. The Book also clears the two errors that had grown up on either side of him, insisting that the Messiah is neither God nor a mere man of no account, and the Sūrah of the Women states the command plainly: believe in God and His messengers, and do not say three (4:171 — *“The Messiah, Jesus, son of Mary, was no more than a messenger of Allah and the fulfilment of His Word through Mary and a spirit ˹created by a command˺ from Him.”*). The verse in front of us does the same work by its arrangement. It lists the honour first and the permission clause second, and it lets the reader see that the man is great and the God who gave him the gifts is greater.
**The Favours Given to His Mother**

The verse begins by naming the mother with him: remember My favour to you and to your mother. The Sūrah of Āl-ʿImrān records the answer of Maryam's Lord, who accepted her with a good acceptance and made her grow in a good way (3:37 — *“So her Lord accepted her graciously and blessed her with a pleasant upbringing—entrusting her to the care of Zachariah.”*), and the Sūrah of the Women names her as one who guarded her chastity and in whom the Spirit was breathed (4:171 — *“Stop!—for your own good. Allah is only One God.”*). The Book's honour for the mother is stated in the middle of a passage that forbids worshipping her, and the two belong together: the sūrah raises the woman and refuses to raise her above her place, in the same breath.

## Verse 5:111

> And how I inspired the disciples, “Believe in Me and My messenger!” They declared, “We believe and bear witness that we fully submit ˹to Allah˺.”

**Ḥawāriyyūn as Disciples Helpers**

Al-ḥawāriyyūn, disciples, from ḥawārī, white, pure, or helper, supporter, were close companions of 'Īsā, twelve disciples, who supported him, as Qur'an says ḥawāriyyūn as helpers of Allah, as in 3:52 — *“The disciples replied, “We will stand up for Allah.”*, 61:14 — *“The disciples replied, “We will stand up for Allah.””*. Awḥaytu ilā al-ḥawāriyyīna, I inspired disciples, waḥy, inspiration, not prophetic revelation but inspiration to believe, as Allah inspires hearts, or via 'Īsā's call. An āminū bī wa bi-rasūlī — believe in Me and My messenger — indicates Allah inspired them to believe in Allah and His messenger 'Īsā, as belief in Allah and messenger required, as earlier verses about believing in Allah and messenger.

**We Believe and Bear Witness We Submit**

Qālū āmannā wa ishhad bi-annanā muslimūn — they said we believe and bear witness we are submitters — āmannā, we believe, ishhad, bear witness, bi-annanā muslimūn, that we are muslimūn, submitters to Allah, indicates disciples declared belief in Allah and messenger 'Īsā and submission, islām, to Allah, as true followers of 'Īsā were muslims, submitters, not Christians claiming Trinity, as earlier verses about 'Īsā's true message worship Allah my Lord and your Lord (5:117 — *“I never told them anything except what You ordered me to say: “Worship Allah—my Lord and your Lord!””*). The declaration shows that ḥawāriyyūn were muslims, submitters, as Qur'an calls them.

Al-ḥawāriyyūn as helpers of Allah, anṣār Allāh, as 61:14 — *“O believers! Stand up for Allah, as Jesus, son of Mary, asked the disciples, “Who will stand up with me for Allah?” The disciples replied, “We will stand up for Allah.” Then a group from the Children of Israel believed while another disbelieved. We then supported the believers against their enemies, so they prevailed”* says O you who believe be helpers of Allah as Jesus son of Mary said to disciples who are my helpers to Allah, disciples said we are helpers of Allah.

---

## Verse 5:112

> ˹Remember˺ when the disciples asked, “O Jesus, son of Mary! Would your Lord be willing to send down to us a table spread with food from heaven?” Jesus answered, “Fear Allah if you are ˹truly˺ believers.”

**The Disciples Ask for a Table From Heaven**

The verse opens the story that gives the sūrah its name. The disciples ask Jesus whether his Lord is able to send down a table spread with food from heaven, and he answers with a warning rather than a promise: "Fear Allah, if you are truly believers." The answer is the point of the exchange — the request was for a sign, and Jesus turns the disciples back to the fear of God that should make the sign unnecessary.

The request comes from inside a believing community, since the verse that precedes it has already recorded God's inspiration to the disciples to believe in Him and His messenger and their declaration of submission (5:111 — *“And how I inspired the disciples, “Believe in Me and My messenger!” They declared, “We believe and bear witness that we fully submit ˹to Allah˺.””*). The sūrah does not read the request as a lapse of faith: the next verse gives the disciples' reasons — to eat from it, to have their hearts at rest, to know that Jesus had told them the truth, and to be witnesses to it (5:113 — *“They said, “We ˹only˺ wish to eat from it to reassure our hearts, to verify you are indeed truthful to us, and to become its witnesses.””*). The Book contains the same pattern elsewhere: Abraham asked to be shown how God brings the dead to life, and he was answered (2:260 — *“Show me how you give life to the dead.””*). Faith and the request for reassurance are not set against each other here, and Jesus' warning is a caution about the ground on which the request is made rather than a refusal of it.

## Verse 5:113

> They said, “We ˹only˺ wish to eat from it to reassure our hearts, to verify you are indeed truthful to us, and to become its witnesses.”

**What the Disciples Asked For**

*Qālū nurīdu an naʾkula minhā wa-taṭmaʾinna qulūbunā* — they said: we want to eat from it, and let our hearts be at rest, and let us know that you have told us the truth, and let us be witnesses to it. The request has four parts, and the Sūrah of the Table records them in the words of the men themselves. They want to eat, which is the plainest need. They want their hearts settled. They want to know that the messenger has spoken the truth. And they want to be witnesses of what happened. The four together describe men who were not testing Jesus but asking for something that would stand them up in front of their own people.
**Why Asking for a Sign Is Not Disbelief**

The Book gives a similar scene with Abraham, who asked to see how the dead are raised and answered the question of whether he did not believe with the words: yes, but so that my heart may be at rest (2:260 — *“Abraham replied, “Yes I do, but just so my heart can be reassured.””*). The two passages say the same thing about the human being: certainty for the mind and certainty for the heart are not always the same thing, and the Book treats the second as a legitimate request. What the sūrah adds is the warning that follows the granting of the request. Whoever disbelieves after this, it says, will be punished with a punishment reserved for no one else (5:115 — *“Allah answered, “I am sending it down to you. But whoever among you denies afterwards will be subjected to a torment I have never inflicted on anyone of My creation.””*). The sign was granted, and the door of excuse closed behind it.
**What "Witnesses to It" Means**

The disciples are the ones who had to carry the news back to their people, and being a witness is the role the Book gives them. The Sūrah of the Ranks records their words to Jesus: we are God's helpers (61:14 — *“O believers! Stand up for Allah, as Jesus, son of Mary, asked the disciples, “Who will stand up with me for Allah?””*), and the Sūrah of Āl-ʿImrān has God promise them a place with the witnesses and the righteous (3:53 — *“˹They prayed to Allah,˺ “Our Lord! We believe in Your revelations and follow the messenger, so count us among those who bear witness.””*; 4:69 — *“And whoever obeys Allah and the Messenger will be in the company of those blessed by Allah: the prophets, the people of truth, the martyrs, and the righteous—what honourable company”*). The request in this verse is therefore about their mission rather than their curiosity, and the table they asked for was given as an evidence to hold in front of others. The passage closes the account of Jesus in this sūrah, and the verses after it hand the account back to God, who will tell what the truth of it was.

## Verse 5:114

> Jesus, son of Mary, prayed, “O Allah, our Lord! Send us from heaven a table spread with food as a feast for us—the first and last of us—and as a sign from You. Provide for us! You are indeed the Best Provider.”

**Jesus Prays for the Table**

Jesus puts the request as a prayer: send down a table from heaven to be for us a feast — *ʿīdan*, from the root that means to return — for the first of us and the last of us, and a sign from You. The word carries the sense of a day that comes back, and the commentators took the prayer to mean that the descent of the table would be something the community returned to in remembrance, for those present and for those who came after. The prayer ends with the confession that gives the scene its direction: "and provide for us, for You are the best of providers."

Two things follow from the wording. Provision is asked from God, not from Jesus, and the verse's own close names God as the provider. And the feast is described as shared — "for the first of us and the last of us" — so the request is made for a community rather than for an individual. The next verse gives the answer, a sending down with a condition attached, and the two verses after that turn from the table to the question that will be put to Jesus on the Day of Judgement. The story is placed in the sūrah to serve that argument: a messenger who never claimed divinity, and a community that was given a sign.

**The Answer and Its Condition**

The table was asked for by the disciples, and the messenger prayed for what his people requested. The answer comes in the next verse, and it is the last thing the passage says about the table: God says, I will send it down to you, and whoever among you disbelieves afterwards — I will punish him with a punishment I have not inflicted on anyone in all the worlds (5:115 — *“Allah answered, “I am sending it down to you. But whoever among you denies afterwards will be subjected to a torment I have never inflicted on anyone of My creation.””*). The condition attached to the sign is what makes the scene so grave. A gift asked for as a feast and a sign would have been answered, and the answer carried a warning that made the sign heavier than the hunger it was meant to relieve.
**What the Commentators Did With the Story**

The reports differ on whether the table was sent. Some of the early authorities held that it came down with bread and fish, and others held that the request was refused precisely because of the condition, or that John and the disciples were told to ask for provision rather than for a wonder. What the sūrah does not allow is the reading that turns the story into a claim about Jesus. The request was addressed to God, the descent was by God's permission, and the passage ends by moving to the question put to Jesus on the Day of Judgement — did you tell people to take you and your mother as gods besides God (5:116 — *“Did you ever ask the people to worship you and your mother as gods besides Allah?””*) — so that the whole account is placed in the sūrah as an argument about a servant of God and the limits of what any messenger can be asked for.

## Verse 5:115
> Allah answered, “I am sending it down to you. But whoever among you denies afterwards will be subjected to a torment I have never inflicted on anyone of My creation.”

**The Answer and Its Warning**

The answer comes in a single sentence: the table is sent down. The condition attached to it carries the verse's weight — whoever among the people denies after it will be punished with a torment inflicted on no one else among creation. The warning answers the request itself, because a sign seen at close range removes the excuse of doubt: refusal after it is no longer ordinary unbelief but the rejection of something witnessed. The passage is a complete arc. The disciples ask (5:112 — *“˹Remember˺ when the disciples asked, “O Jesus, son of Mary!”*), they explain why (5:113 — *“They said, “We ˹only˺ wish to eat from it to reassure our hearts, to verify you are indeed truthful to us, and to become its witnesses.””*), Jesus prays (5:114 — *“Jesus, son of Mary, prayed, “O Allah, our Lord!”*), and God answers with the promise and its condition here. The commentators differed over whether the table was actually sent down; the warning covers the case either way, since it applies to those who would deny after having seen.

The sūrah has been building the contrast between knowledge and inheritance throughout: those who follow the ways of their fathers, who had neither knowledge nor guidance (5:104 — *“˹Would they still do so,˺ even if their forefathers had absolutely no knowledge or guidance”*), and those who lack understanding (5:103 — *“But the disbelievers just fabricate lies about Allah, and most of them lack understanding”*). The next verses complete the contrast by turning to the Day when the prophet is asked whether he had told people to worship him and his mother.

**What the Commentators Handled in the Story**

The early authorities took two positions on what followed the promise, and both are on record. The first is that the table came down and the people ate from it; the reports describe bread and fish, and the last part of the story in some accounts has the community warned not to hoard or to ask for more. The second is that the request was refused or withdrawn, and the verse's condition is the reason: a sign of that kind removes the room for doubt. The two readings lead the reader to the same recognition — the gravity of asking heaven for evidence and then treating it as a story. The sūrah does not resolve the question in the narrative, and it does not need to; what it fixes is the warning attached to the promise, which is stated in the verse itself regardless of what happened next.
**Where the Passage Sits in the Sūrah**

The verses before the table are a run of cautions about the community that had the Book in its hands: the people who took their rabbis and monks as lords besides God (5:116 — *“Did you ever ask the people to worship you and your mother as gods besides Allah?””* comes later; 5:44 — *“So too did the rabbis and scholars judge according to Allah’s Book, with which they were entrusted and of which they were made keepers.”* and 5:47 — *“So let the people of the Gospel judge by what Allah has revealed in it. And those who do not judge by what Allah has revealed are ˹truly˺ the rebellious”* speak of those who judge by other than what God sent down), the followers of ancestral ways who had neither knowledge nor guidance (5:104 — *“˹Would they still do so,˺ even if their forefathers had absolutely no knowledge or guidance”*), those who ask about things that would harm them if disclosed (5:101 — *“O believers! Do not ask about any matter which, if made clear to you, may disturb you. But if you inquire about what is being revealed in the Quran, it will be made clear to you. Allah has forgiven what was done ˹in the past˺. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Forbearing”*). Then comes the table — a community asking its prophet for a sign — and then the Day when Jesus is asked what his people did with him, and he answers that he only said what he was commanded to say (5:116-117 — *“If I had said such a thing, you would have certainly known it.”*). The three parts belong to one argument: the Book corrects a community that has begun to ask for wonders instead of following what it has, and it closes by clearing the messenger of what his followers did with his name.

---
## Verse 5:116

> And ˹on Judgment Day˺ Allah will say, “O Jesus, son of Mary! Did you ever ask the people to worship you and your mother as gods besides Allah?” He will answer, “Glory be to You! How could I ever say what I had no right to say? If I had said such a thing, you would have certainly known it. You know what is ˹hidden˺ within me, but I do not know what is within You. Indeed, You ˹alone˺ are the Knower of all unseen

**Did You Tell Them to Worship You?**

On the Day of Judgement God will ask Jesus whether he told people to take him and his mother as gods beside Allah, and his answer is a denial set in the language of worship: "Glory be to You! I would never say what I had no right to say. Had I said it, You would have known it. You know what is in me, and I do not know what is in You. You are the Knower of the unseen." The doctrine is stated through the question rather than by argument: the messenger never claimed what some of his followers later ascribed to him.
The dual form — two gods — matters for the history the verse answers. The Christians of Arabia, and in particular the group the sources call the Collyridians, are reported to have venerated Mary as a divine figure and to have offered bread in her name. The verse excludes both claims at once, and the passage has already argued the point from need: the Messiah and his mother both ate food (5:75 — *“The Messiah, son of Mary, was no more than a messenger.”*), which is what creatures do. The denial continues into the next verse, where Jesus says he was a witness over his people while he remained among them and that God has been the witness over them since (5:117 — *“And I was witness over them as long as I remained among them.”*). The sūrah has already charged the same error to those who went astray before and led many astray (5:77 — *“Say, “O People of the Book!”*), and the scene ends by placing responsibility for the later doctrine with those who taught it.

**The Question and Its Setting**

The verse begins with God speaking on the Day of Gathering, where the messengers are asked how their communities responded to them (5:109 — *“˹Consider˺ the Day Allah will gather the messengers and say, “What response did you receive?””*). Jesus is asked directly: did you tell people to take you and your mother as gods beside God? The question is not a doubt in God's mind; it is the public record of what was taught and what was invented later. The answer comes from the man himself, and it is a denial, and the sūrah puts it in the mouth of the one whom people had raised above his place.
**What the Answer Covers**

His words are worth reading as a statement of belief. He begins with glory to God, which is the response of a creature who has just been mentioned in the same sentence as his Lord. He says he would never say what he had no right to say, and that if he had said it God would have known it. Then the sentence that turns the whole matter over: You know what is in me, and I do not know what is in You. A man who does not know what is in God cannot be a partner of God, and the verse lets the argument rest there. The denial continues into the next verse, where Jesus says he was a witness over his people while he was among them, and that God has been the watcher over them since (5:117 — *“And I was witness over them as long as I remained among them.”*).
**What the Passage Is Doing in the Sūrah**

The sūrah has already made the case in other words, saying that the Messiah and his mother both ate food, which is what a body does (5:75 — *“They both ate food. See how We make the signs clear to them, yet see how they are deluded ˹from the truth˺”*), and warning the People of the Book not to go beyond the limits in their religion (5:77 — *“Say, “O People of the Book!”*). The Book's argument here is not against the teaching of Jesus but against the elevation of holy people into divine ranks, and it names the two forms that took: the Son and the Mother. The lesson for every later community is the same one the verse demonstrates: a good man who is turned into a god has been badly served by the people who claim to honour him, and the first to object is the man himself.

## Verse 5:117
> I never told them anything except what You ordered me to say: “Worship Allah—my Lord and your Lord!” And I was witness over them as long as I remained among them. But when You took me, You were the Witness over them—and You are a Witness over all things

**What ʿĪsā Will Say About His Message**

The verse is part of the speech the Messiah will make on the Day of Judgement, answering the question put to him in the verse before it (5:116 — *“And ˹on Judgment Day˺ Allah will say, “O Jesus, son of Mary! Did you ever ask the people to worship you and your mother as gods besides Allah?” He will answer, “Glory be to You! How could I ever say what I had no right to say? If I had said such a thing, you would have certainly known it. You know what is ˹hidden˺ within me, but I do not know what is within You. Indeed, You ˹alone˺ are the Knower of all unseen”*). He says that he never told the people anything except what God had ordered him to say: worship God, my Lord and your Lord. That sentence settles what his message was, and therefore what the men who made him and his mother into gods were doing: they were worshipping the creation and ignoring the command of the Creator that the Messiah himself had brought.

**A Witness While He Remained, a Watcher After**

He then describes his position among the people: he was a witness over them as long as he remained with them, and when God took him, God became the Watcher over them — and God is a witness over all things. The word for the taking is the one the Book uses when God takes a soul at the end of its life (39:42 — *“˹It is˺ Allah ˹Who˺ calls back the souls ˹of people˺ upon their death as well as ˹the souls˺ of the living during their sleep.”*), and the commentators read it here, with the Book's own statement that the people did not kill him but God raised him (4:158 — *“Rather, Allah raised him up to Himself. And Allah is Almighty, All-Wise”*), and with the promise made to him — I will take you and raise you to Me (3:55 — *“I will take you and raise you up to Myself.”*) — as his being raised to God rather than dying at their hands. The point of the sentence is not the mechanics of his departure; it is the transfer of testimony. The Messiah's watch over his community ended with his departure, and they were left under the watch of the One whose witness misses nothing.

**Why the Verse Closes the Argument**

The verse comes at the end of the sūrah's longest passage against deification, and it leaves no room for the claim that the Messiah wanted any of it. He preached the worship of God alone; he watched over his people while he was among them; the deviations came afterwards, under a witness who was never absent. The closing words — a witness over all things — answer the rest of the sūrah's argument in three words: nobody who took a man as God can say that God did not know.

---

## Verse 5:118

> If You punish them, they belong to You after all. But if You forgive them, You are surely the Almighty, All-Wise.”

**If You Forgive Them You Almighty All-Wise**

Wa in taghfir lahum fa-innaka Anta al-'Azīzu al-Ḥakīm — if You forgive them, You Almighty All-Wise — indicates that forgiveness is from Almighty, 'Azīz, Mighty, who can forgive despite power to punish. All-Wise, Ḥakīm, whose forgiveness is with wisdom, not arbitrary, as He knows who deserves forgiveness, who deserves punishment, as earlier verse 98 know Allah severe in punishment and Forgiving Merciful, and verse 118 shows 'Īsā acknowledging Allah's might and wisdom in punishing or forgiving. The pairing 'Azīz Ḥakīm appears many times, showing might and wisdom together, as forgiveness requires might to forgive and wisdom to place forgiveness rightly.

'Ibāduka, Your servants, indicates that all people are Allah's servants, 'ibād, owned, created, under His dominion, even those who associate, as they are still servants in sense of ownership, though not obedient servants, 'ibād in general sense, not 'ibād al-Raḥmān in specific obedient sense. The extra verified note that Qur'an uses 'ibād for all created, as in 19:93 — *“There is none in the heavens or the earth who will not return to the Most Compassionate in full submission”* all in heavens and earth come to Most Merciful as servants, shows that even disbelievers are servants in ownership, so punishment or forgiveness is Allah's right, as owner. The verse's closing 'Azīz Ḥakīm shows that punishment is with might, forgiveness with wisdom, both attributes together.

---

## Verse 5:119

> Allah will declare, “This is the Day when ˹only˺ the faithful will benefit from their faithfulness. Theirs are Gardens under which rivers flow, to stay there for ever and ever. Allah is pleased with them and they are pleased with Him. That is the ultimate triumph.”

**The Last Word of the Sūrah**

*Qāla Allāhu hādhā yawmu yanfaʿu al-ṣādiqīna ṣidquhum* — God says: this is a day when the truthful benefit from their truthfulness. The verse closes the sūrah, and it closes it on the one quality that has run through the whole chapter. The Table has dealt with promises kept and broken, with testimony, with oaths and their expiation, with the honesty of witnesses, and with the command to stand up for justice even against oneself. The last sūrah in the Book to be revealed in full ends by naming truthfulness as the thing that pays on that day.
**Why the Scene Is Placed Here**

The verses just before this one are the conversation on the Day of Judgment in which Jesus is asked whether he told people to take him and his mother as gods, and he answers with the truth and leaves the matter to God (5:116-118 — *“Did you ever ask the people to worship you and your mother as gods besides Allah?””*). The verse in front of us is the sentence that follows his answer: on this day it is the honest who are helped by their honesty. The bookend between the two is exact. The sūrah's final word is not a threat against the idolaters but a promise to the truthful, spoken by God in His own voice.
**What the Truthful Are Given**

Three things are named in the sentence: gardens with rivers running under them, dwelling in them for ever, and the mutual pleasure of God and His servants — He is pleased with them and they are pleased with Him. The Sūrah of Repentance gives the same triple reward to the believers in the same words (9:72 — *“Allah has promised the believers, both men and women, Gardens under which rivers flow, to stay there forever, and splendid homes in the Gardens of Eternity, and—above all—the pleasure of Allah.”*), and the Sūrah of the Dawn states the greeting of the pleased soul returning to a pleased Lord (89:28 — *“Return to your Lord, well pleased ˹with Him˺ and well pleasing ˹to Him˺”*). The commentators describe God's pleasure as greater than the gardens, because a servant who knows he has been accepted no longer needs anything else. The sūrah closes the sentence with the name for what that is — the great victory — and then the last verse hands the dominion of the heavens and the earth back to God, where it began.

## Verse 5:120

> To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and everything within. And He is Most Capable of everything

**Kingdom of Heavens and Earth as Allah's Alone**

Lillāhi mulku al-samāwāti wa al-arḍi wa mā fīhinna — to Allah belongs kingdom of heavens and earth and what in them — mulk, kingdom, dominion, ownership, control, belongs to Allah alone, not to 'Īsā, not to Maryam, not to any other, as earlier verses refuted deification of 'Īsā and mother, taking them as gods besides Allah. Verse 120 says to Allah alone belongs kingdom of heavens and earth and everything within, showing that dominion belongs to Allah alone, not to 'Īsā or Maryam, who are within heavens and earth, owned, not owners, servants, not lords, as earlier verse 118 said they are Your servants. The same phrase appears for similar closings, emphasizing Allah's sole dominion.

Mulk, kingdom, dominion, from malaka, to own, control, appears in many closing verses, as 3:189 — *“To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth. And Allah is Most Capable of everything”* to Allah belongs kingdom of heavens and earth, 5:120 — *“To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and everything within. And He is Most Capable of everything”* to Allah belongs kingdom, etc., showing that true kingdom belongs to Allah, not to humans, not to 'Īsā, not to others, as earlier verse 17-18 said Jews and Christians claimed we are children of Allah and beloved. To Allah belongs kingdom, and verse 120 repeats for closing. The extra verified note that mulk includes ownership, control, legislation, judgment, provision, etc., all belongs to Allah, so only He deserves worship, as earlier verses about worship Allah my Lord and your Lord, shows tawḥīd al-mulk, Oneness of dominion, part of tawḥīd.

---
